  • Requirements
    • About the names and the notes
    • The name of the project is TCSpecial
    • TCSpecial shall include a process named tcspecial that runs on the spacecraft
    • TCSpecial shall include a library named tcslib that links with misssion control software
    • TCSpecial shall include a library named tcslibgs that is shared between tcspecial and tcslib
    • TCSpecial shall include applications named tcsmoc and tcssim that provide GUIs for testing tcspecial, tcslibgs, and tcslib
    • TCSpecial must have a trait named Command Interpreter (CI) that parses commands sent by tcslib
    • TCSpecial must have a trait named data handler (DH) that is called by CI to process commands
      • CI must be implemented by CI_UDP that communicates with tcslib code via UDP/IP
      • DH_UDP must implement DH and use UDP to send telemetry to tcslib
      • CI must have an element named dh of type BTreeMap
        • CI must support sending telemetry responses to all CI commands
        • Each CI telemetry response to a command must contain a timestamp, command sequence number, a success or error indication
        • CI must periodically send a Telemetry::Beacon message with a timestamp and unique sequence number
        • CI must parse commands from the ground receved via UDP/IP
        • All CI commands must include a sequence number that increments for each command sent
        • CI must support an EXIT command that terminates the tcspecial process
      • Tcspecial includes zero or more components named Data Handler (DH)
    • The top part of the tcsmoc window is for sending commands to tcspecial's CI and viewing telemetry from tcspecial's CI
    • The middle part of the tcsmoc window is for viewing telemetry from individual DHs

Introduction

Note

TCSpecial has been designed for spacecraft and ground communication with high latency communication links. It is just as applicable for other environments, such as submersibles, drones, etc. Simply translate "spacecraft" to your device type.

What there is, in the order a reader meets it:

tcspecial
The flight software. It serves the payloads its configuration file describes, takes commands from the ground, and sends beacons.
tcslibgs
What both ends share: the configuration file formats and the rules about them, the command and telemetry messages, and the digest each end says it read. Every rule in this document is stated there once.
tcslib
What a control application on the ground is built on. A mission control system drives a client rather than speaking the protocol itself.
tcsmoc
A simulated operations center, for testing: a window of panels, one per payload, that commands them and shows what they have moved.
tcssim
Simulated payloads, for testing: it stands in for the far end of every link a configuration describes.
tcsverify
Reads a configuration and says what is wrong with it, running none of it. See tcsverify.

The ranked quality attributes

Four attributes, in order. The order is the point: where two designs satisfy the requirements differently, the earlier attribute decides, and an argument about such a choice should cite this list rather than taste.

  1. Reliability. The vehicle is not reachable for repair. A mechanism that can fail in a new way loses to a rigid one that cannot, and a fault found when a configuration is loaded beats the same fault found in flight.
  2. Ease of use. An operator under time pressure must be able to read what the system does and say what it will do next. Clarity of the configuration and of what the programs report counts as a requirement, not a courtesy.
  3. Maintainability. The software outlives the people who wrote it. One concern per module, a test per claim, and a written reason for anything that is not obvious.
  4. Flexibility and extensibility. New interfaces, new computations and new vehicle arrangements must be reachable without disturbing what already flies -- but never at the cost of the three above.

Terminology

These words are used in one sense each, here and in the code. Several of them had been used in two, which is why they are written down: "device" in particular had been both a type of endpoint and a protocol beside TCP and UDP.

endpoint
One end of the path a data handler's data travels. A handler has two: the OC endpoint, where the ground's data arrives and leaves, and the payload endpoint, where the payload's does. An endpoint is of exactly one type, and the type decides what is needed to open it and what terms it is operated on.
endpoint type
What an endpoint is made of, and so what locates it and what else must be said about it. Five are supported -- network, device-file, serial, i2c and spi -- and five more are experimental: can, iio, rpmsg, gpio and hdlc. A type is not a protocol -- a network endpoint carries the protocol it runs over, and no other type is addressed that way.
device
An endpoint that is a device file, opened and read as it comes. Not the general word for hardware: a serial line, an I2C device and a SPI peripheral are all reached through device files and are none of them a device endpoint, because each has terms of its own that a plain device has not. A device file is the thing in the filesystem; a device endpoint is one type among the nine.
bus
A device that several devices are reached through, each by an address on it. I2C is the one in use. The bus is named by a device file and the device on it by an address, which is why an endpoint on a bus carries both.
payload
What is at the far end of a payload endpoint: the instrument, recorder or radio whose data a handler carries. Also what tcssim stands in for, and what the payload configuration describes.
data handler
The pair of conduits between one payload endpoint and one OC endpoint, named by a name, which is what every command addresses it by. "Handler" alone always means this.
conduit
One direction of one data handler: a thread that reads one endpoint and writes the other. A handler has two, and they share each endpoint between them.
OC
The Operations Center: the ground. The OC endpoint of every handler is a UDP address, and the OC's own address is learnt from what it sends rather than configured.
commander
Whatever tcspecial answers to: what commands it, and what its telemetry goes to. The ground over a radio is one, a cFS flight computer on its software bus another, and an F´ flight computer through a bridge component the third. "MOC" was the word while the ground was the only one, and is still the word for the ground itself, which is one kind of commander.
gateway
The node through which tcspecial reaches its commander, and the link it holds: gateway_radio, gateway_cfs or gateway_fprime. It was also the word for the commander itself, which left one word for both ends of a link. See Gateways: what the vehicle answers to.
test harness
Whatever sets up a test run and takes it down: it starts the simulator, waits for it to be ready, starts tcspecial, and stops what it started. Tcsmoc is one, besides being a ground station; with cFS or F´ as the commander, the harness is not the commander at all. See Waiting for the Simulator.
link
A configured pairing of two endpoints that data passes between. Used of the whole path, where "endpoint" is used of one end of it.
tcspecial configuration file
The file tcspecial is configured by: the command interpreter's own settings in its tcspecial section, and the payload configuration section beside them. No name of its own -- any name ending .yaml or .xml -- the shipped examples being examples/minimal/minimal.yaml and the rest.
payload configuration section
The part of that file describing payloads: which data handlers there are, how tcspecial reaches each one, and how large its packets are. A section rather than a file, because what tcspecial serves and what tcspecial is are one configuration.
simulator configuration file
The file describing how a simulated payload behaves: how often it produces a packet and how it divides one into segments. Properties of a simulation rather than of a payload, which is why they are not in the payload configuration.
stand-in
What a payload endpoint reaches when it does not reach the payload: a pseudo-terminal or a socket that tcssim answers, or a kernel's simulated bus, chip or interface -- i2c-stub, gpio-sim, iio_dummy, vcan. See How a Stand-in Is Recognised.
simulation mode
How many of a run's payloads may be stand-ins: none, some or all, given to each program as --simulate. See Simulation Modes.

The Node Architecture

What this supersedes

TCSpecial began as a set of data handlers, each one a fixed pair: a payload endpoint at one end, an OC endpoint at the other, and a conduit in each direction between them. dh.rs says so in its own fields -- one ground_to_payload and one payload_to_ground -- and that is the whole of the topology the software could express. It served while every payload's data went to the ground and nowhere else.

It cannot express a control loop. Three sensors whose readings are averaged and compared against a setpoint, with the comparison driving heaters, needs a reading to reach two places at once, needs three readings to arrive at one place, needs something aboard that remembers a setpoint, and needs a set of discrete lines to be driven rather than read. None of those is a type of endpoint; all of them are shapes of connection.

So the fixed pair is replaced by a graph the configuration describes. A data handler is no longer a thing: what it did -- joining a device to the ground in both directions -- is two links, and the software that moved its bytes is the machinery that moves bytes along any link.

The four concepts

Deliberately four, and no more. Each is a thing an engineer already thinks about, which is the ease-of-use argument; each is checkable before anything runs, which is the reliability one.

node

Anything the configuration names. A node has a name unique across the configuration, a type, and the attributes its type requires. Three families of type:

devices -- i2c, spi, serial, hdlc, can, gpio, iio, rpmsg, network, device-file -- which are the interfaces real hardware arrives on. Each is what used to be called a payload endpoint, and the attributes are unchanged: a bus and an address, a chip and a set of lines, a path.

MOC links -- moc -- a network link carrying telemetry to the ground and commands back. This is what the OC endpoint was.

compute elements -- compute -- software that transforms what arrives.

sinks -- log -- somewhere data is kept rather than passed on. See The logging node.

generic vehicle nodes -- null-moc, null-payload, tee-moc, tee-payload -- nodes that do nothing to what passes. Each takes one stream: a null node drops it, and a tee sends each datum on both ways, on its own stream and again on the other. See Null nodes and tees.

link checks -- ping -- a node that answers each ping the ground sends it with the vehicle's time. See The ping node.

group

A set of devices read on one interval, so that readings which will be compared with one another are taken as close to together as the hardware allows. A device in no group is read on its own interval, as every payload was.

The interval belongs to the group and not to its members. A member may not state one: two sensors in a group with intervals of their own would be a configuration whose own description disagrees with itself, and it is refused.

link

A directed connection from one node to one or more destinations. One destination is the ordinary case. Several is how a reading reaches the ground and an on-board computation at once -- the arrangement usually drawn as a "T" -- and it is a property of the link rather than a node of its own, because a node that only copied its input would be a type to implement, test, configure and get wrong for no gain.

Within one stream, that is. A link carries one stream and may not carry it into the other, so a copy that has to cross -- what the ground sent, back to the ground as payload data; a reading, to something that takes the ground's data -- is what the tee-moc and tee-payload nodes are for. The crossing is a node, drawn and named, rather than a link that could be drawn backwards by mistake: see Terminals: where a link is attached.

Where a destination takes more than one input, the link names which: a mean over three sensors is three links into three named inputs, and a configuration that leaves one unbound is refused rather than averaging two numbers and a zero.

A link is written as an attribute of the node it leaves, named after the data source it leaves by, with one entry per line:

- name: k_fore
  type: compute
  function: "to_kelvin"
  payload-out:
    - target: moc
    - target: mean_temp
      input: in0
    - target: housekeeping

An entry's target is the node the line goes to and its input is which of that node's inputs it feeds, where it feeds a named one. One entry per line is what makes several of them safe: the alternative was two lists read against each other, and a list one name short is a configuration that reads as valid and delivers a reading to somebody else's input. A word an entry does not know is refused rather than ignored, because targte: moc quietly ignored is a line that goes nowhere.

The short spelling is still read -- payload-out: moc, or payload-out: [moc, mean_temp.in0], the node and its input separated by a dot. Every configuration written before entries existed says what it said, and a link to one place is still a line somebody can write in a line. All of them read into the same thing, because every rule about a link is about where it goes and not about how the file said so.

So there is no links section. A node and the links out of it are read, written and deleted together, which is where a reader of the file wants them: a list at the bottom has to be read against the nodes to make sense of either. It also settles a question the old shape left open -- which file of a configuration a link belongs in. A link is in the file its source node is in, once, however far it reaches.

The node a link arrives at says so as well, under the data sink it arrives at:

- name: mean_temp
  type: compute
  function: "mean"
  inputs: "in0,in1,in2"
  payload-out:
    - target: moc
    - target: thermostat
  payload-in: true

That is a record of the links above and not a link. The point of it is a node whose entry said nothing about what fed it: a sensor's entry named everywhere its reading went, and the computation at the other end named nothing, so a reader had to read the whole configuration against a node to see whether anything reached it. Now a node's entry says it is connected at both ends.

A flag and not the name of the node the line comes from. That name is written on the node the line leaves, which is where the link is; a copy of it here would be the same connection written twice, and at a sink that several lines arrive at one name would read as "and nothing else" -- the ground's sink is such a sink in every configuration, every payload aboard reporting to it. What a node can say about its own sink without repeating the graph is whether anything arrives there, and that is what it says. false is a node saying none does.

Which sink a line arrives at is the stream's own answer -- what leaves by payload-out arrives at a payload-in and nowhere else -- so a destination's named input, mean_temp.in0, counts at the stream's sink. Which input it feeds is written once, on the node the link leaves.

Two statements of one fact can disagree, and the answer to that is not to believe the second one. The flag is checked against the links it is a record of where the configuration is read, and both directions are refused: a sink that says a line arrives where none does, and one that says none does where a link is written. Nothing downstream reads it -- tcslibgs::graph::mirror has it only long enough to check it -- so the graph the vehicle flies comes from one place however many places say so.

It is optional. A file written by hand may say nothing about its sinks and be complete: there is nothing in a flag the links do not already say, and every configuration written before these existed still reads. A word that is neither true nor false is refused saying what the field means, rather than read as a name -- a name there was legal for a while, and a file written against that is better refused than half understood. The editor writes the flags on every node it writes.

In memory they are still a list, because every rule about a link is about both of its ends and a list is what a rule can be written against.

command binding
A statement that a named command from the MOC sets a named attribute of a node. A setpoint arrives this way. The binding is in the configuration, so what the ground is able to change in flight is written down and checkable, rather than being whatever the compute element happens to accept.

The three sections

A configuration's nodes are written in three sections, and they are the three working panes of the editor:

moc
the ground links: the other end of the link, which is not the vehicle at all.
vehicle
what the vehicle itself runs -- computations, logs, pass-throughs, pages of their own.
payloads
what it carries this flight: a device on a bus, a line it drives, a link to something aboard.

Which section a node belongs in is its type's own answer, and there is one statement of it: tcslibgs::graph::section_of. The editor's panes read it and so does the file reader, so a file and a drawing cannot come to disagree about what a node is.

Requirement
A node written in the wrong section is refused where the file is read, naming the node, the section it is in and the one it belongs in. A payload written under vehicle is a file whose own arrangement contradicts the rule the editor enforces everywhere else, and a reader would have to be told which of the two to believe.
Requirement
A section with nothing in it is not written. A configuration with no payloads yet is one somebody is partway through, and payloads: with nothing under it reads back as nothing anyway.

The sets of payloads that share a sampling interval are payload_groups, which is what the simulator's file calls them too: one vocabulary for the two languages, so a reader who has learned one has learned the other.

Terminals: where a link is attached

A link joins one node's data source to another's data sink. Each end has a name, and the name says two things about the data and nothing about what is at the other end: whose it is, and whether it arrives here or leaves. payload- is a payload's own data, on its way to the ground; moc- is what the ground sends, on its way to a payload. -in is where it arrives and -out is where it leaves -- so every ``-out`` is a data source and every ``-in`` a data sink, which is the whole of the rule a reader has to remember. There are four:

stream leaves at arrives at what it carries
groundward payload-out payload-in a payload's readings, on their way to the ground
vehicleward moc-out moc-in what the ground sends back: a command, a trigger, a setpoint

So one stream is named twice -- payload-out where it leaves a node and payload-in where it arrives at the next -- and the two words are a sentence about the same bytes read from either end. The names say nothing about the type at the far end: with no vehicle node in the way, a payload's payload-out connects to a MOC link's payload-in, and with one it connects to that node's payload-in instead and the node's own payload-out carries it the rest of the way.

A link end is written node.terminal, and may be written as node alone wherever the type has one terminal of the kind the end needs: { from: temp_fore, to: moc } is unambiguous, because a payload has one data source and a MOC link has one data sink. A type with two of either has to be told which, which is why the links out of a tee name their terminals.

Which types have which:

payloads
moc-in and payload-out. A payload receives what asks it for a reading -- a trigger, a command, a byte written to a line -- and sends what it read.
MOC links
payload-in and moc-out. The ground is the other end of both.
null-moc
moc-in alone: what the ground sends arrives, and nothing leaves.
null-payload
payload-in alone: a payload's data arrives, and nothing leaves.
tee-moc
moc-in, and both sources, moc-out and payload-out: what the ground sends arrives, and leaves both ways.
tee-payload
payload-in, and both sources, payload-out and moc-out.
app_all_veh
all four, a program with both interfaces: see App nodes.
ping
moc-in and payload-out, a payload's pair: what the ground sends arrives, and the answer leaves groundward, to arrive at a MOC link.

A computation's and a log's terminals are their own rules' business: a computation's data sinks are the inputs it declares, and a log's are whatever is written to it. Both are checked where the type is.

Requirement
A data sink is either absent or linked to one data source. Two sources into one sink would be two streams interleaved on one link, arriving in whatever order they were sent, with nothing to say which was which.
Requirement
A data source is either absent or linked to one data sink or several. One reading reaching the ground and an on-board computation at once is the ordinary case -- the arrangement drawn as a "T".
Requirement
A MOC link's data sink takes as many sources as the configuration gives it. The exception to the rule above, and not one made to let the examples through: the ground is the one place that can tell interleaved streams apart, because each payload's telemetry arrives at its own socket -- payload_telemetry.source_base plus the payload's id -- and the id is what says whose a datagram is. Every payload aboard reporting to one moc node is the ordinary shape of a configuration.
Requirement
A payload or a MOC link with no link on it at all is refused. Either a sink or a source will do: a payload that is only read has a source and no sink, one that is only driven -- a heater, a valve, a latch -- has a sink and no source, and both are configurations somebody means. Neither is a payload the configuration describes and nothing reads or drives.
Requirement

Each terminal may be linked to one other terminal, and the pairing is four sentences: a payload-out only to a payload-in, a payload-in only to a payload-out, a moc-out only to a moc-in, a moc-in only to a moc-out. Which is one sentence twice over -- a stream leaves at one terminal and arrives at the other -- so the pairing is derived from the table above rather than written out again.

The editor is held to it as a gesture: a line dragged from a terminal goes to the terminal that pairs with it and to no other, and a node with none takes nothing -- so a bad link cannot be drawn in the first place. The checking refuses a written-out configuration for the same reason, which is what a file edited by hand is still held to. What that rules out is a link that carries one stream into the other, and with it the circular link a drawing could otherwise be made to say. Loops within the vehicle are still possible -- a vehicle node may feed another and be fed by it -- and this rule is not what would stop one.

Requirement
A link whose two ends carry opposite streams is refused. One end of it is drawn backwards, and nothing else in the configuration would show it: both nodes exist, both terminals exist, and in flight it is a payload that answers nothing.
Requirement
A link end naming a terminal its node does not have is refused, and a terminal named at the wrong end of the link is refused as the mistake it is rather than as a name nothing knows. The name is right and the end is wrong, which is worth saying in those words.
Requirement
A terminal is written into a file only where it says which. A node with one data source and one data sink needs neither name -- { from: temp_fore, to: moc } is unambiguous, and the schema reads an unnamed end as the only terminal of the kind that end needs -- so the editor writes a terminal only where the type has two of the kind, a tee's sources and an app_all_veh's four. A tool that wrote a name into every link of every file it saved would churn every configuration it opened for no gain.
Requirement
A terminal is not a named input. in0 is which of several ways into a computation and the mean is built on knowing; a terminal is which way the data is going, and a node that has one has only one of each kind. So a terminal is resolved where the routing table is built and never reaches a port -- a device's port refuses an input a device does not have, and a configuration that passed the ground check and stopped delivering when it flew would be the worst of both checks.

What tcspecial itself is configured with

Four concepts describe what a vehicle carries and what is done with it. None of them says where any of it goes, and one section does: tcspecial, the same section every configuration holds, and it is required of every one of them.

tcspecial:
  gateway: radio
  tcs_cmd:
    recv: 0.0.0.0:4000
    send: 127.0.0.1:4100
  payload_command:
    source_base: 127.0.0.1:4300
    dest_base: 0.0.0.0:4200
  payload_telemetry:
    source_base: 0.0.0.0:6000
    dest_base: 127.0.0.1:6100
  beacon:
    interval: "5 s"
    send: "0.0.0.0:5550"
    recv: "127.0.0.1:5650"
  log:
    directory: /var/log/tcspecial
    segment_size: 65536
  restart_arm_valid: "20 min"

Five of its seven entries are subsections, because each of them is one thing with more than one attribute to it: the spacecraft command link has two ends, the commands about nodes have two, a payload's telemetry has two, the beacon has two and a timing, and the log has a place and a size. The other two, gateway and restart_arm_valid, are one fact each and are written as one. gateway is what the vehicle answers to -- see Gateways: what the vehicle answers to. A prefix repeated on two or three flat names -- beacon.send, beacon.recv, beacon.interval -- is a subsection somebody declined to write, and the names under one are short for the same reason: beacon.send says what beacon.send said, where the reader is already looking at the beacon.

Ten of the thirteen attributes must be stated, and are required rather than defaulted for one reason: a configuration that has not been asked the question has not answered it. What the vehicle answers to, where commands are taken and where beacons go are mission facts, and a file that was silently given an answer to either is a vehicle nobody can reach whose configuration looks complete. The two in log are the exception, and so is log itself: no log.directory means the telemetry log is not kept, which is a configuration somebody may mean -- a vehicle with no writable storage -- and log.segment_size is a tuning choice with a defensible answer, so it has one.

restart_arm_valid is the third that may be left out: how long a RESTART_ARM licenses a RESTART for, as a length of time -- 20 min, 90 s. Absent is twenty minutes, tcslibgs::commands::RESTART_ARM_VALID_DEFAULT, the length an arm had before it could be configured: long enough to arm, look at what the vehicle is doing and decide over a link with minutes of latency, short enough that an arm left behind by a session that ended does not stand ready for the next RESTART anybody sends. A mission whose link is slower, or whose operators want the window narrower, says so. Nought is refused, being an arm that lapses as it is taken and a vehicle that can never be restarted.

Five of them are a host and a port written together, as address:port: two things only ever used together are one fact about a configuration, and writing them apart invites a file that states one and not the other. Each is split at its last colon, so whatever a host is called the port is the part after it, and the address is kept as text -- what a name resolves to is the business of the moment the socket is made.

tcs_cmd is the spacecraft command link, which is one conversation and two sockets. tcs_cmd.recv is tcspecial's own: it receives commands about the spacecraft there and sends its responses to the MOC from there, a response going back out of the socket its command arrived on. tcs_cmd.send is the MOC's own, which it sends those commands from and receives the responses on. Both ends in one file because both ends read this file: a configuration stating only its own end would leave the other to be told separately, and two places to say one thing is one place for them to disagree.

Separate command links because what the ground needs in a hurry must never be queued behind what it asked for at leisure: a command about the spacecraft -- ping it, arm it, restart it -- is taken on tcs_cmd.recv, and one about a payload on that payload's own socket. On a space link these become virtual channels, which is where the priority between them is really decided; here they are sockets, which is what an IP network has to divide traffic with.

payload_command is where the payloads are commanded, and it has two bases for the reason payload_telemetry does: the link has two ends. A payload's socket on each side is that side's base plus the payload's own id, so four payloads based at 4300 and 4200 are commanded from 4300 to 4303 and take those commands on 4200 to 4203.

Simple payloads -- a device or a socket that tcspecial reads or drives -- do not use payload commands. What a simple payload is configured with is in the tcspecial configuration file, and it is not changed while tcspecial is running. At present only applications, the payloads and vehicle nodes that are programs of their own, use the payload_command values.

One socket per payload rather than one for all of them, which is what payload_interface was. That worked, and left two things undone. Nothing said which socket the ground sent from -- the MOC bound whatever port the system gave it -- so tcspecial could not be told what a payload command should come from, where every other link in this section is written down at both ends. And one socket is one queue: a command about a payload that takes its time to start was ahead of a command about one that would have answered at once.

payload_telemetry is where each payload's own data link is, and it has two bases because the link has two ends. tcspecial binds its end at payload_telemetry.source_base plus the payload's own id -- the position the payload has in the configuration -- and the MOC binds its end at payload_telemetry.dest_base plus the same id. Four payloads based at 6000 and 6100 are at 6000 to 6003 on the vehicle and 6100 to 6103 on the ground.

Both ends come out of the one file both ends read, so neither has to be told the other's: tcspecial sends a payload's telemetry to the end the MOC binds, from the first datagram, and the MOC sends nothing at all.

It did have to be told. The ground's end was whatever port the system chose, so nothing in the configuration said where it was; tcspecial's end learnt it from whatever arrived there, and the MOC taught it by saying hello -- an empty datagram, once a second, to every payload. Once a second because starting or stopping a payload rebound that socket and the learnt peer went with it, so a ground station that said hello once was silently cut off by the next Enable. Naming the ground's end removed the hello, the silence after it, and the last traffic the MOC sent without being asked.

That is why a payload states no address for its ground link. It used to state oc_address and oc_port, which for forty payloads was forty chances to repeat a port -- and a repeated port is two payloads whose data arrives interleaved, each reporting part of the other's stream as though it were the whole. One base and one arithmetic rule cannot be written inconsistently, and a base so high that the payloads counted from it would pass 65535 is refused by name while the file is being read rather than when the socket is made.

The protocol is not stated and cannot be. Every link in this section is UDP, so an attribute for it would be an attribute that could say something untrue -- and a configuration able to describe a link the software cannot make is worse than one that cannot describe it at all.

It is read by one struct wherever it is written. A second statement of what the command interpreter needs would be two statements drifting apart a flight at a time, and the first symptom would be a configuration that verifies on the ground and starts something else aboard.

Only the top file of a configuration may state it. A configuration has one command interpreter, so one of its files says what that is; an included file that declared a section of its own is refused as the configuration is read, rather than quietly ignored, because two sections would be two answers with the winner decided by the order the files happened to be read in.

Gateways: what the vehicle answers to

Specified, and built as far as the F´ gateway node. tcspecial.gateway is read and required; radio and fprime are the kinds it accepts, and a configuration naming cfs is refused as specified but not yet built -- tcslibgs::gateway is the one place that says which kinds are built, which node type is which kind's, and what each calls its far end. The radio is the moc node, under its old name; the F´ gateway is the gateway_fprime node, opened, counted and driven as the radio's is, and examples/fprime flies it. Not yet built: the bridge's F´ model generated from the configuration, and the TcsBridge component itself.

The gateway nodes are written in the moc section, linked through moc-in and moc-out, with tee-moc, null-moc and app_moc_veh beside them. The rename to gateway changes configuration keys, so it is held back to land with the payload rename, when the configuration schema is complete; until then a file says moc wherever this section says gateway, and the radio's node type is moc where this section says gateway_radio. The rest of this section is written in the names it will have, because it is the statement the code is to be built to.

tcspecial was written to answer to the ground. What it does well -- reach a payload over any of the interfaces in Payloads from a configuration alone, and check that configuration before it flies -- is what a flight computer running cFS or F´ does not have: there, each instrument is an application or a component written, built and tested for it. So tcspecial is also to run as the payload processor beside such a computer, answering to it rather than to the ground, and the computer answers to the ground in turn. Whatever tcspecial answers to is its commander, reached through a gateway node, and there are three kinds of gateway:

kind what is at the far end how it is reached what the node states
gateway_radio the ground, through the vehicle's radio UDP, as the moc node is today address, port, ground_base
gateway_cfs a cFS flight computer's software bus as a peer of its Software Bus Network (SBN) application, over SBN's UDP protocol module cmd_msgid, tlm_base
gateway_fprime an F´ flight computer, through a bridge component in its deployment UDP, in the radio's own protocol, the bridge playing the MOC address, port, bridge_base, fprime_id_base

All three are in the gateway section, and all three have the terminals a MOC link has today: payload-in, which takes as many sources as the configuration gives it, and gateway-out.

One kind per configuration

A vehicle answers to one gateway kind, and the tcspecial section says which:

tcspecial:
  gateway: cfs
Requirement
tcspecial.gateway is stated, as radio, cfs or fprime, and has no default. What the vehicle answers to is a mission fact, and a file given an answer it was never asked for is a vehicle that listens for commands where nobody sends them.
Requirement
Every node in the gateway section is of the kind tcspecial.gateway names, and one that is not is refused, naming the node, its type and the kind the configuration states.

One kind, because the gateway is not only where data goes. Commands about the spacecraft, commands about a payload, a payload's own telemetry and the beacon all go to whatever the vehicle answers to, and a configuration that could send its data to cFS and take its commands from the ground by radio is one whose operator has to ask which of two masters a command came from. Every mixture is also a combination to check and to test, for a flexibility no mission has asked for.

So the kind decides how the other links in tcspecial are written, as an endpoint's type decides what locates it:

link radio and fprime: UDP sockets cfs: software bus message IDs
tcs_cmd recv, send, as now cmd_msgid, response_msgid
payload_command source_base, dest_base, as now cmd_base, response_base
payload_telemetry source_base, dest_base, as now tlm_base
beacon interval, send, recv, as now interval, tlm_msgid
the gateway node's data ground_base or bridge_base plus the source's device id tlm_base plus the source's device id
Requirement
A link in tcspecial states the attributes of the gateway kind and no others. A recv in a configuration that answers to cFS is refused by name rather than ignored: it is an address somebody expects to be listened on, and nothing will be.

Every base counts on by the same rule, the one payload_telemetry has now: a base plus the node's own id. A message ID is a number, and counts as a port does; tcslibgs::graph::telemetry stays the one place that counts, for ports and message IDs alike.

The radio: gateway_radio

The moc node, renamed, and nothing else changed. Every requirement on a moc node in What a device node says and Where the Addresses Are is a requirement on it, and its link is UDP, which it will remain.

cFS: gateway_cfs

tcspecial is a process of its own on a payload processor, and cFS's software bus is a set of calls inside the cFS process; nothing outside can call them. What cFS already has for a bus that spans processors is SBN, the Software Bus Network application: each cFS that runs it is a peer, and a message one peer publishes reaches every peer that subscribed to it. tcspecial joins as one more peer, and so needs no new code in the cFS build -- the mission adds a peer to its SBN table, and allots tcspecial its message IDs.

What follows was checked against SBN v7.0.1 and cFE as they stood in October 2026, by reading their source; where a rule rests on one of their details, it says which.

The cost of joining as a peer is that tcspecial speaks SBN's own wire format, which is SBN's to change and has changed between releases. That is answered by naming what the far end was built from, and refusing anything else, at both moments it can be seen.

The peer is one connection for everything the vehicle sends and takes, so it is stated once, in tcspecial:

tcspecial:
  gateway: cfs
  sbn:
    release: "v7.0.1"
    ident: "0653995939d714ac94906f867b4397e9953ca66a"
    spacecraft_id: "0x42"
    processor_id: "2"
    local: "0.0.0.0:2234"
    peer: "10.0.0.1:2234"
    peer_processor_id: "1"
    max_message: "32768"
  msgid_range: { first: "0x4200", last: "0x42FF" }
  tcs_cmd: { cmd_msgid: "0x4280", response_msgid: "0x4200" }
  payload_command: { cmd_base: "0x4290", response_base: "0x4210" }
  payload_telemetry: { tlm_base: "0x4220" }
  beacon: { interval: "5 s", tlm_msgid: "0x4201" }

gateway:
  - name: fsw
    type: gateway_cfs
    cmd_msgid: "0x4281"
    tlm_base: "0x4240"
    payload-in: true

(The numbers are illustrative, and laid out for the message-ID mapping below; the allotment is the mission's.)

What SBN is, on the wire

Every SBN message over UDP is one datagram: a 13-byte header -- the length of what follows as four bytes, the message type as one, the sender's processor ID as four and its spacecraft ID as four, all most significant byte first -- and then the message. The types are a subscription, an unsubscription, an application's message (a cFS packet, carried whole), and SBN's protocol version; the UDP module adds a heartbeat, an announcement and a disconnect of its own. A peer is connected by the first datagram that arrives from it, sends a heartbeat when it has sent nothing for five seconds, and is disconnected by ten seconds of silence. Each of those numbers is compiled into SBN and none can be configured from this side.

Two facts in that shape decide requirements here:

  • SBN on a cFS announces itself only to a peer whose spacecraft ID and processor ID both differ from its own. tcspecial is on the same spacecraft, so cFS will never speak first: the link exists when tcspecial starts it, and not otherwise.
  • What SBN checks a peer by is not a release number. Its protocol version -- SBN_PROTO_VER, 11 in v7.0.1 -- is compared and a difference only logged. What is enforced is SBN_IDENT: 48 bytes that open every subscription message, and a subscription whose bytes are not the receiver's own is refused. Those bytes are git's $Id$ of sbn_types.h -- the hash of that one file as it was checked out -- so they differ between releases, and between a release and a mission's copy of it with that file changed.
Requirement
sbn.release names an SBN release this build of tcspecial speaks, and one it does not is refused where the file is read, naming the releases it does. The release decides the layout of every message.
Requirement
sbn.ident is the hash in the far end's SBN_IDENT, 40 hexadecimal digits, as SBN prints it in its start-up event, and tcspecial puts it into every subscription it sends. It is stated rather than known from the release because it is the far end's build that it has to match, and it has no default because a wrong one is a link that connects, heartbeats, and carries nothing: SBN refuses every subscription, so nothing tcspecial publishes is wanted and nothing it subscribes to is sent.
Requirement
A peer whose protocol-version message is not the release's, or whose subscriptions carry another ident, is refused, and the refusal is logged with what was expected and what arrived, every time the peer tries again: a link that half works is the fault this is here to prevent, and SBN itself only logs the first of the two.
Requirement
tcspecial announces itself to sbn.peer until it is answered, and once connected sends a heartbeat after five seconds without sending anything, as SBN does, so that the far end's ten seconds of silence never come. A peer silent for ten seconds is disconnected on this side too, and announced to again.
Requirement
SBN's UDP protocol module is the one spoken. SBN has others -- TCP, SpaceWire, serial, DTN -- and each would be a second implementation to build, test and keep in step with SBN, for a link between two computers on one vehicle that UDP already serves.
Requirement
sbn.local and sbn.peer are address:port, as every other address in tcspecial is; spacecraft_id, processor_id and peer_processor_id are the numbers SBN identifies peers by, and processor_id and peer_processor_id may not be equal. The far end's SBN table must list tcspecial with the same three, which nothing on this side can check, so the manual says so where these are described.
Requirement
sbn.max_message is the cFS build's CFE_MISSION_SB_MAX_SB_MSG_SIZE -- 32768 in cFE's example -- and has no default, because it is a compile-time setting of the far end. A source whose largest datum, with the packet's headers, would not fit is refused where the file is read, naming the node and both sizes, rather than cut short or dropped as it is sent: SBN drops a message longer than this on arrival, and says so only in an event on the cFS side.

What crosses the bus

Requirement
What crosses the bus is a cFS packet: the CCSDS primary header, cFS's extended header, and a secondary header -- for a command, a function code and a checksum; for telemetry, a time -- and then exactly what the UDP link carries in a datagram. A command's function code is its tcspecial command code, and its data is the command as tcspecial already encodes it; a response, a beacon and a datum are the data of a telemetry packet. Each command, response and the beacon is then still stated once, in tcslibgs, and the gateway changes how it is carried and nothing about what it says.
Requirement
The extended header is the one supported, as a cFS built with MISSION_INCLUDE_CCSDSEXT_HEADER frames every packet, and the primary-only header is not. A cFS built without it is a different packet on the wire, which each end would misread as the other's, so a mission running one cannot use tcspecial until it builds with the extended header. One header is one packing to build and test, and none to state wrongly: the configuration says nothing about it, because there is nothing it could choose.
Requirement
A cFS function code is seven bits, so every tcspecial command code is below 128. tcslibgs holds the codes to that where they are listed, in a test, rather than leaving it to be found by the first code that is not.
Requirement
A command's checksum is checked as it arrives, as cFS's own applications check theirs, and a command whose checksum is wrong is answered as one that could not be read; every command and response tcspecial sends carries a correct one.
Requirement
At start tcspecial subscribes to its command message IDs -- tcs_cmd.cmd_msgid, each payload's payload_command.cmd_base plus its id, and each gateway_cfs node's cmd_msgid -- and to nothing else, and it publishes nothing outside msgid_range.
Requirement
msgid_range is the block of message IDs the mission allotted tcspecial, and every message ID the configuration states, or counts to from a base, lies inside it; one outside it is refused, naming the attribute, the ID and the range. No ID is used twice, and an ID stated as a command's is one the mapping reads as a command, and a telemetry ID one it reads as telemetry. tcspecial cannot see the message-ID header the cFS build was compiled with, so the range is the one fact the two can be held to, and a collision with another application's ID -- two kinds of packet arriving under one ID, each read as the other -- is refused on the ground rather than discovered in flight.
Requirement
CONNECT and the beacon go over the bus as they go over the radio: the first on tcs_cmd, the second published on beacon.tlm_msgid every beacon.interval. What the cFS side does with the beacon -- forward it to the ground, record it, watch it -- is the mission's, and the digest it carries is the same digest.
Requirement
A gateway_cfs node that the bus sends through -- a command binding comes from it, or a line leaves it -- states cmd_msgid, as a gateway_radio node states address and port. What each node drawn into it produces is published at tlm_base plus the source's device id.
Requirement
tcssim stands in for the cFS end as an SBN peer, and tcslib gains an SBN transport, so that tcsmoc can test a configuration that answers to cFS as it tests one that answers to the ground. A run against cFS's own Linux build, with SBN, is the test that the wire format is SBN's and not only ours.
Requirement
A message ID is read and written by cFE's version 2 mapping, which a cFS built with MISSION_MSGID_V2 uses, and by no other: the low seven bits are the low seven of the packet's APID, 0x80 is set for a command and clear for telemetry, and the high byte is the extended header's subsystem. The rest of the APID is zero in every packet tcspecial sends. One mapping is one packing to test, and it matches the extended header the vehicle is built with. A mission's allotment is then naturally one subsystem -- 0x4200 to 0x42FF above being subsystem 0x42, its telemetry below 0x4280 and its commands from there.
Requirement

A telemetry packet's time is tcspecial's own clock when the datum was taken -- the reading's time, not its arrival's -- in cFE's six bytes: seconds as four and 1/65536ths of a second as two, most significant first. It is counted from the epoch, and on the time scale, the cFS build was compiled with, which the configuration states:

tcspecial:
  cfe_time:
    epoch: "1980-01-01T00:00:00"
    scale: tai
    leap_seconds: "37"

epoch is a UTC date and time, scale is tai or utc, and leap_seconds -- TAI less UTC -- is stated where the scale is TAI and refused where it is UTC. None has a default: each copies a compile-time setting of the far end -- CFE_MISSION_TIME_EPOCH_*, CFE_MISSION_TIME_CFG_DEFAULT_TAI -- and a wrong one is every packet stamped wrong by the same amount, which nothing downstream can see. The leap-second count is fixed for as long as the configuration is, and a change is a new configuration; none has been added since 2017.

Requirement
Commands are carried tunnelled, as stated above, for now. A cFS ground system sees a tcspecial command as its function code and its bytes, and stating each command's parameters in XTCE, so that one can show them, is a later step.

F´: gateway_fprime

The F´ end is a component in the F´ deployment, TcsBridge, built on tcslib-c and so in C++ against the header every C and C++ ground station uses. To tcspecial it is a MOC: it opens the same configuration, sends CONNECT and compares digests, takes beacons, sends commands and receives data, all in the radio's own UDP protocol. To the rest of the deployment it is a component like any other, with commands in and telemetry channels out, and the deployment sends them to the ground with whatever it already uses.

tcspecial:
  gateway: fprime
  # tcs_cmd, payload_command, payload_telemetry and beacon as for the radio

gateway:
  - name: fsw
    type: gateway_fprime
    address: "0.0.0.0"
    port: "7600"
    bridge_base: "10.0.0.1:7700"
    fprime_id_base: "0x3000"
    payload-in: true

A type of its own although the wire is the radio's, because what is at the far end decides what the configuration has to say and what can be made from it.

Requirement
A gateway_fprime node states bridge_base where a radio states ground_base, with the same meaning and the same counting, and states address and port under the same rule a radio does.
Requirement
fprime_id_base is where the F´ IDs of what tcspecial sends begin: each source drawn into the node is the channel at fprime_id_base plus its device id, named after the node. The bridge's F´ model -- its channels and their names -- is generated from the configuration, by the same code that counts ports, and never written by hand, so the F´ dictionary and tcspecial cannot disagree about which channel is which. An ID the generated model would repeat is refused where the file is read.
Requirement
The component lives in fprime/ in this tree, is built with F´'s own tools rather than cargo, and is tested by an F´ deployment that runs it against tcssim, as tcsmoc is tested against it.
Requirement
The bridge allocates nothing once the deployment is running, as a ground station's loop built on tcslib does not: a datum is taken into a buffer made when the component was, and a channel is written from it.

What a device node says

A node of an interface type says where it is, what the link is set up as, and how it is read. The second of those -- a data rate, a bus address, a clock mode, a set of line numbers -- is written once, in tcslibgs, per type: those rules are the ones that have been tested against real buses, and a second statement of them would be two statements of every rule drifting apart one flight at a time. A node's attributes are handed to those per-type rules, which refuse in the same words wherever the node is written.

What locates a node depends on its type:

network, moc    address, port, and for network the protocol
i2c             path (the bus) and address (the device on it)
can             interface and can_id
everything else path

And every interface type may say, beyond the terms of its own link:

group           which group reads it, which the schedule reads
read_size       how many bytes one read asks for
trigger         what to write to make it answer
max_length      where one read of a line ends. A node's attributes are
read_timeout    flat, so these are written beside the line's terms
terminators     rather than nested
length_prefix
Requirement
An attribute nothing reads is refused, by name, for the type it is written on. A term belonging to another type is refused by that type's own rules; a term belonging to no type at all would otherwise vanish, and max_speeed ignored is a peripheral clocked at whatever the last program left the bus at, which looks exactly like a configured one.
Requirement
An attribute that locates another type is refused. A port on a node reached through a device file is a file that looks configured and is not.
Requirement
read_size bounds a read, and the buffer offered bounds it too: the smaller wins. A bus master clocks exactly as many bytes as it asks for, so a sensor that answers in two bytes read sixty-four at a time is a sensor clocked sixty-two bytes past the end of what it has to say.
Requirement
A node with a trigger is a node that is asked, and there is no separate word for the mode: a trigger and nothing else is what makes a node triggered, so a second word could only disagree with it.
Requirement
A trigger on an interface that cannot be asked is refused. A set of discrete lines is read or driven and not both, so a trigger written on one is bytes nothing would send; an IIO device's sample buffer is read and never written, what such a device is configured with being in sysfs and not in the character device, so a trigger there would be a question asked down a path that carries answers. Both are refused with the configuration rather than at the first read, because what the node would then do is nothing at all -- a vehicle that came up and asked a question it cannot ask would have a sensor reporting nothing and nothing saying why.
Requirement
A read_size larger than one read of the interface carries is refused, and the message names both numbers. A CAN frame carries eight bytes, or sixty-four on an FD bus, and an rpmsg message carries what the channel's max_message_bytes says, 496 by default. A read asking for more than that is refused rather than split across two frames: a reading assembled out of two frames is a reading that half arrives when one of them is lost, and nothing in the configuration says how to put the halves back together.
Requirement
A port of 0 is refused. It is the one number a socket would accept and a configuration cannot mean -- it asks the kernel for whichever port is free, so a configuration stating it describes a link nothing could be told where to find.
Requirement
A moc node needs no protocol and a network node does. A ground link is a datagram link, as the one tcspecial has always had is; a device reached over a socket could be any of the four, and guessing which is how a stream payload comes to be addressed as datagrams.
Requirement
A moc node is the ground's end of the vehicle's data, and states where the ground is as ground_base, address:port. What each node drawn into it produces is sent by tcspecial as UDP datagrams to that address, at that port plus the source node's device id -- its position among all the configuration's nodes, in the order the files write them (tcslibgs::graph::order). So each source arrives on a port of its own and the ground tells them apart by the port, and the vehicle sends from the first reading without being spoken to. tcslibgs::graph::telemetry is the one place that counts the ports, for both ends; two sources landing on one port are refused.
Requirement
The ground and the vehicle are held to one configuration. Both digest the configuration files they read with one function, tcslibgs::config_digest::digest_of_graph: an MD5 over everything the vehicle and the ground act on -- the tcspecial section, the payload groups, the nodes in the order the files write them with their attributes, the links and the command bindings -- across every included file, the same for either spelling, and not the editor's layout or the description. tcspecial says its digest as it starts and sends it in a beacon every beacon.interval, from beacon.send to beacon.recv. tcsmoc compares the digest in each beacon with the one it computed itself; when they differ it says so on the beacon line and the console and asks the operator whether to continue anyway, quitting -- and stopping the tcspecial and tcssim it started -- if not.
Requirement
Ground software gets a moc node's data with a function call -- tcslib::moc::Moc::open names the node in the configuration, and Moc::receive returns each datum with the name of the node that produced it -- rather than opening sockets of its own. One function between the configuration and the data is one place that counts ports, and it counts them as the vehicle does.
Requirement
A ground station's loop allocates nothing once it runs: a beacon pass, a datum taken with Moc::receive_into into a buffer of moc::DATUM_BYTES made beforehand, a ping and the folding in of its answer, and a command sent each reuse room made when they were opened. A datum longer than the buffer is refused, saying how long it was, rather than cut short. What still allocates does so only where something happens -- a remark on the beacon pass where something changed, the answer to a command, a datum that is not an answer to a ping -- and says so where it does; Moc::receive, which hands each datum back to be kept, allocates it, and the ground programs here -- tcsmoc and lorem_moc -- use receive_into.
Requirement
A moc node that the ground sends through -- a command binding comes from it, or a line leaves it -- states the address and port tcspecial binds to receive what the ground sends. One that only receives the vehicle's data states neither. ground_base is refused on every other node.

Numbers, as a configuration writes them

Every integer in every file this program reads has one grammar, in tcslibgs::whole: decimal, or hexadecimal with an 0x prefix, with underscores anywhere among the digits. 1000, 1_000, 0x3e8 and 0X3E8 are one number written four ways, and the underscores carry no meaning beyond grouping digits for a reader.

Hexadecimal because half of these numbers are hardware's own: an I2C address is 0x48 in every datasheet and 72 in none of them, and a configuration that made somebody convert it by hand would be a configuration with a transcription error in it. Underscores because a number long enough to be miscounted is a number somebody will miscount, and they are the convention Rust and most other languages already use, so nobody has to learn one here.

No sign. Every integer in these files counts something -- bytes, ports, microseconds, retries -- and a negative count is not a value to refuse later but a thing to refuse where it is read.

Requirement
One grammar, in one place. These numbers were parsed in five places with three slightly different grammars -- one took hex, one did not, one took a sign nothing wanted -- which is three answers to "may I write this in hex", and which answer somebody got depended on which field they were filling in.
Requirement
Two different complaints, because they have two different answers: a number that is not one at all, and a number too large for what it is counting. The second names the limit, since "65536 is not a port" is only useful beside "a port is 1 to 65535".

Every length of time in a configuration is a number, whitespace, and a unit, and the units are these:

The units of a length of time
Unit What it is Written
us microseconds 250 us
ms milliseconds 500 ms
s seconds 3 s, 1.5 s
min minutes 15 min
h hours 6 h
d days 1 d, 0.5 d
w weeks 1 w

The number is decimal, whole or a fraction, with underscores allowed among the digits: 1_000 ms, 1.5 s and 1500 ms are one length of time written three ways. The value is kept in whole microseconds, rounded to the nearest, so a fraction finer than a microsecond is rounded rather than silently truncated. There is no hexadecimal and none is planned, and no sign and no exponent: a length is neither negative nor written for a machine to read.

Requirement
One or more whitespace characters stand between the number and the unit. A tab reads as a space does and a run of them reads as one, so 500 ms and 500  ms are the same length of time, and 500ms is refused -- told to write the space rather than told that 500ms is not a unit, which is a message about the wrong half of the value.

The separator is what makes the two halves two things. A value written together has to be taken apart by a reader that knows every unit there is and looks for one at the end, which is how the longest match and its surprises arrive -- and it is what would stand in the way of the number ever growing a notation of its own, hexadecimal being the obvious one. Nothing supports hexadecimal today and nothing is planned to; the point is that the grammar no longer forecloses it.

Every unit from a microsecond to a week, because a reading taken once a week is written 1 w and not 604800 s, and a number nobody can read at a glance is a number nobody can check. The same argument covers the middle of the range: 15 min and 6 h are how a schedule is discussed, and making somebody write 900 s and 21600 s is making them do the arithmetic the mistake hides in.

Nothing in the table is ambiguous, and that is the reason minutes are min. A single m is one letter from ms and a thousand-fold apart from it, so a slip of a finger would have made a reading taken sixty thousand times less often with nothing in the file to say so. It is refused by name, and so is anything else that could be read two ways:

m
Say min for minutes or ms for milliseconds.
a month or a year -- mon, mo, month, y, yr, year
Not lengths of time at all. A month needs a calendar, a reference date and a timezone, none of which a vehicle carries; a span of about that size is written exactly, as 30 d or a number of days. These are refused so that nobody writes one and is answered by whatever a calendar would have said.
µs
Say us. This grammar is ASCII.

An interval of nought is refused in every unit -- it is no interval at all, and a schedule asking for one is a schedule asking to be run again before it has finished -- and so is a fraction that rounds to nought, which is the same thing spelled differently. So is a length too long to count: a number that overflowed rather than an interval anybody meant.

Where a configuration does mean "as fast as that can be done" it says so in a word rather than in a length of time: a simulated payload's packet_interval and segment_interval take continuous, and nought is refused there as it is everywhere else.

Requirement
A value that means "as fast as it can be driven" is a word and not nought. Nought is what a file arrives at by accident -- an interval computed to nothing, a field cleared to a zero, a setting carried over from a reader that counted milliseconds -- and what it would ask for here is the fastest thing this software does, which nobody asks for by accident. A file writing 0 s is refused and told the word, and the word is read only where it means something: how often a packet is produced, and how often the segments of one are. A jitter of continuous is refused for the same reason from the other side, a packet not being able to be late continuously.

The unit is a word of its own, so nothing depends on which spelling ends with which: 1 w, 7 d and 168 h are one interval, and ms is read as ms rather than as an m that happens to precede an s.

A timeout takes everything in the table and one word besides: none, which is a read that does not time out at all. That word is the whole of the difference between the two, and it is why an attribute says which of the two it takes rather than leaving it to be guessed from the attribute's name -- see the kind of an attribute in the node description tree, which is interval or timeout. A computation's timeout is an interval for that reason: a deadline that may be none is a program that may hang.

One reader for all of it, graph::parse_interval, with the timeout's word in front of it in endpoint_config::parse_timeout. There were two grammars, differing by two units and by the fraction, and a configuration could say 1.5 s in one place and not the other with nothing to say why.

So a timeout takes every unit in the table, including the ones no read would sensibly use. A timeout of hours is a configuration that will look like a hang, which is a thing to notice in review rather than a thing to spell differently from every other length of time in the files.

The logging node

Keyword type: log records what reaches it, built on tcslog -- the same library the telemetry log already uses, so a log node is a generalisation of something the software does rather than a new mechanism to trust. Two attributes:

directory      where the log's segment files are written
format         fixed: <n> | variable_simple | variable_ts_rc

It is the first node with no fixed number of inputs. Any number of sources may link into one log, which is the point of it: a flight's housekeeping is one log holding what several devices produced, and a reader of that log wants the records interleaved as they happened rather than a file per sensor. A node with named inputs, like a mean over three sensors, is the other case and is checked the other way.

What "any number" does not mean is any type. A source is accepted only if its records fit the format the log was opened with, and the three formats accept different things:

fixed: <n>
Every record holds exactly n bytes and no data header is written, which is the most compact log and the one whose length cannot change for the life of the log. A source is compatible only if every record it produces is exactly n bytes -- a device read in fixed-size packets, a computation with a fixed-width result. A source whose records vary is refused, naming the source, the log and the two lengths.
variable_simple
Records of nought to tcslog::RecSize::MAX bytes, each with a four-byte length header. Any source whose largest record fits is compatible. Suited to data that already carries its own timestamp.
variable_ts_rc
As variable_simple, and the header also carries the time the record was written and its position in the session. The format to choose when the question asked of the log later will be when, which for a control loop it usually is.

The compatibility is settled when the configuration is loaded, not when a record is written. A log that would have to refuse a record in flight is a log that loses telemetry at the moment telemetry is worth most, so the arrangement is refused on the ground instead: either every source fits the format or the configuration does not load.

A log with no source links into it records nothing, and is named at load for the same reason a node with no outbound link is -- a configuration that cannot do anything is more likely a mistake than an intention.

Requirement
A temperature a configuration states is held to the range a value carries, and the attributes that are temperatures are the ones held to it: a hysteresis's setpoint and half_band, and a conversion's offset, which is what a count of nought converts to. A scale is not -- it is kelvin per count rather than a temperature, and what it may be depends on the part's counts, which no file states.
Requirement
A fixed-length log takes only sources whose record length the configuration states, which means a device with a read_size. A computation's length is its function's and not the file's -- four bytes from one built-in and one byte of a decision from another -- and it is deliberately not read off the function: a configuration that fitted a log only because of what a built-in happens to produce would be made valid or invalid by a change to tcspecial, which is not something a file can be checked against. A source whose length is not knowable is refused with that reason rather than with a number it does not have. The two variable formats take any source.
Requirement
A log is never read, and no link may leave one. It is where data stops; a link out of a log would be a log read back into the vehicle that wrote it.
Requirement
Segment files are named for the node: log-<node>- and the sequence tcslog gives them. Two logs may write into one directory -- a housekeeping log and a log of one noisy instrument, say -- and a reader of that directory has to be able to tell which files are which.
Requirement
A log node may say segment_bytes and need not. The default is the telemetry log's, because a log node whose segment size differed from it for no stated reason would be two answers to one question; a segment smaller than one record is refused, being a log that would fill a file per record and then fail on the one that would not fit.
Requirement
The directory must already exist. A log node is not the thing to be creating directories on a vehicle: a mistyped path created would become a log nobody is looking in, where a mistyped path refused is a configuration somebody fixes.
Requirement
A record is not flushed as it is written. A log flushed per arrival is a write system call per reading, and the records are in the kernel's hands either way; what is flushed is a pass, and anything the log should be on the far side of.
Requirement
A write that fails is reported and counted, not swallowed. The fabric attempts each destination of a link independently, so a log that cannot be written does not stop the ground getting its telemetry -- which is exactly why the failure must be visible: a silent one would be a log that stopped recording with everything else still working.
nodes:
  - { name: housekeeping, type: log, directory: /var/log/tcspecial,
      format: variable_ts_rc }

links:
  - { from: k_fore, to: [moc, mean_temp.in0, housekeeping] }
  - { from: k_aft, to: [moc, mean_temp.in1, housekeeping] }
  - { from: k_bay, to: [moc, mean_temp.in2, housekeeping] }
  - { from: thermostat, to: [heaters, housekeeping] }

That is the thermostat's three converted readings and what the comparison decided, in one log, in the order they happened -- which is what reconstructing the loop's behaviour afterwards requires, and what a log per sensor could not give.

Null nodes and tees

Four vehicle nodes that do nothing to what passes, each taking one stream. They replace the null node, which took both streams and passed both on through one queue: what arrived from the payload side and what arrived from the ground left by every link it had, so a configuration that used both of its streams sent commands to the ground and readings to the payloads, which nothing in the file showed.

null-moc and null-payload
take one stream -- what the ground sends at a null-moc's moc-in, a payload's data at a null-payload's payload-in -- and drop it. For a stream that has to go somewhere and has nowhere to go yet. They have no data source.
tee-moc and tee-payload
take one stream and send each datum on both ways. A tee-moc sends what arrives at its moc-in on by its moc-out, as it was going, and again by its payload-out; a tee-payload sends what arrives at its payload-in on by its payload-out and again by its moc-out.
moc:
  - { name: ground, type: moc, address: 127.0.0.1, port: "5000",
      ground_base: "127.0.0.1:5100", moc-out: echo }
payloads:
  - { name: heater, type: gpio, direction: output, lines: "4",
      path: /dev/gpiochip0 }
vehicle:
  # What the ground sends the heater, and back to the ground to be seen.
  - name: echo
    type: tee-moc
    moc-out: [heater]
    payload-out: [ground]
Requirement
A tee delivers each datum that arrives at it to every link leaving each of its data sources, unchanged and in the order it arrived: one copy for each link, each link's copy on that link's stream. A data source with no link on it sends nothing, so a tee with one source linked is a pass-through.
Requirement
A null node accepts every datum delivered to it and keeps none. Each is counted as delivered, because it was.
Requirement
None of the four takes an attribute, and one written on any of them is refused naming it: what each does is fixed, and a word nothing reads is most likely left behind by a type that was changed.
Requirement
A null node is refused as a type that does not exist, naming the types there are. Which of the four replaces one depends on which of its streams the configuration used, and that is a choice the file's author makes, not the reader.

The ping node

type: ping answers the ground. Each message the MOC sends it comes back with the time the vehicle had it written on the end, so that the ground can see how long a message takes to reach the vehicle and to come back, and how far apart the two clocks are -- which a ping is the one exchange able to show.

moc:
  - { name: ground, type: moc, address: 127.0.0.1, port: "5000",
      ground_base: "127.0.0.1:5100", moc-out: pinger }
vehicle:
  - { name: pinger, type: ping, payload-out: ground }

The ground sends its pings to the MOC link's address and port, and the answers arrive at its ground_base plus the ping node's device id, as any node's telemetry does.

Requirement
A ping is ping # followed by the count, a space, and the time the MOC sent it: ping #1 1760900000.000000001. The count is a decimal number with no leading nought, which is one for the first ping and one more for each after it. The time is the number of seconds since the UNIX epoch, with exactly nine digits after the point, so that it is written to the nanosecond.
Requirement
The answer is the ping as it arrived, one space, and the vehicle's time, written as the ping's time is: ping #1 1760900000.000000001 1760900000.250000000. The vehicle's time is the time the ping was handed to the node, which is the vehicle's account of when it arrived.
Requirement
Every number has a largest value -- a count and a whole number of seconds are each at most u64::MAX, twenty digits -- so a ping is at most 57 bytes and an answer at most 88. A ping is answered in a buffer of the second size, which holds the largest ping, the space and the largest time the vehicle could add: an answer is never cut short, and nothing is allocated per ping.
Requirement
A message that is not a ping is refused and not answered, and the refusal is logged aboard naming the node and what was wrong. An answer to it would be a message the ground could not read as one, which tells it less.
Requirement
A ping node has no attributes, and one written on it is refused when the configuration is checked: what it does is fixed by the format.
Requirement
A ping node may be neither stopped nor started from the ground. A STOP_NODE or START_NODE about one is answered InvalidCommand and changes nothing. A ping node is how the ground learns that the link and the vehicle are alive, and a check that can be switched off is one that can be silenced by mistake, after which a vehicle that is well and one that is not look the same from the ground. It comes up enabled, as every node does, and so it stays.

What a ping and its answer say is tcslibgs::node::vehicle::ping, which a ground station writes pings and reads answers with: the format is stated once, for both ends. The node is tcspecial::node::vehicle::ping.

Compute elements

Two of them, and the difference is where the code lives.

What a computation reads and writes is a value: four bytes of a signed count of micro-kelvin, least significant byte first. A reading is a value from the conversion that made it to the computation that uses it, to the log that records it and to the link that carries it to the ground, and it is a value in kelvins -- one unit everywhere, so that a comparison between two readings is a comparison and not a conversion.

Decimal text was tried first and gave the number up a digit at a time: a temperature written to four places is not the number the sensor's counts converted to, every hop rounded it again, and a vote between three sensors was being decided on the fourth decimal place of numbers that had each lost it. What replaced it is a whole number of a unit finer than anything these sensors resolve -- the parts the examples use report in sixteenths and hundredths of a kelvin -- so nothing a part said is lost in the rounding, and the number on the link is then exact. That is the property the arithmetic wants. Three doubles of one temperature can differ in their last bit, and a comparison between them turns on dust; three counts of micro-kelvin are equal or they are not. Little endian because every other number this software puts on a wire is: a timestamp's microseconds, a node's id, a beacon interval. A sensor's own bytes are the exception, and say which way round they are.

Signed, although a temperature in kelvin is not. The arithmetic that produces one is signed -- a conversion is counts * scale + offset, and a wrong scale produces a negative, which has to arrive as a negative to be seen for what it is rather than as a temperature near 4295 K. And a difference of two temperatures is a width, which a band is, and that is signed by nature.

The range is what it costs, and it is stated rather than worked around: a signed count of micro-kelvin reaches 2147.483647 K either way. That is above anything these nodes are pointed at and below a great deal that exists -- a thruster's throat is hotter -- so a temperature outside it is refused, and refused where the value is made, which is where the number is still in hand to name in the complaint. Not wrapped, which would turn 2200 K into a cabin 42 K below zero, and not pinned to the end of the range, which would turn a runaway into a part that looks hot but surviving: either is a thermostat acting confidently on a number nobody produced. A node that must carry more than this wants a unit of its own, and that is a change to make deliberately.

The same range holds a temperature a file states, and that is checked where a file is read: a setpoint of 3000 K is a thermostat whose readings can never reach it, so the heaters would be driven on and held there for the whole flight, and an offset of -2500 K is every reading of that part outside the range. On a desk each is one line to change, which is the argument for the rule living with the other rules. The form of a value lives there too, in tcslibgs: the range and the representation are the same fact, and half of a fact in each crate is two places for it to drift. It is also one thing the vehicle writes and the ground reads back, so it belongs beside the protocol the two already share -- a ground station that decodes a reading rather than showing four bytes of hexadecimal has it to hand.

Four bytes carry one other consequence worth saying plainly. Every four bytes are a value, there being no bit pattern of a 32-bit integer that is not a count of micro-kelvin, so a four-character word delivered where a reading was meant would be read as a temperature -- where eight bytes of a double at least refused a NaN. What catches a datum that is not a reading is its width, and almost nothing a link can deliver here is four bytes long: a sensor's raw bytes are two or three, a decision is one, a line of text is longer. The one that is would not be caught, and nothing downstream could catch it either -- which is the argument for the conversions being the only nodes that make values.

A decision is not a value and is still text: 1 or 0, one byte, which is what a thermostat sends a heater. A device driven by a decision is driven by the byte the configuration says that device wants, and a heater's line is not a number. Where a value becomes text -- a program's pipe, a command's value from the ground -- the conversion happens at that boundary and nowhere else.

The rest of the cost is paid where data is shown rather than used. A log holds four bytes per reading where it held six or seven characters, and a panel showing the head of what arrived shows four bytes of hexadecimal where it showed a number somebody could read.
