The log is read by a program that knows the format; the panel is the one place a reader loses something, and it is a reading nobody is deciding on.

A built-in names a function compiled into tcspecial: to_kelvin, mean, hysteresis and the rest of a catalogue that grows as flights need it. These are the ones to prefer. They are written in Rust, tested with the rest of the software, cannot fail to start, and cost nothing to call.

An external process names a program. tcspecial starts it, writes what arrives on its standard input and reads results from its standard output. It buys a computation that can be written in anything and changed without rebuilding the flight software, which is why it is here; it costs a process to supervise, and that is a new way to fail. Reliability outranks the flexibility, so the arrangement is deliberately strict:

  • the program is started when the configuration is loaded, not at the first datum, so a program that cannot be executed is a configuration fault found on the ground.
  • every result has a deadline. A program that has not answered within its timeout is treated as having failed, not waited for.
  • a failed or exited program does not silently swallow data. Its link is refused: what would have gone into it is dropped, counted, and reported in telemetry, so the ground sees a computation stop rather than seeing plausible numbers that are stale.
  • restarting is declared, and the answer is yes or no. restart: false is what a node that says nothing means, because a heater driven by a computation that restarts under load is worse than a heater driven by nothing -- so coming back at all is something a configuration has to ask for, in the word true.
  • a built-in and an external process are interchangeable in the graph, so a computation can be proved as a program and later moved into the catalogue without the configuration changing shape.

A node that runs a program names it in executable -- the same word an app node names its application with, a program being a program -- says how long it may take, and says whether it may be restarted:

- { name: attitude, type: compute, function: process,
    executable: /opt/flight/attitude, timeout: 500 ms, restart: false }

What the program sees is a line per arrival on its standard input and a line per answer on its standard output:

in:  293.15          an arrival on a link that names no input
in:  in1=293.15      an arrival on a link that names one
out: 291.0           a result, which is passed on
out:                 an empty line: nothing to send yet
Requirement
The program is handed lines and answers in lines, in decimal. A value on a link is four bytes of micro-kelvin, and tcspecial converts at the pipe in both directions: a line is what every language reads without a library, which is the point of this type of node existing at all, and a program that had to unpack a little-endian integer and scale it would not be writable in a shell. Nothing is lost by the trip -- a value is a whole number of micro-kelvin, so six decimal places say all of it -- and a program's answer is held to the range a reading is held to, with the complaint naming the program.
Requirement
An empty line is the program saying "nothing to send yet", and is the same answer a built-in gives by handing back nothing. A computation over several inputs has to be able to say it: an external mean over three sensors answers on the third arrival and not before, and a program that answered something on every line would have to invent a value twice per pass.
Requirement
A program that misses its deadline is put down, not left running. It still holds the datum it was given, so a second datum handed to it would be answered with the first one's result -- a control loop reading last pass's number as this pass's.
Requirement
A datum that went into a program which then failed is refused, including when the program was started again. It went into a computation that died without answering, and saying otherwise would be inventing a result.
Requirement
A restart happens at once, in the pass the program died in, rather than at the next datum. A vehicle is better off learning what became of the program -- that it is back, or that it is not coming back -- while something is still watching the pass that killed it.
Requirement
A program is killed and waited for when its node goes. A child that is killed and not waited for is a zombie, and a vehicle that restarted a computation a few times would collect one per restart; a vehicle that stopped and left one running would leave the next start to find a program already holding whatever the last one held.
Requirement
Standard error is left alone. Whatever the program complains about arrives wherever tcspecial's own complaints do, which is where somebody reading a vehicle's log is already looking.
Requirement
A child inherits nothing but its two pipes and the standard error it shares. Everything this software opens, it opens close-on-exec, so a computation starts without the command socket, without the chip another node holds, and without the log's segment file -- none of which it has any business with, and one of which, a chip's line request, is given to a single owner at a time. Sweeping the descriptors closed in the child by hand was tried and taken out again: the descriptor the standard library reports a failed exec on is one such a sweep closes, and with it closed a program that cannot be run looks like a program that started.
Requirement
A blocking receive that a signal interrupts is waited on again, not treated as a failure. This matters because of the node above: a computation exiting is enough to interrupt a receive elsewhere in the process, and the two command loops used to return on it -- which would have stopped the link a spacecraft is commanded over because a program on it had finished.

App nodes

The other way of putting a program of somebody's own into the graph, and the one that does not pretend to be arithmetic. A compute node whose function is process runs a program on values: a line of text in, a line of text out, one answer per arrival. An app node runs a program on streams: packets arrive, packets leave, and how many of the second follow from the first is the program's own business. Which is what a part of the telemetry and control system looks like when it is not a calculation -- a protocol converter, a recorder, something written in another language because that is where it already exists.

Five node types, differing in which of two file interfaces the program is run with and on which side of the graph the node stands. Three are the vehicle's own, parts of the telemetry and control system itself:

app_payload_veh
Reads payload data, writes MOC data. A sink for what travels groundward and a source of what travels vehicleward, which is the shape the ground link has: it takes readings and answers with commands.
app_moc_veh
The mirror of it. Reads MOC data, writes payload data, which is the shape a payload has: it is asked for something and answers with it.
app_all_veh
Both, and so all four terminals. The one kind that can carry a stream through -- payload data in at the one interface and out at the other -- and the one type that carries both, each kept apart, with something done to what passes.

And two are payloads: what the vehicle carries this flight, where what it carries is a program the mission wrote rather than a device on a bus. A payload sends readings and is sent what asks for them, so neither of these is run with the payload interface -- payload data is what they write, and the interface payload data is written on is the MOC one. Both are written in the payloads section, drawn on the payload pane, and read the way a sensor is read.

app_pl
Writes payload data and reads nothing. A payload that is never asked: it samples on a clock of its own, or reports when something happens, and what decides is in the program. The one node type in the whole configuration with no data sink at all, which is not an omission but the statement of what it is -- there is nothing to ask it with, so nothing may be linked into it.
app_all_pl
Reads MOC data, writes payload data, which is a payload's own shape: asked for a reading, and answering with one. It states no trigger as a device on a bus does, and for the reason a trigger exists: the bytes that ask a sensor are written before a read because a sensor has no other way of being told, where a program is told by being given the packet.

The payload pair hold the same interface an app_moc_veh holds and do the same two things with it. What tells the node types apart is which side of the graph each stands on -- which section of the file, which pane of the editor, which family of tcspecial::node opens it -- and not anything the program can see. Which is the right place for the difference to live: a program that answers what it is asked is the same program whether the thing asking is the ground or the vehicle's own graph.

The interfaces are files in the sense that matters: things read and written the way a file is, which every language already has a library for. On a UNIX-like system they are the program's own descriptors 0, 1 and 2, inherited when tcspecial starts it -- the first two each one end of a socket pair, because each of them is read and written, and the third for what went wrong. A program that can read its standard input can take part.

Every interface carries packets rather than bytes: four bytes of length and then that many bytes. The length is in the machine's own byte order, which is what makes it four bytes and not a stated encoding -- both ends are processes on one vehicle, started by one program, so there is no byte order to agree on. A zero-length packet is a packet, and that is deliberate: a program with nothing to say and a program that has said something empty are different things, so reading answers with an Option and the interface ending is the None.

An interface the app does not support is not opened for it at all: the descriptor is closed in the child between the fork and the exec, so reaching for it is EBADF -- for any program that leaves it closed. A Rust program is not such a program, and the reason is worth writing down: its standard library opens /dev/null over any of descriptors 0, 1 and 2 that it finds closed, before main runs, guarding against this very hazard -- a closed standard descriptor is the number the next open hands out, and then a write meant for the terminal goes into somebody's file. So what a Rust app finds at an interface it does not support is a descriptor that swallows what is written and reads as having ended: no error, but no data either.

Which is why the refusal that matters is the one that needs no descriptor. An app holds a type with nothing on it for the interface it has not got, so reaching for it does not compile; the closed descriptor is the second line of defence, and the third is that an app says at once when what it was handed is not what its kind is run with -- a socket where it should have none, or none where it needs one. Asked of what the descriptor is rather than of whether it is open, for the reason above.

What a configuration says about one is where the application is, what to tell it about each stream it handles, and what to do when it stops. executable is required and there is no default: an app node is the program it names. A leading ~ in it is the home directory and $HOME is the value of the environment variable HOME, both expanded as a shell would expand them, and that happens where the program is run rather than where the file is read -- the value is kept as written, so a ground tool is not answering a question about its own workstation. A process computation names its program with executable as well, one program being much like another. Nothing else is expanded: another variable would be a configuration that behaved differently on two vehicles, and ~somebody is refused rather than guessed at. restart is the same attribute a process computation takes, read in the same one place and false unless a configuration says true, because a part of the control system that restarts under load is worse than one that stays stopped and says so. It is started when the configuration is loaded rather than at the first datum, so a program that cannot be executed is a fault found on the ground.

What it is told on its command line is a short, fixed list. The name of the node it is comes first, for every kind: one program may be several nodes of one configuration, and three processes that cannot say which of them each is are three processes whose complaints an operator cannot act on. Then one argument per stream the kind handles -- app-payload-arg for the kinds that handle payload data, app-moc-arg for the kinds that handle MOC data, payload before MOC where a kind has both:

vehicle:
  - name: through
    type: app_all_veh
    executable: $HOME/libexec/tcs/through
    app-payload-arg: --readings
    app-moc-arg: --commands

payloads:
  - name: bay_2
    type: app_pl
    executable: ~/libexec/tcs/bay-2
    app-payload-arg: --bay-2

which are run as through --readings --commands and bay_2 --bay-2, each after its program's own path. Both attributes are required of the kinds that take them, and refused of the kinds that do not. Required because the order is the whole of what makes the list readable from the program end: a list whose length depends on which attributes somebody wrote is a list a program has to count before it can read, and it would count wrongly on the day a node's type changed. Refused because an app-moc-arg on a kind that handles no MOC data is an argument written for a program that will not be given it.

The data never comes that way. What a node was given arrives on an interface, and a command line carrying readings would be a node whose work was in neither the configuration nor the program.

Where it all lives: tcsapp is the one statement of the protocol and holds both ends of it -- what the program itself holds, and the starting of one with the other end of each interface. tcslibgs::node::app is what a file may say and what is wrong with what it says, which is what the editor and tcsverify read; it sits beside the two node families rather than in one of them, because the rule is one rule and the kind says which family a given app belongs to. tcspecial::node::vehicle::app_payload_veh and its four neighbours are the flight side, two of them under tcspecial::node::payload because two of the kinds are payloads. And app_payload_veh, app_moc_veh, app_all_veh, app_pl and app_all_pl are five programs of their own, built as crates and not installed: they are what the arrangement is tested with -- three of them answer what arrives, one carries each stream through to the other interface, one reports without being asked, and a test that a packet comes back out of the interface it was not going to is how the streams are held apart.

Which stream a datum is on

An app node is what made the fabric learn this, and the rule is worth stating on its own because every node type is now part of it.

Most nodes need no opinion about which stream a datum belongs to. A device has one source terminal, so where its readings go is the link's business and not its own; a computation's result is one result however many ways the configuration sends it -- a thermostat's answer goes to the heaters and to the ground, and neither of those is the stream it is "on". Those say nothing, and every link out of them is followed, which is what the fabric always did.

A node with more than one terminal at either end has to be told and has to say. An app_all_veh is the one that does: it reads payload data on the one interface and MOC data on the other, so each datum it produces belongs to one of the two, and delivering a reading to the links leaving its moc-out would send telemetry out as a command. So the stream travels with the bytes -- Taken carries it out of a node, Arriving carries it in -- and Fabric follows only the links that carry it.

Which stream a link carries is settled once, where the routing table is built: from the terminal either end names, and failing that from the only terminal the type at either end has. A payload has one source and one sink, so a link from one is groundward whether or not the word was written, which is why nearly every link in every file needs none of this. route::stream_of is where those three answers are, in that order.

Two consequences worth knowing. A delivery whose stream nothing settled takes whatever comes, and a datum on no stream in particular goes everywhere its node's links go: it is only a node that says which stream it produced on, delivering to a link that says which stream it carries, that can be told apart at all -- so every configuration that worked before works unchanged. And a tee uses it: one stream arrives at a tee, and each datum leaves on no stream in particular, so that it goes down every link the tee has, on both streams -- one copy for each link. A node that took both streams and passed both on that way would hand the ground's commands to the ground and its readings to the payloads, which is why the null node that did is gone and each of the four that replaced it takes one stream. A configuration that needs both carried through has app_all_veh, whose two interfaces are two streams.

An app node is read like a device in no group: system::is_polled includes it, so every pass asks it, and both interfaces of an app_all_veh are asked turn and turn about so that a busy stream does not starve the other. Nothing waits on it -- tcsapp's reads answer with what has arrived and keep the packet that has half arrived until the rest of it comes -- because a vehicle that waited on a program would stop reading its sensors while it waited.

The architecture

A vehicle is a graph of nodes and the links between them, and the software that runs one is in two halves that never meet: what the file becomes, which opens nothing, and what runs, which opens everything. The first half is arithmetic over the configuration and can be exercised with no vehicle present; the second is threads, descriptors and bytes.

What a configuration becomes, and what runs at once.

What the file becomes

tcslibgs::graph reads the file and checks it whole: a fault refuses the configuration rather than the node it is in, because a vehicle that started with one node missing would be a vehicle flying a configuration nobody wrote. What survives the check is turned into four tables, described under Running a graph: route says where each node's output goes and which link fills each of its named inputs, schedule says when each group is next read, telemetry gives each source its own link to the ground -- a moc node's ground_base counted on by that source's device id -- and parameters reads the tcspecial section for the beacons, the log and the two command ports. None of the four opens anything, which is what lets each be tested against a file on a workstation.

system then opens one port per node and one per ground link, and holds them in a fabric. A port that will not open stops the start and names itself: a vehicle half opened is a vehicle whose configuration nobody has run.

What runs at once

Four things run at the same time, and the division between them is what keeps any one of them from holding up the others.

the command interpreter
Its own thread, owning both command links -- the spacecraft's and the payloads' -- so that a command is read and answered while the pass is running. There are two links because what the ground needs in a hurry must never be queued behind what it asked for at leisure.
the conductor
One thread, running one pass: it asks the schedule which group is due, hands each of that group's reads to its node's thread, waits for them all, and then walks the routes, one breadth-first wave at a time, each wave's deliveries handed out the same way.
the node threads
One per node, made as the vehicle starts. Every operation of a stage -- a read, a delivery, a computation, a write to the ground -- runs on its node's thread at the same time as every other operation of that stage, and no node operation waits on another node.
the beacons
Their own thread: a beacon every beacon.interval, carrying the digest of the configuration, from beacon.send to beacon.recv.
Requirement
The beacon socket is bound and beacon.recv resolved before the beacon thread starts, and either failing stops tcspecial before it starts, naming the setting to change. Found on the thread, the failure ended the thread in silence and the vehicle flew on without beacons -- which the ground cannot tell from a vehicle that is not there. Once beacons are going, nothing ends them: a send that fails is reported once when the beacons stop going and once when they go again, so an hour without a route is two lines in the log rather than one per beacon.
Requirement
Commands are read and answered on a thread of their own, and never on the thread that runs the pass. A command interpreter that took its turn inside a pass would answer at the rate of the slowest group in the configuration, and the one command that matters most -- stop that payload -- would be the one waiting on it.
Requirement
What a command changes takes effect between stages, so that no node has its configuration altered in the middle of an operation. The exception is stopping a node, which takes effect at once: a payload the ground has disabled is a payload that must stop, not one that stops after the current read finishes.
Requirement
Every node operation of a stage may run at the same time as every other. What is ordered is the stages: the reads of a due group go out together and are all collected before any delivery begins, and delivery follows the routes breadth first, a wave at a time. That is the order the graph's meaning rests on -- readings that will be compared are taken together, and the ground has a reading before an on-board computation over it begins -- and it is the only ordering parallel execution may not take away.
Requirement
Each node has a thread of its own, made when the node is first held, as the vehicle starts. A stage hands each of its operations to the thread of the node it is about, and ends when the last of them has finished. Not a thread made for each operation, and not a pool.

A thread made for each operation was the design, and it allocates: a thread's stack, its handle and the closure it runs are all made when it is started, and nothing a pass does may allocate -- memory is allocated as the vehicle starts and not once it is running. A pool has a size, and nothing can choose it correctly. A pool smaller than the stage serialises operations the graph says may run together, which is the one ordering parallel execution may not take away -- and the figure that is too small depends on the configuration, so a number chosen here would be wrong for some file somebody writes later. A pool larger than the stage holds threads that do nothing. And a pool needs a queue between the conductor and the workers, which is a second place for a stage to stall and a place where one slow operation delays an operation it has nothing to do with.

A stage never has more operations than there are nodes, since a node is held by one thread at a time -- a group of three sensors is three reads, a wave that gives one computation three arrivals is one operation that takes all three in turn -- so a thread per node gives exactly the parallelism the file describes, bounded by the file rather than by a figure nobody chose, and nothing is made once the vehicle is running. A thread that the system will not start is said once, and leaves its node's operations to the conductor, done in turn: slower, and still the vehicle's work done.

Requirement
A node operation that panics is a failure of that node, reported against it like any other, and the stage ends as it would have. A thread that died without saying it had finished would leave the conductor waiting for it for the rest of the flight.
Requirement
A wave reads the results of the wave before it and writes its own somewhere else: each node has two banks of result buffers, and the waves take them in turn. A node whose result is on its way to its destinations can be given something itself in the same wave -- a diamond in the graph does that -- and one buffer would be written while it was read.
Requirement
A port is held by one thread at a time. A node is a thing with a descriptor, a position in a stream and a buffer, so two threads inside one port would be two readers of one socket: the fabric hands a port to a thread for an operation and has it back before the stage ends.
Requirement
A node's state -- enabled, disabled or faulted -- is its own, and is read without taking anything the pass holds across an operation. A pass that had to take a lock the command interpreter also takes would be a pass the command interpreter could stall, which is the thing the separate thread was for.

How a node is driven

Two questions decide it, and between them they make four cases. The first is about the interface: does the payload put data there by itself, or is the data there only because the vehicle went and read it? The second is about the part: does it have to be asked?

A part that originates nothing holds a value that the act of reading refreshes -- a register on a bus, a device file, a peripheral on a clocked link. Nothing arrives at the vehicle unless the vehicle reads, so a periodic timer drives the reading:

  • with no trigger, the action is the read itself, no oftener than poll_interval;
  • with a trigger, the action is the whole exchange: send the trigger, wait read_delay, read what came back. Every trigger_interval.

A part that sends puts data on the interface when it has some -- a socket, a line, a channel, a set of discrete lines. The vehicle does not decide when:

  • with no trigger, data arrives when it arrives and the vehicle takes it as it becomes readable. There is no rate to state and none to state it with;
  • with a trigger, a periodic timer drives the trigger alone, every trigger_interval, and the answer arrives when it arrives. There is no read_delay, because nothing waits: the answer is taken on the pass it becomes readable on, like any other arrival.
Requirement
A periodic timer drives the action, and the timer's period is what the configuration states. The clock runs from when an action was due and not from when it finished, so a pass that ran late does not push the next one later: a reading every three seconds stays every three seconds rather than drifting by the cost of the work. That is the rule for a group's interval and for trigger_interval, which are rates somebody chose.
Requirement
poll_interval is a floor and not a rate: the least time between issuing one read and issuing the next. It is not a cadence because the data is not on one -- nothing says how soon a descriptor will have something, so a vehicle that promised a reading every so often would be promising on the part's behalf. What the interval does is keep a descriptor with nothing on it from being read again immediately, which is a pass spent on a question already answered.
Requirement
poll_interval is for a part the vehicle must read and is not asked: a register that sits unchanged until it is read, and a device file, where there is no telling how soon anything will be there. It says nothing about a part that is asked, which is read because it was asked.
Requirement
read_delay is for a part that originates nothing and is asked. It is the time that part takes to answer, which its interface document states; a part that sends answers when it answers, and a vehicle that waited a stated time for it would be guessing at a link's latency.
Requirement
trigger_interval is for any part that is asked, whichever kind of interface it has. What the timer does differs -- an exchange for one, a write for the other -- and how often the question goes out does not.
Requirement
A part that sends and is not asked states no rate at all, and a configuration that gives it one is refused. Nothing in the vehicle decides when that data exists, so a number there would describe something nobody controls.
Requirement
Which kind of interface a node has is the node's type, and the one table that says which is which is in the library. It is not a thing for a file to state: a payload that said it originated nothing while sitting on a socket would be a file disagreeing with the interface it names.

A payload that is spoken to first

A heater, a valve, a relay: a payload the vehicle writes to and never reads. Or a sensor the vehicle asks: one whose trigger is the first word on the link. The links and the trigger say which a payload is, and for every kind of interface but the datagram sockets that changes nothing about how it is opened. A device is opened whichever way it is used, and a TCP payload is connected to.

A datagram socket is the exception, and the reason is the one that decided the ordinary case. A UDP payload that is read and not asked is reached by binding its address and waiting for it to speak, because a datagram connect sends nothing and such a payload produces data because it is running; the vehicle learns where its payload is from the first packet. A payload that is never read has no first packet, and one that is asked has none until it has been asked. So the vehicle would wait at the address for ever, and every write would be refused for want of a peer -- the vehicle deciding to switch the heaters on, or sending a trigger, and having nowhere to send it.

Requirement
A datagram payload speaks first when it is read and not asked, and every other datagram payload is spoken to first. The rule is stated once, in the library, and the vehicle, the simulator and the configuration check all ask it, so that the two ends of a link and the check of it cannot disagree about which end waits.
Requirement
For a UDP payload that is spoken to first, the ends swap roles: the vehicle sends to the address and port the node states, from a port of its own that nothing has to find, and the payload is the end that waits at the stated address. A payload that is asked answers to the vehicle's port, where its answers are read.

That is what the address already means. address and port say where the vehicle reaches the payload, and for a payload that is spoken to first the reading is the literal one.

Requirement
What arrives on that socket does not change where it sends. A stranger that speaks to the vehicle's end of a heater link does not become the heater.
Requirement
Nor is it read. A datagram from anywhere but the address the node states -- the exact host and port, or the exact path -- is turned away and counted, never handed on as the payload's reading: a reading nobody measured, voted on and acted on, is the failure nothing downstream could catch. A read turns away a bounded number before it gives up for the pass, so a flood costs a payload a pass and not the vehicle its loop. A payload that answers from another address than the one its node states -- a host with several, answering from the wrong one -- is turned away too, and the climbing count is what says to bind it to the address its node states.

A datagram payload that speaks first has no address in the file to read from: the vehicle learns where it is from what arrives, and the first sender it hears is taken for the payload. Locking onto that sender would be strict and brittle at once -- a payload that restarts from a new port would be shut out until the node was restarted -- so the file says it instead, where it can.

Requirement
A udp or unix_dgram payload that speaks first may state sends_from: a host and port over UDP, an IP address and not a name, or a path over a Unix datagram socket. With it, the payload is read from that address alone, others turned away and counted as for a payload reached at its address. Without it, the payload is learnt from what arrives, as before. It is refused on any other protocol, on any other kind of node, where it is the node's own address, and on a payload that is asked or only driven -- the first three having no use for it and the last being read only from its address already. The simulator's stand-in sends from it, and a simulator file that states payload_address or payload_port for that payload is refused, the address being said once.

A Unix datagram payload has the same shape and is reached the same way, with one difference: the vehicle's end is bound to a name the kernel chooses in the abstract namespace, where the UDP one binds a port of its own. A path for the vehicle's end would be a thing for a file to state rather than for the software to invent, and a name outside the filesystem is neither: it is unique without anyone choosing it, a payload that is asked has somewhere to answer to, and nothing of the vehicle's end is left in the filesystem to be cleared up after a vehicle that was killed.

Requirement
For a Unix datagram payload that is spoken to first, the ends swap roles as they do for UDP: the vehicle sends to the path the node states, from a socket bound to a name the kernel chooses in the abstract namespace, and the payload is the end that binds that path. The simulator's stand-in binds it, and the dead-socket rule applies to it like any other path this software binds.
Requirement
The simulator stands in for a datagram payload that is asked by waiting at the address the configuration gives and answering whoever asked. A simulator file that gives such a payload an address of its own -- payload_address or payload_port -- is refused, the address being one nothing would read.

What a payload starts in

A payload the vehicle drives is in whatever state its hardware came up in until the vehicle first decides something about it, and that may be a while: a thermostat decides when its group has been read, and a heater that came up on warms the cabin until then. A payload's init says what it starts in instead.

Requirement
init is bytes, written in the notation a trigger is -- parts one after another, each a C string in quotes or bytes in brackets -- and read by the same code, which says what is wrong with one in the words of the attribute it is in.
Requirement
The init is written as the node is opened, before anything else is written to it or read from it: as the vehicle starts, and again whenever the ground starts the node after stopping it, opening being the same work by the same function both times.
Requirement
A write of the init that fails is a node that did not open. The vehicle does not start, or the START_NODE is answered with the failure, and either way the message names the node and the init. A payload whose starting state nobody knows is what init exists to prevent, so it is not flown without one.
Requirement
init is refused, where the configuration is read, wherever nothing can be written as the node opens: a set of discrete lines that is watched rather than driven, a sensor's sample buffer, a link to the ground, and a udp or unix_dgram payload that is read and not asked. The last is the payload that speaks first -- the vehicle learns where it is from what arrives -- and as the node opens nothing has, so the write would fail every time. A datagram payload that is spoken to first, being driven or asked, is reached where it is, and takes one.
Requirement
It is not only for what is driven. A part that must have a register set before it reads sensibly states that write as its init, and is read as any other.

Holding a device at a decision

A heater is not told a reading; it is told what to be. So the thing that decides -- a thermostat -- and the thing that records what was decided want opposite treatment, and treating them alike cost this project a failure mode worth writing down.

The thermostat used to emit only when its decision changed. The argument was about the record: a node re-sending the same command fills the telemetry log with a decision nobody made and leaves a reader unable to see when the heaters actually switched. True, and it was applied to the link as well, where it does not hold. A heater's state then lived in one place -- the vehicle's last decision -- and was asserted only on a change, with nothing reading back what the heater was doing. One lost datagram stood for ever: the vehicle believing it had switched the heaters off, the mass climbing past the top of the band, and the thermostat deciding Off again, finding it had already said so, and staying silent. The decision that would correct it is the one it thinks it has already sent.

Requirement
A computation that drives a device asserts its decision on every sample. What the device is held at is asked of the computation on every arrival that produced nothing new, so a lost datagram costs one sample rather than standing until something else changes.
Requirement
A re-assertion reaches the devices and nothing else. A log or a ground link told the same decision ten times a second would bury the moment it changed, and a further computation would be made to compute again over a value that has not moved. What is recorded is the transitions, which is what the argument above was really asking for.
Requirement
A re-assertion that is not sent to a node is not counted as anything. It is not a datum dropped -- it was never for that node -- and counting it as loss would make every counter on a log climb for ever.
Requirement
A computation that produces measurements asserts nothing. A conversion and a vote answer that there is nothing to hold a device at, because a reading nobody took is not a reading, where a decision nobody has changed is still the decision.
Requirement
A thermostat that has decided nothing holds nothing. Before the first reading, and for a reading in the band before any decision, nothing is sent at all: asserting Off because the vehicle had not yet looked would be a decision nobody made.

How often "every sample" is depends on what feeds it. The decision is asserted once for every temperature that arrives, and a vote over a trio of sensors produces one on every reading -- so a trio read ten times a second is thirty assertions a second to each heater. That follows from the vote, not from the thermostat, and it is the rate at which a lost decision is corrected.

What it is not is a substitute for reading the device back. Nothing here knows what a heater is doing; it knows what it was last told. A payload that can be read as well as driven would say more, and the discrete lines of the shipped example can be: that is worth having and is not what this is.

What each command means

Ten commands. Which link each belongs on is CommandType::link, the one table both ends read; what follows is what a vehicle made of nodes does with each.

PING
Answers. It says the interpreter is reading its socket, which is a different fact from the beacon's -- a beacon says the vehicle is alive, and a PING says this link reaches it.
CONNECT
Answers with this build's version and the digest of the configuration being flown. Asked as the link comes up, and what licenses every command that carries a node id: see How a command names a node.
RESTART_ARM and RESTART
The process is what restarts: a vehicle that comes back reads the configuration file again, so a RESTART is how a changed configuration is flown. The arm key is the interlock, and an unarmed RESTART is refused. See Restarting.
CONFIG
Sets the beacon interval. The beacon thread is retimed where it waits, so the next beacon goes at the new interval rather than after the old one elapses.
START_NODE and STOP_NODE
Enable and disable a node. The unit is a node of the graph, so a computation, a log and a payload are each something the ground can stop.
QUERY_NODE
What that node has moved, from the node's own counters, and whether it is running. Both, because they are read together: counts that have stopped going up mean one thing for a node the ground disabled and another for one that is running, and an operator who had to ask twice to tell those apart would be asking twice every time.
QUERY_NODE_SAMPLE
The last datum that node moved each way, for an operator who wants to see the bytes rather than a count of them.
SET_NODE

Sets an attribute of a node. A graph's commands section says which attribute of which node the ground may set, so this is the command that section was written for -- until there was one, the bindings were checked when the file was read and then applied by nothing.

Which node, by its id; which attribute, by the name the section spells; and the value, as bytes. The attribute is text where the node is a number because a node has a position in the configuration and an attribute has no such number of its own -- a binding's position in the commands section would be one, and would carry the node with it -- and a command that says setpoint is one a log reads back.

Each has a largest size: an attribute of 64 bytes, ATTRIBUTE_BYTES, and a value of 256, VALUE_BYTES, both in tcslibgs::commands. Fixed, so that the vehicle reads a SET_NODE into room it holds and allocates nothing to take one.

Requirement
Any node may be disabled, whatever its kind, but a ping node. A graph does not divide into payloads and the rest -- a computation that must stop and a sensor that must stop are the same request -- and a rule that admitted only some kinds would be a rule an operator has to remember in the middle of a fault. The one exception is the node the ground checks the vehicle with, which is refused both commands: see The ping node.
Requirement
Enabling a node opens what it needs again, and a node that cannot be opened is answered with the failure and left disabled rather than left in a state of its own. Disabling released the endpoint -- see Disabling a node that is waiting -- so enabling is the same work as starting it at startup, and it can fail the same ways: a device that is gone, an address already taken, a program that will not execute.
Requirement
Enabling a node that is enabled, and disabling one that is disabled, answer success. A command that arrives twice, or whose answer was lost, is the ordinary case on a link with latency, and an operator who presses Disable twice has asked for a state, not an action.
Requirement
A node comes up enabled. Every node of a checked configuration is opened when the vehicle starts and runs until it is stopped, because what a graph describes is a system that works as a whole: telemetry reaches the ground with no ground station having said anything, which is what a vehicle out of contact has to do. Coming up idle and being told which nodes to start would suit a set of payloads that are separately useful, and does not suit a graph whose links describe one machine.
Requirement
A START_NODE carries no kind. A kind in the message, refused on a mismatch, would be a check against the two ends reading different configurations; the node id and the digest make that check beforehand and for every command, so it would be a second answer to a settled question.
Requirement
Both carry one thing, the node id, and both are answered with the status and that same id. One body for the two: there is nothing to say about a node beyond which node it is.
Requirement
Every node counts what it has moved: bytes taken and bytes given, the operations that produced them, the failures, and when it last moved anything. They are the node's own, and they survive a disable -- a node that was stopped and started again has moved what it moved before, and an operator asking what a payload has done wants the answer for the flight and not for the latest spell of it.
Requirement
A read of nought bytes is counted as neither a read nor a failure. It is the ordinary answer from a device that is not due, and counting it would make a silent payload the busiest thing in the telemetry.
Requirement
Triggers are counted apart from the deliveries, and the triggers that would not go apart from the ones that did. A trigger is a write with no read behind it, so without a count of its own it would appear in none at all -- and a payload that has stopped answering, one that has gone away, and a vehicle that has stopped asking are three states that one count could not tell apart.
Requirement
Datagrams a node's socket turned away as a stranger's are counted, apart from everything else, as strangers. Counted and not logged: each failure is a line in the log, and a sender flooding a port would be a log filling and a message made in the loop for each datagram, where a count is one number climbing. A count that moves at all is something on the vehicle's network talking to a node it has no business with.
Requirement
Deliveries dropped because a node is disabled are counted apart from the ones it refused. They are different news: a refused delivery is a node that would not take what it was given, and a dropped one is the configuration working as the ground asked.
Requirement
The counts are read without the pass stopping to be read. They are atomics the pass writes as it moves data and the command interpreter reads from its own thread, so a query is answered while a pass is running and neither waits for the other. A query that had to take something the pass holds would be a query that could stall the vehicle.
Requirement
A node's samples are kept best effort. The path that moves payload data does not wait for a reader of the samples to finish: a lost sample costs one stale line on a display, where a blocked pass costs data.
Requirement
A node that has moved nothing has no time, which is not the same as one that moved something long ago. The first has never spoken and the second has gone quiet, and a display that showed them alike would hide the difference an operator is looking for.
Requirement
What a pass reports and what a node counts are different things. Moved says what one pass did, which is how the loop reports itself; a node's counters say what that node has done since the vehicle started. A QUERY_NODE answered from the pass's figures would answer about the last twenty milliseconds.
Requirement
A SET_NODE is refused unless the configuration binds that attribute of that node to the ground. The commands section is what says a setpoint may be set from the ground, and a vehicle that took any attribute from a command would have no checked configuration at all: a bus address or a set of line numbers is what the file said.
Requirement
A command binding whose attribute is longer than ATTRIBUTE_BYTES is refused where the configuration is read, since the ground could never name it. A SET_NODE whose attribute or value is longer than its largest is refused on the ground and not sent; one that arrives anyway is refused by the vehicle as it is read, rather than cut short.
Requirement
An attribute no binding mentions and a value the node will not take are both InvalidParameter. From the ground's side they are the same thing -- the configuration does not allow what was asked -- and what tells the two apart is the reason, which the answer carries as well as the vehicle's log: an operator told only the status would have to read a log aboard to find out which word to change.
Requirement
The answer to a SET_NODE carries why it was refused, as text of at most REASON_BYTES, 160, in tcslibgs::telemetry: the attribute no binding names, with its spelling where that was what was wrong; what the node would not take; the id no node has. A longer reason is cut at a character and ends ..., the whole of it being in the log. Held in room of that size, so that the vehicle makes an answer without allocating, as it reads a command. Empty for a SET_NODE that was done, and read as empty from a vehicle that sends none.
Requirement
Nothing is half applied. The value is read and checked before anything changes, so a command that is refused leaves the node running on what it had: a thermostat that took half of a setpoint would be a thermostat holding a temperature nobody chose.
Requirement
A SET_NODE is applied between stages, like every other change a command makes. A node whose setpoint moved in the middle of a computation would be a node doing half of one thing and half of another, and the answer waits for it, so a success means the node is running on the new value.
Requirement
A command about a disabled node is answered rather than ignored. A QUERY_NODE of a disabled node answers with its counters and says it is disabled; a SET_NODE of one is applied if the attribute is bound, since what it sets is the node's configuration rather than its traffic -- so a payload the ground stopped can be reconfigured before it is started again.

How a command names a node

By its node id: the number the node has from the order the configuration declares it, counted across every node whatever its kind. It is not a number invented for commands -- it is the one a node's link to the ground is already counted by, a moc node's ground_base plus that node's id -- so the id in a command and the port its telemetry arrives on are the same number, worked out in one place.

Requirement
A command about a node names it by its node id, in a field of fixed length. A name is text of no particular length, and every message carrying one is a message whose construction and parsing have a length to get right; a number is a field like the sequence and the type beside it.
Requirement
Node ids are given by one algorithm, in one place, from the order the configuration is read in. Two components that compute them separately would be two tables that can drift apart, and the drift would show as commands about the wrong node rather than as an error.
Requirement
What makes the two ends' ids the same is the digest. It is computed over the configuration including the order the nodes appear in -- see What Each End Read -- so two ends whose digests agree have read the same nodes in the same order and cannot disagree about which node an id names. Agreeing about the digest is what CONNECT establishes, and it is asked on the spacecraft's link before any node command is sent.
Requirement
A ground station whose digest does not match the vehicle's does not send commands about nodes. The id it would send means a node of the configuration it read, which is not the one being flown, and a command acted on under that misunderstanding is a command that starts or stops something nobody chose.
Requirement
An id the configuration does not have is refused, saying which id and how many nodes there are. A fixed-length field holds any number, so a command carrying one is a thing to answer rather than a thing to parse: the range check is the whole of what the vehicle can say about it.
Requirement
An answer carries the id the command carried, so a response can be matched to its command without the name. The ground shows the name, which it has from the configuration it read -- the same file the id is counted from -- so what an operator sees is a name and what the link carries is a number.

How many links, and what the thread does with one

Requirement
Two links: one for the spacecraft and one for commands about nodes. What the ground needs in a hurry must never be queued behind what it asked for at leisure, and a RESTART waiting behind a node's statistics is a vehicle that cannot be rescued while it is busy.
Requirement
One link for every node, and not one per node. A socket per node would do two things: say which node a command was about, and keep a command that takes time from delaying a command about another node. The node id does the first, and the thread a node operation runs on does the second. A graph also has many more nodes than payloads -- a thermostat of four sensors is eleven nodes -- so a socket apiece would spend ports on computations and logs that nothing addresses from the ground.
Requirement
That link is bound at payload_command.dest_base with nothing added to it, and commands on it are taken from payload_command.source_base. The base itself, with nothing counted off it: the node id is in the message.
Requirement
Both links are waited on together, with the descriptor that stops the interpreter in the same poll set. The thread therefore wakes when there is a command or a shutdown and for nothing else. A thread per link with a read timeout would be a wakeup several times a second per link to ask a question whose answer is almost always no.
Requirement
A command this vehicle does not implement is refused and not ignored. It is something the ground can see and work around, where a command that goes quiet is a link the ground cannot tell from a dead one. A refusal carries the telemetry type the command asked for, so the ground can pair it with what it sent.
Requirement
The command interpreter does not do a node's work on its thread. It hands the work over and answers when the work is done, so that a START_NODE opening a device does not stop the next command being read.
Requirement
A pass waiting for the next group to come due is woken when work is handed to it. The wait between passes may be seconds, and a command answered when the next group happened to be read would be a ground station left wondering for most of an interval whether its command arrived.
Requirement
A STOP_NODE is answered after the release, not before it. Stopping the node happens first and at once, so it stops whatever the pass is doing; the answer waits for the release, because a ground station that stopped a node to free a device is about to give that device to something else. An answer carries the sequence of the command it answers, so answers that overtake one another are still matched to what was asked.
Requirement
An answer goes back to the address the command came from: a datagram's sender is the only statement of where the ground is, and a configuration that stated it as well would be a second answer that can disagree.
Requirement
A command from anywhere but the address the configuration states is ignored. A command is a command to restart a vehicle or stop a payload; taking one from whoever sent it would be taking the ground's word from a stranger.

That two ends address the same node by the same id is checked before any command and for every command: the ids two ends use are the same ids exactly when their digests agree, and CONNECT is what establishes that.

The telemetry log

One log for everything the vehicle sends to the ground, where the tcspecial section's log.directory says and in segments of log.segment_size. The beacons record into it, and so do the command interpreter's answers. Each segment it fills is handed to segsend: see Filled segments.

Requirement
A log.directory that is not there, or is not a directory, stops tcspecial before it starts, naming the directory and saying to create it or to change the setting. tcspecial makes no directory of its own: one named wrongly would be a log kept where nobody looks.
Requirement
Every sender records into one log. Beacons go from a thread of their own and answers from the command interpreter's, and a log only one of them could reach would hold part of what went to the ground -- which is the one thing a telemetry log exists to make reconstructable. The handle is shared and a handle on no log records nothing, so no sender has to ask whether logging is on.
Requirement
The log is opened before anything records into it, and a directory that is not there stops the start. A vehicle that came up and discovered at the first beacon that it had nowhere to write would have lost the beacons it sent in between.
Requirement
What is recorded is flushed when the vehicle stops. The log is the account of what was sent, and an account missing its last page is one nobody can rely on.
Requirement
A log node is not this and this is not a log node. A log node records what is delivered to it, and the configuration's links decide what that is; this records what leaves the vehicle on the links no graph describes -- beacons go to beacon.recv and answers to whoever asked, and neither travels a link a node could be drawn into. The two use one library, tcslog, which is why a log node was a generalisation of something the software already did rather than a mechanism of its own.

Filled segments

A segment that has filled is no longer the log's to keep, and a long flight that kept them all would fill the directory it was given. Each one is handed, by name, to a program called segsend, which takes it from there: downlinks it, compresses it, or -- the one built with tcspecial, which is for testing -- deletes it. What a mission does with its segments is the mission's own business, and a program of its own is where that can be written without changing the flight software.

Requirement
Every segment a log fills is handed to segsend, the telemetry log's and every log node's alike, with the segment's file name as its one argument. So is every segment a log finds in its directory as it opens, which is what an earlier run was writing when it stopped.
Requirement
segsend is the program of that name beside tcspecial's own executable, where a build puts both and where tcsmoc finds tcspecial. A configuration that keeps a log -- a log.directory, or a log node -- does not start without one there that this user may run, and is told where it was looked for. A vehicle that found out at its first full segment that it had nothing to hand it to would fill its log directory without a word. A configuration that keeps no log does not need one.
Requirement
A segment leaves the log's name before it is handed over: .segsend is put on the end of it, and it is that name segsend is given. tcslog requires that a filled segment leave its naming pattern, and a rename within a directory is cheap enough to do while the log is held. A segment renamed and not yet dealt with -- tcspecial stopped first, or segsend fell behind -- is found by its suffix at the next start and handed over then, before anything that fills after it.
Requirement
segsend is run on one thread of its own, one segment at a time, in the order they filled, and waited for. Neither the pass nor the command interpreter waits for it. One at a time because the segments of every log go down one link, and two at once would be racing for it.
Requirement
Handing a segment over allocates nothing. Its name is copied into a buffer of fixed size, the queue to the thread has its room made at the start, and segsend is started with posix_spawn from those buffers. A queue that is full leaves the segment renamed in the directory, said once until the queue has room again, for the next start to hand over.
Requirement
A segsend that ends with a status other than nought is reported, with the segment it was given. What it did with the segment is its own to say; tcspecial does not hand it over again.

Disabling a node that is waiting

Nothing in a pass waits for data to arrive. A descriptor is asked whether it is ready and a node with nothing to say reads as nought bytes, so a device that is silent costs a poll and not a pass -- see EndpointPort::ready_to_read. What remains are the waits that are part of an operation rather than a wait for one: a process computation waits for its program's answer up to the deadline the configuration gives it, and a write to a part that is not ready can wait on the part.

Requirement
Every wait a port makes has that node's wakeup descriptor in its poll set. Disabling a node writes to that descriptor, so the wait returns at once, the operation is abandoned and the node stops where it is. A disable that waited out a deadline would be a disable that arrives up to a timeout late, which on a part that has stopped answering is exactly when it is wanted.
Requirement
An operation abandoned that way produces nothing and reports nothing. The node has been told to stop, so what is on its interface is the answer to a question that is no longer being asked, and a stop the ground asked for is not a fault to be counted.
Requirement
The flag goes down before the descriptor is written. A wait woken before the state said disabled would look up, find the node enabled and wait again, and the disable would arrive a timeout later after all.
Requirement
Enabling a node clears whatever a previous disable left in its descriptor. Otherwise the first wait the node makes after being started would return at once, and every wait after it, on the strength of a disable that has already been answered.
Requirement
A disabled node releases what it held: the endpoint is closed and the device, bus address or socket is free. A node held open but idle looks from outside like a node that is running, and the port it keeps is a port nothing else may take.
Requirement
A delivery to a disabled node is dropped and counted, not queued. A queue for a node nobody has re-enabled is a queue that grows without bound, and a reading delivered minutes later is a reading that describes a moment that has passed.

The mechanism is a descriptor in the poll set beside the endpoint's: stopping a node writes one byte to it, so a thread waiting on a silent payload returns at once and is joined. There is one per node, so stopping one node wakes that node's wait and no other.

Note

What runs today. One thread makes the whole pass, in order, with the beacons beside it on a thread of their own and the command interpreter on a third. It never blocks waiting for data -- that part of the design is already true -- and the only wait inside a pass is a process computation's deadline.

The command interpreter is there: two links, each taking commands only from the address the configuration states, answering PING, CONNECT, CONFIG and the five NODE commands, and recording every answer in the log beside the beacons. Each node has a state and a wakeup descriptor, and stopping a node takes effect at once -- it is not read, not asked, and deliveries to it are dropped and counted.

A stopped node releases what it held: the port is dropped, which closes the device, frees the bus address or gives up the socket, and starting it again opens it with the same function that opened it at startup -- so it can fail the same ways, and a node that will not open is answered with the failure and left stopped. That work is the pass's and not the command interpreter's: the interpreter hands it over, the pass does it between stages, and the answer goes out when it is done, so a success means the device really is free.

Every wait that is part of an operation has the node's wakeup descriptor in it: the delay between a trigger and the read that answers it, and a process computation's deadline. Disabling the node ends either one where it stands.

A stage's node operations run at once, each on its node's own thread: the reads of a due group together, then each wave of deliveries together.

SET_NODE is answered too, so the commands section finally does something: a setpoint set from the ground reaches the node between stages, and the next pass decides by it.

Every node counts what it has moved, and QUERY_NODE and QUERY_NODE_SAMPLE answer with those counts and with the last datum each way. They are answered on the interpreter's own thread and hand nothing over: the counts are atomics and the samples are taken only if the lock is free.

RESTART_ARM and RESTART are answered: see Restarting.

Restarting

A RESTART runs tcspecial again, in the process it is already running in.

Requirement
A RESTART_ARM is taken, with its key, and answered Success. A second arm replaces the first, so the arm that counts is the one the operator gave last.
Requirement
A RESTART is taken only with the key of an arm taken within restart_arm_valid -- twenty minutes where the configuration does not say -- and uses the arm up: one lost or repeated datagram must not be able to restart the flight software. Anything else is answered NotArmed. A key that does not match leaves the arm standing, so an operator who mistyped it sends the RESTART again without arming again.
Requirement
A RESTART that is taken is answered before anything stops, so the ground hears that it was taken before the links close under it. The vehicle then stops as it does on a signal -- the pass finished, the command links closed, the telemetry log flushed, every node released -- because a restart that left a log part way through a record would lose what the restart was meant to keep going.
Requirement
Then every descriptor tcspecial opened is closed, and it execs its own executable with the arguments it was started with, keeping the descriptors it was started with and only those: the standard three and whatever a supervisor handed it. Which those were is asked as the process starts, before anything is opened, from /proc/self/fd or /dev/fd. The process id stays the same, so a supervisor sees one process throughout, and the beacons start again at #1, which is how the ground tells a restart from a gap.
Requirement
Where that cannot be done -- neither directory could be read as the process started, or the exec fails -- tcspecial says why on its error output and exits. A restart that cannot keep what it was handed is not one, and a process that carried on after being told to restart would be flying something the ground believes has gone.

Running a graph

Four pieces, and the first three open nothing. That is on purpose: they are the arithmetic of the graph, they can be tested against a configuration with no vehicle present, and a fault in any of them would otherwise look like a fault in a device.

routing
The links, turned into a table once at startup: for each node, where its output goes, and which link fills each of its named inputs. A link is written the way a person wants to write one -- from a node to one or more destinations -- and that is not the shape running software wants, which is "this node has produced bytes, where do they go". Answering that by walking every link would be a scan per datum.
schedule
When each group is next read. A group's clock runs from the time a read was due and not from the time it happened, so a pass that ran late does not push the next one later -- a reading every three seconds stays every three seconds rather than drifting by the cost of the work.
fabric
What holds the nodes and moves the bytes, using the other two. Delivery is attempted per destination and the failures are counted rather than raised: a log that cannot be written must not stop the ground getting its telemetry, and a heater that will not take a command must not stop the next reading being taken. What must never happen is a failure nobody hears about, so every one is counted and named.
ports
The piece that does open something: an endpoint, presented as a node the fabric can move bytes to and from. One type for every interface rather than one per interface -- every endpoint already answers the same two traits, and a port per type would be nine copies of one delegation with nine places to drift apart. A computation is a port of its own, holding what a function produced until the fabric takes it.
Requirement
A delivery that produces a result carries on from there, in the same pass. Three sensors read on one interval must become three temperatures, an average, a decision and a command to the heaters before the pass is over; a fabric that stopped at the first delivery would leave a result sitting in a computation until something else happened to read it, and a control loop whose steps happen on different passes is a control loop whose delay depends on the shape of the graph.
Requirement
A node is asked whether it has a result rather than simply read after a delivery. The two are different things: a sensor has a reading when its group is due and a delivery does not change that, while a computation has one because something arrived. Reading every destination instead would cost a device read per delivery, and would take a command off a MOC link and pass it on as though it were a result.
Requirement
Delivery is breadth first. The three destinations of a "T" are all reached before any of their own destinations are, so the ground has a reading before an on-board computation over it begins -- the order an operator watching telemetry expects, and the order that loses least when a later step fails.
Requirement
A result is passed on at most as far as there are nodes, and a cascade that goes further is stopped and reported as a cycle. A path through a graph with no cycle visits each node at most once. The links are checked for cycles before any of this runs, so this is the backstop for a check that was wrong rather than the rule itself -- and what it must not do is loop: a fabric going round a cycle is a vehicle that has stopped reading its sensors, with no telemetry to say why.
Requirement
A computation that produces nothing produces no deliveries, and that is not a failure. A function waiting for the rest of its inputs and one that decided nothing needs to change give the same answer, and a relay commanded to the state it is already in every three seconds is a relay nobody could tell had stopped answering.
Requirement
A result too large for the buffer it is asked for is reported and kept, not cut to fit. Half of a number is a different number, and keeping it leaves it there for a caller with room.
Requirement
What a compute node may say is checked by the same code in both places, and it lives in the shared library rather than in the flight software. tcsverify reads a configuration on a desk and cannot check what it cannot see: these rules used to sit beside the arithmetic, inside tcspecial, so a thermostat with no band or a program with no deadline passed the ground check in silence and was refused when the vehicle started. That is the wrong end of the trip. The arithmetic stays where it was; what moved is the reading of attributes.
Requirement
A command from the ground has to get past two gates, in this order. The configuration must have a binding saying that this attribute of this node may be set -- an attribute no binding mentions cannot be set, which is what makes what the ground can change in flight something written down and checkable rather than whatever a computation happens to accept. The node must then accept the value, which is where a setpoint that is not a number, or a band of nought, is refused.
Requirement
A device accepts no settings. A bus address or a set of line numbers is what the configuration said, and a vehicle whose hardware could be re-described from the ground in flight would have no checked configuration at all. What the ground changes is a parameter of a computation.
Requirement
A refused command changes nothing. The value is read and checked before anything is written, so a thermostat whose new setpoint was rejected goes on running against the one it had rather than against half of a number.
Requirement
A command does not act on its own. Setting a setpoint does not recompute the decision; the next reading does, one interval away at most. Acting immediately would switch the heaters on the strength of a number rather than of a temperature.
Requirement
One pass is: every group that is due, read in full and in order; then the nodes in no group, polled; then the logs flushed. A group is read in full before anything else happens because that is what a group is for, and each reading is delivered as it is taken -- so a converted reading reaches the ground, the mean and the log before the next sensor is read, and the decision reaches the heaters inside the pass that read the sensors.
Requirement
A pass asks a descriptor whether there is anything before reading it, with no waiting at all, and a node with nothing to say is a read of no bytes. Some endpoints are opened blocking -- a device file is opened as a file -- so a read of one with nothing in it does not return, and a pass that blocked there would never end: the group that was due unfinished, every other group starved, and a stop unnoticed, a stop being noticed between passes.
Requirement
A node is polled only if it is in no group, its output goes somewhere, and it produces by the clock rather than by arrival. Each of those rules out a read whose result would be discarded. The one that matters is a MOC link: what arrives on one is a command, which sets an attribute rather than travelling a link, so polling it would be taking commands off the socket and throwing them away. A ground link that does feed the graph says so with a link out of it, and is polled like anything else.
Requirement
A vehicle starts only if every node opens, and the complaint lists every reason rather than the first. The configuration was checked on the ground, so a failure here is the hardware. Carrying on without one node sounds kinder and is not: a thermostat whose third sensor did not open would vote over two live readings and one that never arrives, which is a trio that has already spent the redundancy it exists for -- the next sensor to fail takes the decision with it -- and nothing in the telemetry would say that is why. An operator who wants to fly without a sensor changes the configuration, which is checkable, visible in the diagram and recorded in the beacon's digest.
Requirement
The checks are run again before anything is opened, by the same code the ground tool runs. A configuration that reached a vehicle unchecked is the one case the ground tool cannot help with, and a bus opened on the strength of a file nobody checked is worse than a vehicle that will not start.
Requirement
A stop takes effect between passes. A signal sets a flag and returns; the loop notices within one pause, finishes the pass it is making and flushes its logs. Stopping inside a pass would leave a log part way through a record and a heater in a state nothing decided. Both an interrupt and a termination are answered, those being the two ways a vehicle is stopped: a person at a console and a service manager.
Requirement
A pass that delivered everything reports nothing. A vehicle reading three sensors every three seconds would otherwise fill its log with the news that it is working, and the refusals -- which are what somebody is looking for -- would be the hardest lines to find in it.
Requirement
A command binding counts as a connection at both of its ends. A MOC link exists to carry commands as much as telemetry, and a computation whose setpoint is the only thing the ground sets is reached by its binding and by nothing else -- so a configuration judged by its links alone would call both of them unconnected and refuse something correct.
Requirement
A node is opened for the direction the configuration uses it in, and the other direction is refused. The links say which: a node whose output goes somewhere is read, a node things are delivered to is written, and a node that is both is both. The refusal names which way the configuration has it, because the fault is in the file and not in the attempt.
Requirement
A MOC link that a command binding arrives on is read as well as written, though no link leaves it. A command sets an attribute rather than travelling a link, so the commands section is the only statement that anything arrives from the ground -- and a ground link judged by its links alone would be opened as something only written, with a setpoint that never arrives and nothing saying so.
Requirement
A node no link touches is refused. A device opened and never read holds its lines or claims its bus address and says nothing; the usual cause is a node whose links were deleted and whose declaration was left behind. The configuration checks say so on the ground, and the port says so again aboard.
Requirement
A datum is delivered whole or refused. A write that moved some of the bytes is reported, naming how many moved, and a write that moved none of them is a failure and not a success of zero bytes. A reading cut in half is not a smaller reading, it is a different number, and the ground cannot tell a truncated record from a short one.
Requirement
Nothing to read is not a failure. A read of no bytes is the ordinary answer from a device that is not due, which on most passes is most devices. A port that reported those would bury the real faults among them.
Requirement
Both halves of an endpoint are kept whichever direction the graph uses. They are not independent -- a UDP writer learns where the far end is from what its reader received -- and a device that is only read today is one a command binding reaches tomorrow, which should be a configuration change and not a code change.
Requirement
A link that names an input of a device is refused rather than delivered anyway. Only a computation has named inputs; a device has one way in and does not name it. Delivering it would make a file that says something it does not mean work by accident. A terminal is not one of those -- see Terminals: where a link is attached -- and never reaches a port: a device's moc-in is its one way in, spelled, and is resolved where the routing table is built.
Requirement
A device that must be asked before it answers is asked after it is read, not before. Asked and read in the same breath, a device has had no time to answer, so what comes back is either nothing or the answer to the previous question arriving by luck -- and a control loop cannot tell those apart. A pass takes what the last question produced and then asks again, which costs the first pass after a start and makes every later reading one of a known age: one interval.
Requirement
A question that could not be asked is reported even when the reading succeeded. The reading in hand is the last one that node will produce, so a pass that took one and could not ask for the next has not succeeded.

Configuration

One logical configuration, which may be written as several files.

The top file may carry an include list naming others; their contents are merged into it. Nothing is discovered by scanning a directory: what is read is what a file names, so the set of files that make up a configuration is itself written down. The digest the beacon carries is taken over the whole set, so the ground can say which configuration is flying rather than which top file is.

A small vehicle needs no split and should not have one. Where a split earns its place, the useful seam is who owns a file and how often it changes, not what type of data is in it:

platform.yaml   what hardware exists and how it is reached -- bus,
                address, chip, lines, paths. Changes when the vehicle does.
graph.yaml      nodes, groups, links, command bindings. Changes when the
                control strategy does.
tuning.yaml     setpoints, intervals, thresholds: the values the MOC may
                also change in flight.

What is checked before anything runs

The reliability argument for the whole design is that a graph is checkable, and tcsverify performs these checks on the ground against the same code that performs them again aboard. One command, on one file:

$ tcsverify tests/manual/thermostat.yaml
$ make verify TCSGUI_FILE=tests/manual/thermostat.yaml

The editor reads what it writes. A configuration opened in tcsgui comes up on the canvas -- nodes where their editor positions put them, links between them, groups and command bindings held and written back -- and the comments come with it: a comment is written as a comment rather than as a field, so the editor reads the run of comment lines above an entry as that entry's.

An entry is a node, a link, a group or a command binding, and that is the limit of it. Four kinds of comment in a hand-written file are not kept, and all four are lost the first time the editor writes the file -- which the Save button does on demand, whether or not anything was edited:

  • prose attached to nothing: a heading at the top of a file, or a paragraph after the last entry, belongs to no entry.
