  • a comment above the tcspecial section, which is not an entry either.
  • a comment above one attribute of an entry rather than above the entry itself -- the line explaining why a sensor is at 0x49 is kept, and the same line written directly above the address attribute is not.

A comment above a link is kept, and a link is now an attribute -- so this is the one attribute whose comment survives a save: the run of comment lines above a payload-out: or moc-out: is read as a note about the links that attribute makes. Where the attribute names several destinations the note is about all of them, one line carrying one comment.

A comment above a payload-in: or moc-in: is not kept, those being a record of links rather than links: the note belongs above the link, at the end it is written at, and a comment the editor kept in a derived line would be a comment it moved. It is the fourth kind in the list above.

The attribute order is not kept either: the editor writes a node's attributes and the section's parameters in the order a dictionary gives them, which is alphabetical, where a hand-written file usually groups them by what they mean. So a hand-written configuration opened and saved stays correct and stops looking hand-written. For the shipped examples that is what git diff is for; for a working configuration it is worth knowing before the first Save.

The canvas is divided in three, and each part says in its top left corner what it is for. Left to right: the payload working pane, labelled Payloads, which holds everything the vehicle carries: a device on a bus, a line it drives, a link to something aboard; the vehicle working pane, labelled Vehicle, which holds the vehicle's own graph -- the computations, the log, a page of its own; and the moc working pane, labelled MOC, which holds the ground link. It is the order the examples' diagrams in the manual are drawn in, so a diagram there and the same configuration in the editor read alike.

The labels are light grey and italic, which is this window's way of saying "not part of the drawing": one in the colour and the face of a node would read as something placed. They are drawn under the nodes, so a node may cover one. A label is there to be found when a pane is empty and when somebody has lost their bearings, and a pane that reserved a row for its own name would be a pane with a row nothing can be put in.

There is a selection pane for each, one above another in the same order the working panes are in -- payloads at the top, then the vehicle's, then the MOC's -- and which one a shape was pressed in is what decides where it lands. A node stays in its pane: a drag that would carry a payload rightwards across a divider stops at it.

Each of the three lists is as tall as its own share of the column, with a line between each pair that can be dragged to change the two it is between -- the same arrangement as the working panes, turned on its side, and kept in the same settings file as percentages. A line moves the boundary between its own two neighbours and leaves the third alone, which is what a person dragging it expects: the list above grows by what the list below gives up. The upper line is between the payload list and the vehicle's, the lower one between the vehicle's and the MOC's. The arithmetic is tcsgui::list_shares_after, in Rust where it can be tested, and the markup reports a drag as how far it went and how tall the column is -- what a share is depends on what it is a share of.

No list may be squeezed below a twentieth of the column, so a line dragged to the top or the bottom stops a twentieth short of it. It is the rule the working panes already have and the same constant: a list with nothing showing is a list whose entries cannot be reached, and a line dragged to the edge is easy to do by accident and hard to understand afterwards.

And every list shows a node when the window opens, which a share of a column cannot be trusted to do by itself: a twentieth of an 820-pixel window is forty-one pixels, enough for the list's own heading and nothing under it. So the three default shares -- 20, 35 and 45 -- are checked against what the three lists actually have to draw at the height the window opens at, and the test reads both the window's preferred height and the list's own padding out of main.slint rather than restating them: a rule about what fits is a rule about numbers in two files, and a test that copied them would pass while the window showed nothing.

What that buys is a drawing that can be compared with last mission's. The three parts change on different schedules -- the ground is the other end of the link and not the vehicle at all, the vehicle's own graph persists, and what it carries changes between flights -- so a diagram that puts each in its own column shows at a glance what is different. Links cross the dividers freely -- that is what a payload is for -- and only positions are confined.

Each divider is drawn, and each can be moved: it carries a handle halfway down it, and dragging that handle moves the line and resizes the two panes either side of it. No pane may be squeezed below a twentieth of the working pane, so none can be dragged shut -- a pane with nothing visible in it is a pane whose nodes cannot be reached, and the drag that does it is one press away. With three panes that means a divider stops a twentieth short of its neighbour as well as of the edge. A divider also stops against a node rather than passing over it: against the right edge of the rightmost node of the pane on its left and the left edge of the leftmost node of the pane on its right. The alternative was to let it go where it liked and shunt the nodes it passed, which is a gesture that quietly rearranges a diagram -- and one that would have to carry every node it moved through the history to be undoable. A line that stops where it would orphan something says what it is doing while it does it. A node already on the wrong side holds nothing, so one such node cannot freeze the control.

Where the dividers sit is kept in $HOME/.tcsgui/preferences.xml, as the percentages of the working pane the moc and vehicle working panes take, the payload pane taking what is left. Each is kept by the pane it is about rather than by its place on the screen, so a file written when the moc pane was the leftmost still means the same widths now that it is the rightmost. The file is read when the editor starts and is what places the dividers; it is written whenever a line is moved. The directory is made automatically, and a preferences file that is missing, unreadable or hand-edited into nonsense leaves the defaults in place and says so on the error output: an editor that refused to start because it could not remember a pane width would be an editor traded for a preference. A file written before there was a moc working pane states only the vehicle's share, and the new pane's share is taken out of that one rather than out of the payload column somebody had arranged. The file was called config.xml in one build and is still read under that name when the current one is absent, so the rename forgets nothing; the old file is left for its owner to delete.

That file and not the configuration, which is the distinction worth stating. A node's position is in the configuration because it is part of the diagram -- two people reading one configuration should see one drawing -- in a tcsgui section at the end of the file rather than on the node it is about:

tcsgui:
  nodes:
    - { name: payload, x: 40, y: 160 }
    - { name: moc, x: 784, y: 40 }

Last, and in a section of its own, because it is a different kind of thing. The sections above say what the vehicle carries and what it is told; this says where somebody put the boxes on a screen, which the flight software ignores entirely. So a reader meets the vehicle first and can stop reading when the vehicle stops, and a node entry is about the vehicle and nothing else. A node's position written on the node is refused where the file is read, saying where it has gone.

How the canvas is divided is a different question again, and the answer is the other way about: it is not part of the diagram at all: it is a working preference of the person at the keyboard, and writing it into the configuration would make resizing a pane a change to a file that flies. A percentage rather than pixels for the same reason: a percentage is the thing the person chose, where a pixel count is how this build happens to lay a canvas out.

Within a session the divider is one position in the document's own coordinates, shared by every window, and not three quarters of whatever window it is drawn in. Which pane a node is in says what it is allowed to be, so a divider that moved with the window would make a payload into a vehicle node by being looked at on a smaller screen.

Reading a configuration puts every node in the pane its type belongs to. A payload the file draws in the vehicle working pane is a drawing that contradicts the rule the editor enforces everywhere else, and a reader has to be told which of the two to believe; the commonest source of one is a file written before the canvas was divided at all. A node that has to move keeps its height and takes the near edge of its own pane, which is the smallest move that obeys the rule, and steps down the grid from there if that would land it on top of another -- a file with its payloads in a row would otherwise come up as one payload with the rest hidden behind it. A node off the grid is brought to the nearest grid point in the same way.

That is a change to the document, so it is worth saying what it is not. It is not an edit: nothing goes into the history, because undoing it would put a node back somewhere the editor refuses to put one. And it writes nothing -- the file is byte for byte what it was until something is actually edited, at which point the corrected positions are written along with everything else, because every edit rewrites the whole file. Opening a configuration to look at it is not editing it, and an editor that rewrote a file under version control by being pointed at it would be an editor nobody could point at anything.

Every node is drawn the same width. Nodes of four widths in a column have no edge to line up on, and a diagram built from them looks approximate however carefully each node was placed.

And every node is drawn the same height, 64 pixels, for the reason that follows from the terminals: they are drawn a fixed inset from the top and the bottom of a node, so one height is what puts every node's two rows of terminals the same distance apart. Two nodes level on the grid have their terminals level too, and a line between them runs straight across rather than stepping by the difference between two heights. Height used to be one of the channels that told two types apart, running from 40 to 96 pixels; without it, and with every payload a parallelogram, four edges by two weights could no longer keep the twelve payload types two channels apart. So there are six edges -- one line, two, dashed, dashed and doubled, dotted, and dotted and doubled -- each payload has a line and a weight no other payload has and a typeface of its own, and the other types that leaned on height were given an edge, a weight or a typeface instead. The rule that no two types differ by colour alone is unchanged, and the test that holds it is unchanged.

The canvas is measured in millimetres -- 96/25.4 of its pixels, which is what the window toolkit means by a millimetre -- each one rounded to the nearest whole pixel, so positions in the file stay whole numbers and the grid never drifts. A node's terminals are on lines five millimetres apart, and a node moves in steps of five millimetres to keep them there: its upper left corner is a millimetre short of a line -- at 4 mm, 9 mm, 14 mm and so on -- which puts its terminals' first line a millimetre inside it. A node at any millimetre would have its terminals rounded up to five millimetres from where they belong, and in a node seventeen millimetres tall the two rows would meet. A node placed, a node dragged and a node read from a file all land on one of these places; the dividers between the panes sit on the millimetre grid, and a pane's edges are taken in to the places just inside them, so a node held against an edge is where a node may be as well. The canvas draws a faint line every five millimetres, which are the lines the terminals are on.

Each working pane is painted, and every node type has its own colour inside its pane's family. The MOC pane is a pale blue, the vehicle pane a pale green and the payload pane a pale blush: all three light and neutral -- no chroma above 14 -- so that a pane reads as tinted paper rather than as a coloured panel. The nodes drawn on one are darker members of its own hue family: a payload is a plum or a maroon, a vehicle node a green or an olive, the ground link an azure.

So a node's colour says two things at once. The hue says which family it is in, which makes a node drawn in the wrong pane visible as one; the step within the family -- lightness mostly, with chroma and a little hue beside it -- says which type it is. The selection panes carry their family's ground too, so picking a shape out of a list on a blush ground and putting it on a blush pane says where it goes twice.

The colours were searched for rather than chosen, and three things were asked of the search at once: that every tint stay in the hue family of its own pane, that it be dark enough against that pane to read as a shape -- a contrast of three to one, which is the threshold for something that is not text, and at least 25 CIE units darker -- and that, subject to those, the smallest perceived difference between any two of the fifteen node tints be as large as it can be, measured in CIE L*a*b* under normal vision and under simulated protanopia, deuteranopia and tritanopia at once.

It is fourteen CIE units, where two or three is "the same colour in two printings" and ten is "clearly two colours". So a pair anybody can tell apart is a pair everybody can tell apart, which is what colour blindness asks of a palette; the three pane grounds are fifteen apart under all four.

Fourteen and not the seventeen the earlier family-free palette managed, and the three units are the price of the families: a palette free to put its colours anywhere spreads them further than one whose reds must stay red. What the families buy is a colour that says which pane a node belongs in, which no amount of separation would have told anybody. The families are told apart by more than hue as well, because hue is what a dichromacy takes: the payload family is dark, the vehicle family light, and the ground link's one tint sits where nothing else does. Lightness is the one channel every kind of colour blindness keeps, the total ones included.

The simulation and all of these checks are in the editor's own tests, run against the colours the window is actually given, because a tint nudged by hand would be a near-copy of another under one of the dichromacies and nobody with normal vision would see it. A failure there means the search has to be run again rather than a value adjusted.

A link is drawn as an orthogonal route rather than a straight line -- out of the source, across, into the target, corners rounded -- and it goes around whatever is in the way. Out of the source and into the target, and which way each of those is, is what the terminals decide: the groundward stream leaves a node's right side and arrives at the next one's left side, the ground being the rightmost working pane, and the vehicleward stream leaves a node's left side and arrives at the next one's right side. So a line's first turn is to the left of a node whose terminal is on its left, and the arrowhead at the end points the way the line is actually travelling when it arrives. Before there were terminals a link left every node by the middle of its right edge and arrived at the middle of the next one's left edge, which made a command from the ground to a payload drawn to its left run out to the right and all the way back. A line that runs through a node says there is a connection where there is none, which is worse than a line that is hard to follow: a reader tracing it arrives at the wrong node and has no way of knowing. So a route is chosen rather than computed. A handful of shapes are laid out -- straight through, a vertical run down the middle or beside each node in the way, and a run along a clear row above or below everything -- each is checked against every node on the page, and the one that crosses fewest, turns least and runs shortest is drawn, in that order of priority. Crossings first because a line through a node is a lie about the graph; turns before length because two orthogonal routes between one pair of points are usually the same length, and of two equal routes the one with fewer corners is the one that can be followed. A line keeps six pixels off any node it only passes, because a line along a node's edge reads as a line that touches it. And a route's runs are on the millimetre grid, as the nodes are -- every vertical run, and every horizontal one but the two level with its own ends, which are where its terminals put it -- with the lanes a line may be moved into two millimetres apart, so the lines of a drawing line up with each other and with the boxes they run between. Where nothing misses everything -- a target walled in on every side -- the best available is drawn anyway: a link with no line is a connection that does not appear in the drawing at all.

What is wrong with a node is not drawn on it. The node is painted red -- its outline, its label and its terminals, and in the one shape no real type uses, so colour is never the only thing carrying it -- and the sentence is in that node's own window and in the alert that pops the first time it is wrong. On the canvas it was a sentence inside a box forty-eight pixels tall, which is a paragraph in the middle of a diagram; a diagram is read for its shape.

A node is drawn with its terminals on it: a rectangle of 36 by 14 pixels in each corner it has one in, with an arrow in it pointing the way the data on it travels across the canvas. Two rows, which are the two streams. Along the top, left to right, the payload's own data on its way to the ground: it arrives at payload-in and leaves at payload-out. Along the bottom, right to left, what the ground sends on its way to a payload: it arrives at moc-in and leaves at moc-out. Each box's upper left corner is on one of the lines five millimetres apart, so the terminals of nodes line up with each other and with those lines, and a line between two level terminals runs along one: worked out from where the node is, a millimetre not being a whole number of pixels, and taken at the first line in from the border -- the last at the bottom and the right -- so a box never leaves its node or crosses its slant. The two rows are ten millimetres apart.

So the arrows along the top point right and the arrows along the bottom point left, because that is the way the canvas itself is laid out -- the payloads are the leftmost working pane and the ground the rightmost. A reader who has worked that out once need not read the names again; and a reader who reads them is told whose data it is and whether it arrives or leaves, which is the question somebody about to draw a line is asking.

The boxes were 24 by 10 -- wide enough for an arrow and no wider -- and that is a strip to aim at. A press has to land on a terminal exactly, the terminal being what says which stream the line carries, where the release only has to land on a node; so the box is now twice the area and bigger in both directions, the hand being short in whichever one it is short. What bounds them is a node's size, 152 by 64, app_all_veh being the one type with all four: two rows and their insets have to fit inside that height with daylight between them, and two columns inside the width. A name never collides with them however they grow, being padded away from whichever side carries terminals.

Only the terminals the type actually has. A payload carries its two on its right, a MOC link its two on its left, an app_all_veh all four, a tee its three, a null node its one, a log the one it receives on, and a page of its own none -- what a page has is what the nodes inside it have, and nothing outside it knows that. A drawing that showed four on every node would say nothing about what may be linked to what, which is the one thing about a graph that the shapes cannot say. The table is the schema's own, read rather than copied, so a terminal added there appears on the canvas without the editor being touched.

The label is padded away from whichever side carries terminals rather than a row being reserved at the top and the bottom: the name stays in the middle of the node and stops short of the corner boxes, where reserving rows would leave a few pixels for a name on the shortest node.

Things that might be wrong are counted on a button rather than refused.

A concern is a shape that might be a mistake and might be meant: a payload whose readings reach nothing, a vehicle node a stream arrives at and nothing leaves, one something leaves and nothing arrives at. None of those stops a configuration being read, and every one of them is the ordinary state of a diagram halfway through being drawn -- so an editor that called them errors would be an editor crying wolf at its own user, and one that said nothing would leave a sensor nobody reads to be found in flight.

So the bar carries two buttons, and only when there is something to count: "3 concerns" and "1 set aside". Each opens a list with a line per concern, a Go to it that chooses the node, scrolls the sheet to it and opens its window, and a button that moves it between the two lists. Two buttons because they are two questions: "is there anything I have not looked at", and "what did I decide to leave" -- a decision nobody can find again is a decision nobody can change.

Which rules apply to a node depends on which pane it is in, because that is what the data flow is: a payload is where the payload's own data begins and a MOC link is where it ends, and the other stream runs the other way. A payload's payload-out with nothing on it is a concern -- those readings reach nobody -- where a computation's is not, a computation being free to send its result the other way or to be read by nothing yet. What is asked of a vehicle node is that a stream passing through it comes out somewhere, which is the shape of the whole graph. A log is the exception by having no data source at all: it is where a stream stops on purpose.

Setting one aside is an edit, so undo takes it back, and it is written in the file's own tcsgui section beside where the nodes were drawn: two people editing one configuration should not each have to decide again that a payload nobody reads yet is on purpose. It is remembered by node and kind, so a concern that stops applying leaves both lists by itself and comes back if the line it was about is deleted -- nothing has to be tidied up after drawing.

A click on a node opens a menu of what there is to do with it: Edit, which opens the window of what the node says, and Delete, which asks before anything goes. A node is not in the menu bar -- that is for the configuration as a whole -- and it has nowhere else to put its own commands, so the node itself is where they are asked for. Edit is first because it is what nearly every click wants, and because it is what a click used to do on its own.

A right click opens it, as a right click opens a menu anywhere else, and so does a left click that moved nothing: a press is how a drag starts, so a gesture that moved the node is a move and one that stayed put is this. Choosing a node is still the left press -- a node somebody has started to move is the node they are interested in -- so the node the menu is about is the node drawn as chosen while it is open.

The right click had to be handled by hand rather than left to Slint's ContextMenuArea. The grip that takes a drag covers the whole node and is above everything, so it takes every press on it and the menu area underneath never sees one; the grip shows the menu by name instead.

"Stayed put" is within three pixels, not exactly nowhere. The test was that the release landed on the very pixel of the press, which a hand on a mouse does not manage: every click carried a fraction of a pixel, every click was read as a drag, and the menu was unreachable by the gesture meant to open it. The same slack keeps a trembling click from recording a one-pixel move as an edit.

Delete asks, with the node's name and what goes with it: "two lines are attached to it and go with it". Undo would put all of that back, a removal being one edit, but an undo somebody has to discover is no answer to a node that has already gone and taken four lines with it -- and the press that deleted it is one key away from the press that opens its attributes. Cancel leaves the document exactly as it was, the history included: a question answered no is not an edit.

Lines are also laid clear of each other. Two lines that cross are two lines: the crossing is a point, and a reader follows each one through it. Two lines that run along the same track are one line on the screen -- whichever was drawn second is the only one anybody sees, and a link that is invisible reads as a link that is not there. So each route is laid against the routes already laid: every column and row is offered in lanes either side of itself, seven pixels apart (a line is five wide), and a shared run costs more than a bend. The order the router weighs things in is now: nodes crossed, then length shared with another line, then bends, then distance.

Two overlaps are not the router's to fix, and both are short. Two links leaving one terminal leave from the same point, so their first segments run together for the length of a stub whatever either does afterwards; the same goes for two links arriving at one terminal, which the ground station invites by merging. At that length it reads as one line dividing rather than as a line gone missing, so a shared run shorter than a stub is not counted at all.

What is left is counted and budgeted. On the worked example the lanes take the overlap from a hundred and seven lane-widths to twenty-two -- four fifths of it -- and the remainder is columns with nodes either side and no lane free. The test states the twenty-two as a budget rather than asserting nothing, so that a change which brings wholesale overlap back fails, and so that the day the router learns to clear the rest the number comes down rather than the rule being quietly satisfied by a simpler drawing.

A link is drawn by dragging from a terminal onto the node it is to reach. The terminal is where a line is taken from, and it is the only handle there is: there were two round handles on every node -- one on each side, meaning "a link leaves here" and "a link arrives here" -- and they are gone, because a node has two places a link may leave and two it may arrive at, and a handle in the middle of an edge could not say which of them was meant. So the boxes a reader looks at to see what may be linked to what are the boxes a person presses to link them, which is one thing to learn instead of two.

Where it is let go of is a node and not a terminal. Anywhere on the node will do -- its name, its middle, a corner box, the wrong corner box -- because there is nothing else the gesture could have meant: a stream has one name at each of its ends, so a line drawn from a payload-out can only arrive at a payload-in, and the editor works out which terminal that is and draws the line to it. The terminals stay drawn, since they are what says which streams a type carries and which way; what has gone is having to hit a small box in a corner to say something the stream already said.

Which does not widen what may be joined to what. A node with no terminal of that stream takes nothing: the ground receives readings and is sent nothing, so a line carrying what the ground sends has nowhere to land on a moc node, and a log has nothing to leave it by. The rule is still tcslibgs::graph::terminals; only the aiming has changed.

A link that is already there can be taken hold of by either of its ends. Press the terminal it is attached to and the line comes off the drawing; where it is let go of decides what happens to it. Anywhere on the node it is already attached to: nothing changes, a gesture that has thought better of itself leaving no trace. On another node that has that terminal: the link moves there, in one edit, so one undo puts it back -- the end being carried keeps its own name, a payload-in taken off one node going on another node's payload-in and nowhere else. On a node that has no such terminal, or one the same link already reaches: nothing changes, as a line that cannot be drawn is not drawn.

And over no node at all, it depends which end is in the hand. From the end a line leaves by, the line is deleted -- the gesture a person reaches for, pull the wire out of the thing that is sending and drop it on the floor, and the only way to delete one. From the end it arrives at, nothing happens: a sink takes one source, so dragging its end away and letting go over empty canvas is a slip far more often than a decision, and the line is written on the node it leaves whatever its arriving end was dragged across. Nothing becomes undeletable by that: a line into a sink that takes several -- the ground's -- is deleted from its source end, which has the one line on it.

A terminal with one link on it hands that link over; one with none starts a new one, and so does one with several, because nothing says which of several was meant. A data sink takes one source, so dragging from a sink is usually "move this line"; a data source may reach several sinks, and the one it already feeds is what makes a press on it ambiguous.

Where it lands settles that. A node that could be this end -- one with the same terminal -- takes the end, which is the re-routing gesture. A node that could only be the other end gets a new line instead, and the line that was picked up stays where it was. So the moc node already feeding one payload, pressed on its moc-out and let go of over a second payload, feeds the second one as well: a payload cannot be a data source, so there is nothing else the gesture could have meant.

That case used to do nothing at all, and it is the first thing somebody tried. A second line out of a data source could only be drawn from the sink end -- a rule nobody guessed, and one the drawing gives no hint of.

While the hand is moving, the line being drawn follows it: a dashed line from the end that is staying put to wherever the pointer is, erased and drawn again on every movement. Without it a person aims at nothing -- the line appeared when the gesture was already over, which is to say when it was too late to aim -- and the line in hand is what says the gesture started at all.

Dashed because it is not a connection yet, and straight where a link's own route is orthogonal: this one is being aimed, and a route that turned corners as the hand moved would jump about under it. The dashes are a run of short subpaths, a path in this toolkit having no dash pattern of its own, and the last of them ends under the pointer rather than wherever the pattern stopped: the tip is what somebody is aiming with. Which end stays put is the terminal the gesture started at when a line is being drawn, and the far end when a line is being carried by one of its ends -- that end is in the hand, and the other one is what it still hangs from.

Only the lines are redrawn, never the nodes. Rebuilding the nodes would destroy the element holding the pointer and take the rest of the gesture with it -- which is a fault this window has made once already, when the same press that chose a node rebuilt the canvas under it.

A link may be drawn either way about: from the data source that feeds it or from the data sink that wants feeding. It is the same link, and the file says the same thing -- a person drawing a diagram works in whichever direction they are reading it, and an editor that insisted on one would be an editor to be remembered rather than used.

The gesture will not make a link that the configuration check would refuse. Letting go over a node that has no terminal of the stream being drawn does nothing at all, and so does letting go over the node the drag started on, over empty canvas, or over a node the same link is already drawn to. An editor that cannot make a bad link is worth more than one that makes it and marks it red -- and these refusals are the ways a person's hand slips, not ways of being wrong about the vehicle.

Which terminal was pressed is the whole of what the first half of the gesture says. The second half is a point: a press grabs the pointer, so every event up to the release goes to the terminal that was pressed, and which node is under the pointer at the end is worked out from where the nodes are. That arithmetic is in Rust, with the rest of the geometry, where it can be tested without a window -- and it is now a question about one rectangle per node rather than four small ones, which is the whole of what made aiming hard.

A terminal is drawn inside its node and over the area a drag is taken from, so a press on a corner draws a link and a press anywhere else moves the node. That is the one thing the arrangement costs: a node can no longer be dragged by its corners.

A line's colour says which stream it carries, and a line is drawn twice as wide as it was. Both halves of that are the same point: a line is the thing a reader of a diagram follows, and a grey one two pixels wide left the two streams to be told apart by which corner each line left -- which is reading a diagram by its arithmetic.

So a line carrying the payload's own data is of the payload family and one carrying what the ground sends is of the ground's, each one shade lighter than the lightest node in that panel: ten of CIE lightness above it, where the steps between neighbouring tints are two to eight. Lighter so that a line is not taken for a node's outline, and of the family so that the colour says the stream. tcsgui::tests::a_line_is_one_shade_lighter_than_its_lightest_node holds both to the panels themselves, so a palette change that made a node lighter than its lines fails there -- and holds the two lines fourteen CIE units apart under normal vision and the three dichromacies, which is the floor the palette itself is held to.

What it costs is contrast against the panes: a line at that lightness is between two and three to one against the three grounds, where a node is held to three. The width is what pays for it -- the rule a node is held to is for a shape, and a five-pixel line is read far more easily than its contrast ratio suggests -- but it is a trade and it is written down here rather than left to be noticed.

Each selection panel draws its shapes at three quarters of the size they are on the canvas -- outline, terminals and name together -- so a list is a list of the shapes rather than a column of the canvas, and more of it is in view at once.

Each selection panel is in alphabetical order under each of its headings. A palette is a thing people look in rather than read, and the order it had was the order the type table happens to declare things in, which is no order at all to somebody hunting for rpmsg. Within a heading and not across the panel: a heading is a statement about the entries under it, so a list sorted past its headings would file a device under whichever heading its first letter landed in. The headings keep the table's order, which is the one place that order says something -- devices, then the things that are driven, then the ends of the graph.

There are three outlines, one to a pane, so the outline says which pane a node belongs in before anything else is read: a payload is a parallelogram, a part of the vehicle a rectangle with rounded corners, and the ground the plain rectangle. Within an outline the types are told apart by the line around them, its weight and the way the name is set.

Every part of the vehicle is a rectangle with rounded corners, the process of a data-flow diagram: a computation, a log, a null node, a tee, a page of its own and the three apps aboard. They were five shapes once -- a log the open-ended data store, a page and the apps the barred predefined process -- and each of those said something about the node at the cost of saying it is part of the vehicle. The corners are arcs of nine pixels drawn as a path, in eight straight pieces each, rather than a corner radius on a rectangle: a radius cannot be dashed or doubled, and seven types of one outline need the edges to be told apart by. Nine pixels is tight enough that a terminal three pixels in from the border is inside the curve, so a rounded node's terminals sit where every other node's do.

A payload is a parallelogram, every one of them, the flowchart symbol for input and output on an unspecified medium -- which is what a payload is: a bus, a socket, a channel, a sample buffer, a file, lines driven, a program. The outline alone then says which nodes are what the vehicle carries; gpio was a keyed rectangle and the two payloads that are programs wore the bars of a predefined process, and each of those said something about the node at the cost of saying it is a payload. It is the one symbol in the notation that means data crossing a boundary rather than a thing or a step, and a payload is exactly that boundary. It leans right, as the symbol is drawn, so the corners it gives away are the top left and the bottom right.

The top left is where a payload's payload-out terminal is, so this is the first shape whose slant had to be paid for rather than dodged. Three things move with it, all of them from style::room_at, which says how far in the shape is at the row a given terminal sits in: the terminal box itself, so the arrow stays on the node; the point where a line joins the node, so the line stops where the shape is instead of out in the notch beside it; and the column the name is padded away from, so a name is not padded into a terminal that has moved. The room is measured at the edge of the terminal's box nearest the top or the bottom, not at the row's middle, because a leaning side is furthest in at one end of the box and taking the middle would leave half of it hanging out.

Nothing is drawn inside a leaning shape that could spill out of it. The second line of a doubled edge is inset as a smaller box and so leans by its own slant, and a test holds it inside the outer line.

The shapes are the ones the standard notations use, which is worth more than being inventive: a reader who has seen a data-flow diagram already knows that a parallelogram is data crossing a boundary, a rounded box is a process and a plain rectangle is something outside the system.

The ground station has worn a pill and a five-sided point before this. Both said "not like the others" by being unlike anything; the rectangle says it by being the one shape a reader of these notations already knows the meaning of, and it is free to say it now that the payloads wear the data symbol. It is the same size as everything else, 152 by 64, and it is wide, solid, azure and set in the largest italic, so it catches the eye without being the node shaped oddly.

Every outline -- a side that leans, corners drawn in pieces, a plain box -- arrives as path data from style::shape_of and the markup draws them with a Path -- the same arrangement as the link routes, and for the same reason: geometry that can be read and tested belongs in Rust rather than in a layout language. A Path has no drop shadow, so the lift such a node gets while it is being dragged is a second copy of the shape, three pixels lower and barely visible, drawn under the first.

A terminal is drawn in the corner of a node's box, so a shape that gives a corner away has to answer for the terminal that was in it. A test holds every shape drawn from a path to that: each terminal of the type has to be inside the path, and anything the markup draws inside the shape has to be inside it too. It is checked as the rule rather than as the shape, because the reasoning is about where the terminals are, and a shape kept while a terminal moved would otherwise draw an arrow floating beside the node.

Nothing is drawn as a pattern inside a node. A body is its own colour and nothing else: a pattern there competes with the name written over it and with the colour the body carries, and it was the one channel that looked like noise rather than like a statement. What it used to tell apart is now told apart by the line around the outside, which may be doubled, broken, both or neither -- two independent facts, so four values out of one channel.

Only four pairs of types ever leaned on the old pattern channel, all of them payload kinds drawn at the same shape, width and height, and the new one carries all four: a dashed serial against a plain I2C, and HDLC doubled, Network dashed and IIO both, which separates the three of them from each other.

A broken line is only available to a straight-sided outline. Dashes are walked along the shape's own polygon -- style::polygon_of, which is also where the filled shape and the inner line come from -- and the rounded outline is a corner radius with no polygon to walk. A test holds the two types drawn with a radius to a single whole line, because the markup would otherwise draw a plain rounded rectangle and say nothing about the dash it could not manage.

The dashes themselves are subpaths, one per dash: Slint's Path takes no dash pattern, so a broken line is drawn as the pieces that are there, which is what route.rs already does for the line being dragged. The pattern is walked round the whole shape rather than restarted at each side, so it carries on around a corner instead of putting a dash on all four of them. And the shape is filled by one path and stroked by another, because filling a row of short subpaths would fill each dash rather than the node.

Colour remains the redundant channel for all that. Nothing is told apart by colour alone: every type differs from every other in at least two of outline, border width, the line around it, height and the way its label is set, and a test fails if that stops being true. Red is reserved for things that are wrong.

The colours themselves are three tiers of one hue family, lightest first: a working pane is the palest shade of it, a link line carrying that family's data is darker, and the node bodies are darker still. A drawing then reads as one picture -- a pale sheet, lines drawn on the sheet, solid blocks sitting on the lines -- rather than as three unrelated colour choices, and a line can never be mistaken for a node's own edge. Each step is at least eight CIE units, which is "clearly lighter" rather than "possibly lighter".

The three panes are chosen as a set: a blue, a green and a teal, each a wash, 14 CIE units apart from one another under four ways of seeing. That is the thing that changed. They used to be chosen one at a time, and came out at 84, 91 and 95 of lightness with chromas of 6, 14 and 14 -- a pale blue, a near-white green and a grey. Three pale colours are told apart mostly by lightness, so that spread was their separation, and a canvas whose three grounds have nothing in common is a canvas that reads as three unrelated choices rather than one palette.

The trade was measured before it was made. Holding the three within four points of lightness and three of chroma -- the most coherent the gamut allows -- the best floor any trio reaches is 13.2; within six, 13.8; within eight, 14.6; and with no constraint at all, 17.1, which is the shape the canvas had. These three sit at 90, 90 and 82 with chromas of 13.5, 13.5 and 8.6, and their closest pair is 14.5 against the 15.2 of the three that came before. Half a unit, for a canvas that holds together.

A pane no longer carries its family's hue; the payload pane and what it holds happen to share one, teal, and the other two need not. Which pane a node belongs in is said by the family's own hue -- family_hue -- and a pane's job is to be a quiet ground that the other two are not. What a pane is still held to is the rest of it: above 82 of lightness, under a chroma of 14, 8 clear of both link lines, 10 clear of the window's white chrome, and light enough that its own family's lightest body keeps three to one against it and sits 25 points below it. The contrasts across all fifteen bodies run 3.2 to 9.4 to one, and the scheme floor for everything a reader must tell apart is where it was, at 12.2.

One rule had to change to say what it always meant. A node's colour says which pane it belongs in, and that was checked against the pane's hue; a pane with a chroma of 1.8 has no hue worth measuring against, its number being arithmetic on a rounding error. Each family's hue is declared instead -- family_hue: the blue of the ground link at 265 degrees, the green of the vehicle's own graph at 118, the teal of what it carries at 200 -- and the three are held 60 degrees apart, because a family spans 40 either side of its centre and two families that overlap say nothing about where a node belongs.

Node body colours are derived, not chosen. Each panel's nodes are a ramp: it starts twelve CIE units below the line that carries that panel's data -- the vehicle panel below both lines, having none of its own -- and steps three and a half units darker for each node, taking the panel's nodes in alphabetical order. The ground station takes one rung, the vehicle's four, and what the vehicle carries ten, from 46.2 of lightness down to 15.0 -- the first twelve below the payload line, which is the same family's hue, so that the lightest payload body is a darker teal than the line and plainly not it. A reader finds a node in a panel by how dark it is, and a panel reads as one family instead of as a set of unrelated colours.

The first step is twelve rather than the eight the tiers ask, because the ground station's line and its bodies are the same hue: at one hue a step in lightness is the difference between two colours, and twelve is this scheme's floor. The ramp stops at thirteen of lightness, which is where the three families stop being told apart -- below it a dark blue, a dark green and a dark teal converge under deuteranopia. A panel with more nodes than its ramp has rungs starts again at its first colour, so two of its nodes share one. The apps are the ones that wrap: a panel's other types are walked first and the apps after them, so the two payload apps take the first two payload colours again and the three vehicle apps the first three vehicle colours, and no type that had a colour before the apps came lost it.

What this gives up is the twelve units between neighbours in one panel. Ten steps of twelve would want a hundred and twenty units of lightness out of the eighty-seven there are, so within a panel the steps are three to four units -- "a shade darker" -- while across panels the closest pair is 29.9. That is a deliberate trade and the reason colour is the redundant channel here: every pair of node types differs in two of outline, edge, border width, size and the way its label is set, and a test holds them to it. What a body's colour says is which panel it belongs to and how far down the panel it sits.

A body is painted in its own tint now rather than washed with a fifth of it, and what is written on it -- the outline, the name, the terminals, the marks in its corners -- is white. The pane's own shade was the first thing tried for that and is not light enough: the payload pane is the darkest of the three at 84 of lightness, and its shade on its own lightest body is 3.4 to one, under the floor for text somebody has to read. White clears 4.5 to one against every body, and a test says so.

Each link line is a darker shade of the pane its data comes from. The payload's own readings start in the payload pane and generally end at the ground, so that line is a teal taken from the teal pane they leave; what the ground sends starts in the moc pane and ends at a payload, so that line is a darker blue. A reader can then see where a line's data started without following it back, and the hue is held within twenty degrees of its own pane's by a test.

"A bit darker" turned out to be twenty-four and thirty-two points, and the reason is worth writing down. A line has to stay eight points above the lightest node body, which puts its floor at 57.4 of lightness; and it has to be seen on a pane at 90, which puts the lightest usable shade at 58.2. The two meet, with nothing in between: anything lighter reaches about 1.3 to one against its own pane and vanishes, and nothing in the gamut reaches 2.5 to one at all while staying clear of the bodies. So both lines sit at 58.2 -- as light as a line can be here -- and the panes they are shades of are simply much paler than they are.

A link line also has to be unmistakable from the fault red, which is the one colour that means something on its own. The payload line was once a dusty red at hue 23 -- the fault's own side of the colour space, 27 CIE units from it, which is "another colour" to measure and "something is wrong with that line" to look at. It is a teal now, 42 units away, and the ground's line is a blue at 80. The floor is 35, asked under each of the four ways of seeing, and it is a floor for lines only. A node body is not held to it: a faulty node is drawn in the fault's colour and in the one shape no real type uses, so a dark green body can exist because the shape carries the warning too. A line has no second channel.

Getting the tiers cost two units of separation. Every colour of the scheme is held apart from every other -- the twenty of them, panes and lines and bodies together, under normal vision and each of the three dichromacies -- and the floor is now 12 where the bodies alone managed 14. Six bodies moved down to get under the lines, which took the top off the range they had; the closest pair left is an olive and a gold at 12.2. Twelve is still above the ten that means "clearly two colours" and well above the two or three of "the same colour in two printings".

What was asked for and is not there is a rung per body: each body a step darker than the last, down the panel. It does not exist. Under protanopia and deuteranopia a single hue family is very nearly a lightness scale, so ten payload bodies each far enough from the rest would want ten steps of fourteen -- a hundred and forty units of lightness out of the eighty-seven there are. Forcing a ladder drops the floor to about ten, and re-fitting the family to win it back leaves each colour's name describing a colour it no longer is. The bodies are a range of shades instead, spanning 33 units from the lightest to the darkest, and what separates any two of them is the floor above.

The directory a configuration goes in is made if it is not there. A name the editor is started on need not exist -- that is how a new configuration is begun -- and a name in a directory that does not exist is the same intention one level up. Without that, every write of the session failed: the editor saves each edit as it is made, so somebody who named a path with a new directory in it got a window that worked, an alert about the first edit, and nothing on the disk. All the missing components are made, since a path somebody typed may name several. A directory that cannot be made at all -- usually a file in the way of one -- is its own kind of failure with its own sentence, because its answer is different: the others are answered by freeing space or by waiting, and this one by naming a directory that can exist.

Nothing is raised about a configuration that is one node or none. A new configuration opens with the ground station already in it, and the moment that node exists every rule above has something to say about it: nothing is linked to it, because there is nothing to link it to. A count on the button before anybody has drawn anything is a complaint about the editor's own first act, and it cannot be acted on until there is a second node. The rules come back as soon as there is a graph to apply them to.

A configuration that does not exist yet opens with the ground station already in it, at the upper right, in the moc working pane. Every graph has one -- a vehicle whose telemetry reaches nobody is a vehicle nobody is operating -- so placing it is work the editor can do rather than work it can wait for. It is placed through the history like any other edit, so it can be taken back by somebody drawing a page of a larger configuration.

A configuration the editor can read and the vehicle could not fly is asked about when it is opened, in a window of its own: one row per problem with a node, each said in the words of the rule it breaks, each offering what can be done about that kind of problem. A node named twice, a node with no name, a type this build does not know, attributes that do not hold together, a node in a group nobody declared, a payload that is written to and states no trigger.

What is offered depends on what is wrong. Where a name is the fix -- two nodes sharing one, or a node without one -- the row carries a field and a Suggest button, which offers the name the editor would have placed the node with; that is the one kind of problem the editor can mend by itself. Every row whose node is in the document also offers Edit, which opens that node's own window, and Delete, which goes through the same confirmation any other deletion does. Rename waits until the names all differ, none is blank and none belongs to a third node, since a repair that left the same question standing would be no answer; what is wrong with the names is said under the list rather than beside a row, so the list does not shift as somebody types.

Every fault gets a row. A problem with no row went to the alert on its own, which says what is wrong and offers nothing -- and an alert with nothing to press is a complaint rather than a question. So a line that goes nowhere offers delete the line, a group with nothing in it offers delete the group, and a tcspecial parameter that does not hold together offers edit the parameters, which opens that window. The line whose removal is offered is the one the document holds, origin and input included, because that is what a removal is matched by.

Doing nothing is one of the offers, and there are two of them. Leave this one closes the window on that problem. Ignore them all answers every remaining problem at once, which is what somebody opening a file to look at it wants: the nodes are added as they stand, the faults stay faults -- drawn in red on their own nodes, refused by tcsverify -- and nothing says any of it again for the rest of the session.

That last part is a decision about the session and not about the file, which is worth stating. A judgement about a concern is written into the file, since somebody else reading the drawing should see what was decided; nobody should inherit somebody else's decision to ignore an error, so this one is forgotten when the editor closes. A fault that goes away and comes back is raised again, the set being kept by what each one says.

Either way the node is on the canvas in its own working pane, which is what lets somebody look at the drawing before choosing. A type nothing knows goes in the vehicle pane, the two others meaning something about what a node is where that type says nothing. An editor that would not show a configuration until it was correct could not be used to correct one.

Answering one problem leaves the window on the rest rather than closing it: a file with four problems should not need opening four times, and one that has been mended should leave the list rather than sit there answered.

And the alert says nothing about the state the file arrived in. Both windows used to open on a file with problems -- one that offered something and one that offered nothing -- so what the file came with is counted as said before either is shown. What the alert is for afterwards is what goes wrong while somebody works, which is news.

Whatever comes up as the editor starts is in front of the main window, and the main window takes nothing until it has been answered. The question about the file's problems, and an alert about a file that could not be read, are asked inside the main window, as a dialog in front of its drawing, rather than in windows of their own. A window of its own cannot be kept in front: the order of windows is the window manager's, and on Wayland no program can ask for its window to stay on top, so a question shown in a window of its own could land behind the drawing waiting for it, where nobody finds it and nothing in front of them takes a press. While the main window waits, a grey veil over the whole of it greys everything under it and holds its presses, the dialog is in front of the veil, and the menu items are disabled, the keyboard being another way to them. The drawing is still there to be read through the veil, being what the question is about. What goes wrong while somebody works is still said in the alert's own window, the main window not waiting for it. tcsmoc does the same for the question it asks when tcspecial serves a different configuration: asked inside its main window, in front of a veil that greys the panels. tcsmoc and tcssim show what they found at startup in a window that stays on top and is closed before their main windows open.

Everything a popup says can be copied. A message is often the thing somebody has to pass on, and one that can only be read has to be typed out again, wrongly. So popups say what they say in text that can be selected -- drag across it for some of it, Ctrl+A for all of it, Ctrl+C to copy -- and each has a Copy button for the whole of it at once, heading and lines one to a line. The small popups on tcsmoc's and tcssim's panels close on a press outside them rather than on any press, a press inside being somebody selecting.

Renaming one of two nodes that share a name is done by position, which is the one thing Edit::RenameNode cannot express: a name is how an edit says which node it means, so an edit cannot mean "the second of the two". It is also not recorded in the history, which is the decision worth stating. A duplicate is the file being unreadable rather than somebody's drawing, and an undo would put it back -- which, every edit being written as it is made, would write a broken configuration to the disk. Undo takes back what was drawn; this was not drawn. The repair is written at once instead, so the document and the file never disagree about a name.

The links stay with whoever keeps the name. A link names a name: while two nodes shared one, every link naming it was ambiguous, and renaming one of them does not make the link mean the other. When the last holder of a name is renamed there is no ambiguity left and the links follow, as they do for any other rename.

The simulator's file is held to the same rule from both ends. Two entries of one name are refused outright -- a name is the whole of how the two files are joined, so two of them are two sets of settings for one payload and the file does not say which applies -- and so is an entry naming a payload the configuration does not have. Both are refused rather than half-read: the file parses, so adopting part of it would mean writing the rest of it away at the next edit. A file that cannot be parsed at all is different and keeps its old treatment -- reported, and an empty one put in its place, because an editor that will not start on a damaged file cannot be used to fix one. That is the distinction sim::Trouble draws. And renaming a payload node carries its simulated entry with it, so the editor cannot be the thing that breaks the join.

A node placed from a palette is named after its type, a hyphen, and the first number that is free: i2c-1, then i2c-2, and i2c-1 again if the first one is deleted -- the number says which of them this is, not how many have ever been placed. Numbered from the first rather than from the second, which is what it used to be: a bare i2c and then an i2c-2 is two kinds of name for one kind of thing, the first one's name becomes the odd one out the moment a second is placed, and a diagram of type names reads as a diagram of types rather than of nodes. A number on every one of them also says plainly that the name is the editor's suggestion rather than a description. The ground station a new configuration is given is named the same way, by the same function, because a name the editor chose should look the same whichever of its hands chose it.

Nothing the editor places itself covers a pane's own name. The three names are written across the top of the sheet -- "MOC", "Vehicle", "Payloads", one per pane at the same height -- and they are what a pane has to say when it is empty or when somebody has lost their bearings. A node somebody drags over one is their own business, the sheet being their drawing; a node the editor put there would be the editor covering a name nobody asked it to cover. So the strip they sit in is reserved against automatic placement: pane::NAMES, four pixels down and sixteen tall, across every pane rather than three strips, because a name belongs to a pane and the panes' edges move with their dividers. Sixteen for a ten-pixel face is the line box and a pixel, so a node's border does not sit on a descender -- minimal in the sense asked for, encompassing the three names and nothing else.

It is reserved for everything the editor places, not only for a node placed by hand: arranging the whole canvas goes through the same placing, and the ground station a new configuration is given is held clear of it by an assertion at the constant. A test reads the names' own geometry out of main.slint and holds the reserved strip against it, so a name moved down the sheet cannot leave the strip reserved where nothing is written.

A new node goes as high as it will fit in its own pane without covering anything, and in view: the highest free place in the part of the sheet the window is showing, or failing that the lowest free place above it, or the highest below -- and then the sheet is scrolled so the new node's top is at the top of the view. A node placed where it cannot be seen is a press that did nothing as far as the person who made it can tell.

Edit -> Arrange the nodes applies that rule to every node at once: each to the top of its own pane, in the order the file holds them, nothing covering anything, and the view back at the top of the sheet. The sheet is 960 by 2400 and a window shows a few hundred pixels of it, so a configuration read out of a file -- or one left spread about by a morning's dragging -- can have most of itself outside the window with nothing to say so. This is the one press that brings it back.

It is one edit, not one per node, so one undo puts a hand-made layout back: somebody who arranged a diagram carefully and then tidied it by accident must not have to press undo eleven times and get it half right. Nothing is recorded when there was nothing to move, because an undo that changes nothing is an undo that looks broken. And it is an edit, which Document::settle deliberately is not -- settling puts a node where the editor would never let one be dragged, and arranging puts it where anybody could have dragged it.

The menu bar holds what belongs to the configuration as a whole rather than to anything on the canvas -- there is nothing to press on a diagram that would mean "the configuration", or "the groups", or "the way out". File holds Save, the two kinds of group, and Exit; Edit holds the tcspecial parameters, arranging the nodes, and generating the Rust.

Exit offers to write anything the files do not have before it goes, which is normally nothing. Three answers rather than two: Save and leave, Leave without saving -- because somebody who has just been told the device is full may want to go and deal with it, and a program that insisted on writing first would be one they cannot get out of -- and Stay, because the menu is next to everything else and gets pressed by accident.

The undo history

Every edit is written to the configuration as it is made, so the history is not protecting anybody's work: the work is on the disk already. What it buys is the ability to undo past a restart or a crash -- a convenience and not a safety net, which is what decides where it lives and how long for.

It lives in the user's own state directory: $XDG_STATE_HOME/tcsgui, or ~/.local/state/tcsgui where that is not set. The specification names this exact case -- state that should persist between restarts but is not important enough to be data, with history given as an example -- and it is deliberately not the cache directory, a cache being something a program can regenerate.

It used to sit beside the configuration, as <name>.tcsgui-history, on the argument that a configuration copied to another machine should take its history with it. That argument was worth less than it cost: a configuration lives in a directory under version control, so the file turned up in git status, in review and in release tarballs -- and nobody has ever wanted to undo an edit on a machine they copied a file to.

Each history is named for the configuration's own name and a digest of its absolute path -- graph-3f91c2d4e5f60718.yaml -- so that a person looking in the directory can tell what belongs to what and two configurations called graph.yaml in two directories are two histories. The file says which configuration it is the history of, because a digest cannot be read backwards. The digest is FNV-1a written out in the source rather than the standard library's hasher, which does not promise the same answer across Rust releases: a hash that changed with the toolchain would orphan every history on the disk without saying so.

It is deleted on a clean exit, which is what every other editor does: the history of a session that ended tidily is of no further use, and a directory that only ever grows is one somebody has to tidy. It is kept when the editor does not exit tidily -- a crash, a kill, a machine that went down -- because that is the undo somebody will want when they come back; and kept, too, when the exit was asked for but the last save failed, which is the one case where the history is the only copy of a session's work.

And it is pruned at startup, two ways: a history older than a week, and one whose configuration is no longer where it was. The first is the ordinary bound -- a crash a fortnight ago is not an undo anybody is waiting for -- and the second is tidiness nothing else would do, a configuration being free to be deleted or renamed by anything at all. A file in that directory that tcsgui does not understand is left alone: it is not the prune's place to decide that somebody else's file is rubbish.

What tcspecial itself is configured with is edited from the Edit menu, under Edit tcspecial parameters. That is the one part of a configuration which is about the whole of it rather than about anything in it -- so there is nothing on the canvas to press that would mean "the configuration", and a command is how it is reached. The window offers every attribute the section may have, the ones that must be stated first and marked as such, and the list it is built from is the checker's own: two lists would drift, and what that would look like is a configuration the editor called complete and tcsverify refused.

A field is updated in place rather than rebuilt. Every character typed is an edit, every edit a refresh, and a refresh used to replace the model a window's fields are built from -- which destroys the elements the repeater made from it, the text box among them, and takes the cursor with it. The symptom was a value that had to be typed one character and one click at a time, with the focus underline flickering under each. So a list of the same shape has its rows updated where they differ and is otherwise left alone, and a list that is a different list is replaced, the elements not being the same elements anyway. It is the same rule the canvas already followed for a drag -- rebuild nothing the pointer is holding -- and refilled is where it is written.

The section is read as the words a file holds rather than as the struct the command interpreter wants, and the strictness applied afterwards. That is what lets the editor write a section somebody is halfway through filling in: a port cleared on the way to typing another leaves a file that still opens, says what is missing, and can be put right, where a file read strictly would not open at all -- and a file the editor cannot read back is a file it has destroyed. A parameter somebody clears is written out of the file rather than written empty, absent and empty meaning the same thing to the checking. The same goes for a node's attributes, and on the way in as well as the way out: a hand-written address: with nothing after it is read as a question not yet answered -- as is address: "" -- and saved as neither, the line simply not being there.

The simulator's file is the one place a blank is written out, because there the blank is the point: that file is a form, with every payload of the configuration and every setting a payload may state, so that a reader can see what there is to answer. An unanswered setting is written with nothing after the colon rather than as "": the two read the same, and "" says somebody typed two quotation marks.

A configuration that does not exist yet is given the section's attributes and, with one exception, none of its values. The window then has a field apiece and the configuration answers nothing, which is the honest state of it: an editor that filled in a protocol and a beacon interval on the grounds that they are the same on every vehicle until somebody has a reason would be making a configuration that looks as answered as one somebody thought about. Nothing of an unanswered section reaches the file -- a tcspecial: with nothing under it would read back as no section at all.

The exception is the beacon's interval, which a new configuration starts at 1000 ms. It is an exception because it is a different kind of question. A socket is a fact about this mission and there is no value that is right until somebody says so; "how often does the vehicle say it is alive" has an answer that is right until somebody has a reason, and once a second is that answer. parameters::default_of is where it is written, so the editor and anything else that makes a new section agree about it.

The description tree

What node types there are is data rather than code in the editor. A node type arrives as two files: Rust in tcspecial, and a description tcsgui reads.

Two trees with one structure, family by family. A description at vehicle/compute/vote.yaml has its Rust at tcspecial/src/node/vehicle/compute/vote.rs; one at payload/i2c.yaml has node/payload/i2c/, a directory with as many files under it as it takes. moc.yaml sits at the root of the tree because the ground station has no Rust of its own: it is the other end of the link rather than part of the vehicle, and the code that serves one is the network payload's.

The correspondence is checked both ways by tcsgui::nodes::tests::every_description_has_its_rust: a description with no Rust is a node the editor offers and the vehicle cannot run, and a node type implemented and not described is one nobody can place. The second direction is asked of the directories the descriptions cover, so that once a family has one description in it, every file in that family needs one -- which is what keeps a family from being half described.

Which terminals a type has is declared there, and so is what a node of it may be told. Both were written in Rust where nobody adding a node type could see them, and both are still read from Rust by the parts that cannot afford to read a file -- the checking and the flight software have tcslibgs::graph::terminals, and a window has style::NodeType::attributes for a type the tree does not describe. So the tree is held against those: the_trees_terminals_are_the_schemas and a_description_of_a_whole_type_says_what_the_type_says. A description of one variant of a type is exempt from the second, since mean lists what a mean needs where the table lists what any compute node may say; for those the description is the authority, a node's window being built from it.

Two trees rather than one was a decision, and the reason is what each tree is for. The descriptions are data read at run time, installed on a workstation, and editable; the Rust is compiled into the flight software. A .yaml beside a .rs in src/ would be a file cargo neither builds nor ships, sitting where everything else is both.

A description is YAML or XML as its extension says, file by file, and nothing cares which a given node used -- one of the shipped ones is XML for exactly that reason. One file per node, in one spelling, which every_node_is_described_once_and_in_one_spelling says: a node described twice is two statements of what its terminals are. What it says:

node: mean                 # what the palette shows, and the name a placed
                           # node starts with
type: compute              # the configuration's own word for the type
about: The mean of the readings arriving at its named inputs.
sets:                      # attributes the editor fills in and does not ask
  function: mean
payload-sink: true         # which of the four terminals a node of this
payload-source: true       # type has: the payload's own data in and out,
moc-sink: true             # and what the ground sends in and out
moc-source: true
attributes:                # what a node of this kind says for itself
  - name: inputs
    required: true
    about: Which inputs it is over, comma separated.

The shape of one of those files has two schemas of its own, node.xsd and node.schema.json, beside the configuration's pair and installed with them; they are held against each other and against the reader by tcsgui/tests/schema.rs.

sets is what turns one node type into several things to place. compute is one type, and the tree makes it four entries -- mean, hysteresis, to_kelvin and process -- each a compute node with its function already answered. Pressing mean puts down a node called mean that is a mean from the moment it exists, so the panel asks it for a mean's attributes and the checking holds it to a mean's rules. Before the tree it put down a node called compute with nothing answered, and a person had to know that function was a word and that mean was one of its values.

Which is also the rule for the selection panes: a type the tree describes is offered as its descriptions; a type the tree says nothing about is offered as itself. A page of its own is described by nothing -- what a page holds is what has terminals -- so it is still offered as itself, and so is every payload. An editor that cannot find the tree at all offers every type as itself, which is exactly what it offered before there was a tree.

What a computation is told is the flight software's own list, and the tree is held to it: tcslibgs::node::compute::attributes_of says what each computation takes, params_of refuses anything else, and a test says each description asks for exactly that list. A description that asked for something else would be a panel filling in a field the checking then refuses, or leaving out one it requires.

A file that is not a description is refused on its own and the rest of the tree is still read: a tree people edit has a half-written file in it sooner or later, and an editor that offered nothing because one file has a typo in it would be an editor a typo turns off. What is wrong goes to the error output, as an unreadable preferences file does. Two descriptions of one name are refused the same way -- a name picks out one node here as it does in a configuration.

Each attribute's about is shown at the foot of a node's window, in a box that cannot be written in, while that attribute's field has the focus; the node's own about is shown there while no field with something said about it has. At the foot rather than beside each field, so that the rows stay a row each however long a sentence is, and read-only because what a description says is the tree's to say and not the node's.

Schemas beside the tree

tcsgui/share/schema holds a schema for each file tcsgui edits, in each spelling: a JSON Schema for the YAML and an XML Schema for the XML, for the configuration and for the simulator's file. They are installed with the description tree, under $(PREFIX)/share/tcsgui/schema.

What they are for is the one thing an editor cannot do for a file somebody is writing by hand or with another tool: tell them, in their own editor, that payloads is a section and payload is not. A JSON Schema is what an editor's YAML language server actually reads, and a YAML document is a JSON document in another spelling, so the pair covers both.

What they check is shape: which sections there are, what an entry may say, and which words are words. What they do not check is whether a configuration can be flown -- which attributes a node of a given type must state, whether two payloads claim one socket, whether a mean's inputs are the ones its links bind. Those are rules about meaning, they are tested against real buses, and tcsverify is the one place they live. A file a schema accepts may still be refused, and the refusal will say why; the README beside the schemas says this to whoever installs them.

Two things follow from that division. A node's attributes are the union over every node type -- a bus address, a baud rate and a setpoint are all allowed on any node -- because a schema that knew which type takes which would be a second copy of rules that already exist. And the XSD cannot say that a node's name appears exactly once: payload-out may appear many times, several destinations being several elements, and XSD 1.0 allows neither repeats nor a mixture inside xs:all. A node with two names is refused where the file is read instead.

A schema an editor has to be pointed at by hand is a schema nobody points it at, so every configuration this project writes names its own. In XML that is the two attributes every XML tool reads:

<configuration
    xmlns:xsi="http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema-instance"
    xsi:noNamespaceSchemaLocation="tcsgui/share/schema/tcspecial.xsd">

and in YAML it is a comment, there being no other way to say it in YAML:

# yaml-language-server: $schema=tcsgui/share/schema/tcspecial.schema.json

Both are written by nodes::schema_reference, which gives the path relative to the file where the schemas are above it -- which is a checkout, where these examples live and where the relative path is the one that is true -- and the installed path where they are not. A file saved anywhere else therefore points at $(PREFIX)/share/tcsgui/schema, which is where the schemas are on a machine the program is installed on.

Two consequences. The YAML comment is followed by a blank line, which is what keeps it a note about the file rather than about the first entry: a comment's claim on what comes after it ends at a blank line, and that rule is read::comments_of's.
