And GraphFile names those two XML attributes as fields it reads and ignores, because deny_unknown_fields is worth keeping: a misspelled section is one the software silently does nothing about, so every word a configuration may contain is a word named in that struct.

The simulator's file says the same thing about tcspecial-sim.schema.json, written by sim::to_yaml from the path the file is going to. It names no XSD, the editor writing that file in YAML and nothing else -- the XML spelling of that schema is shipped for a file written by hand.

Nothing aboard reads any of these references. They are for the person editing the file, in the same way the tcsgui section is.

A schema nothing is checked against is worse than no schema: it refuses files the software accepts, or accepts what the software refuses, and either way the person believes the schema. So tcsgui/tests/schema.rs holds each against the lists the software itself reads from -- the section's parameters from graph::parameters::ATTRIBUTES, the simulator's settings from sim_config::SETTINGS -- rather than against a copy written out there, which would be the same problem one step further away. Every shipped XML file is validated with xmllint, and every key of every shipped YAML file is looked for in the JSON Schema, every shipped configuration and every shipped simulator configuration is checked for the reference to its own schema and for that schema being where it says, and every shipped XML file is checked for being well formed XML at all. That last check found four files that were not: they said -- inside a comment, which XML forbids, and nothing had noticed because quick-xml reads them anyway and no other tool had been asked to.

That last test found a real fault the moment it was written: an XML comment may not contain two hyphens in a row, and this project writes -- as an em dash everywhere -- so a note somebody typed in the editor made the file unreadable rather than merely different. The writer now folds a run of hyphens to one in an XML comment, which is a thing the XML spelling does not preserve.

Where the tree is, in order: $TCSGUI_DATA, then $XDG_DATA_HOME/tcsgui (or ~/.local/share/tcsgui), then /usr/local/share/tcsgui, then /usr/share/tcsgui, then tcsgui/share and share under the working directory. The user's own directory before the installed one, because that is where edited descriptions belong and where a person's own node goes; the working directory last, so that running the editor out of a checkout finds the checkout's own. make install-data puts them under $(PREFIX)/share/tcsgui, which is /usr/local/share/tcsgui for software built locally and /usr/share/tcsgui for a distribution's package: architecture-independent data belonging to one package, which is where the filesystem hierarchy standard puts it.

The editor is lazy

It requires every value to be valid and requires none of them to be entered.

An editor that insisted on a complete node before it would let you move on is an editor that fights a person working out what they are describing: a diagram is drawn by placing things and filling them in afterwards, in whatever order the thinking goes. So an empty field is a question not yet answered, not a fault, and nothing in the window is red for being blank. A field the configuration will need says so with a mark beside its name, which is information rather than a complaint.

What is entered is held to the rules that have been tested against real buses. A bus address of 0xZZ is wrong the moment it is typed, an attribute belonging to another type is wrong on this one, and a beacon address with no port is wrong as soon as the section is complete -- each in the words the flight software would refuse it with, because the editor reaches those rules rather than restating them. A node is put to them once everything its type must have is there: until then the only thing to say is "this is unfinished", which the person at the keyboard already knows.

Requiring that it all be entered is tcsverify's and the flight software's, where the complaint can name the file, the line and the attribute:

vehicle.yaml:11: "temp_fore" is an i2c node, so it needs the device it is
reached through: say path
vehicle.yaml:13: "moc" is a network node, so it needs the host it is at:
say address

That check is new, and the editor's laziness rests on it: before it, a node that said nothing at all passed every ground check and was found when the vehicle tried to open it.

The simulator's own configuration is edited from the menu too, where the editor was given one: tcsgui takes a second file name, and Edit simulated groups opens the groups of settings it declares. The entry is greyed when there is no second file rather than hidden -- a menu whose entries come and go is a menu nobody learns.

Requirement

The simulator configuration is written as a form: every payload the configuration has gets an entry, and every entry gets every setting, written empty where nobody has filled it in. A file holding nothing said nothing about which payloads there were to configure or what could be said about them, which is what somebody opening it wanted to know.

Which payloads there are is the configuration's to say, so the entries are its payload nodes in its own order -- a computation, a log and the ground link are not simulated and get none. An entry the file already held for a name that is not a payload is written out after them: it is somebody's typing, and tcsverify is what says a simulated payload names no payload.

Requirement
The simulator's own reader takes a setting written empty as a setting not stated. Without that the form would be a file written to be rejected: an empty numeric setting was a type error, so a half-filled form could not be loaded at all. It is the rule the editor already states for every field it offers -- absent and empty are one thing to the checking, and a cleared field is not an answer -- and a value that is there and wrong is still refused, naming it.
Requirement
That second file is YAML, and its name has to end in .yaml. A name that does not is refused on the command line, naming the file and saying what is wanted, and the session does not start. A simulator configuration is a YAML file -- that is what the shipped sets are -- and one spelling of it is one thing for a reader to know rather than two. The editor could write it as XML, the formats being two spellings of one set of values everywhere else here, and does not: nothing wanted the second spelling, and a file nobody writes is a format nobody has read back. .yml is refused with the rest, being the same language under a second name.
Requirement

-r, --read-only or --readonly, anywhere on the command line, starts a session that shows the configuration and changes nothing: tcsgui --read-only thermostat.yaml. The window is the editor's, with the controls that would change something greyed rather than hidden -- Edit and Delete on a node and in the window about the file's problems, renaming, Undo, Redo, Save, arranging, placing a node, drawing a line, every field in every window -- and nodes stay where they are when dragged. What the file came with is still checked and still said as the editor starts, in the same windows, because a configuration somebody opened to look at is one they want to know the state of; and every window that shows something can still be opened. The title bar says (read-only).

The greying is the window's half. The editor's is that a read-only session takes no edit and writes no file -- not the configuration, not the simulator's, not the history beside them -- whatever asks, and deletes no other session's history as it starts or ends. Any other word starting with a dash is refused, as a misspelled flag would otherwise be taken for the name of a configuration to create.

Nothing of it is on the canvas, and that is not a shortcut. The simulator file adds settings to payloads that are already drawn and declares groups of settings for them to share; it introduces no nodes and no connections, so a drawing of it would be the same drawing with numbers on it. What it is, is two lists of settings.

One of those lists needs no window of its own. A simulated payload's settings are about a payload that is already on the canvas, so they are in that node's own window, below a rule: a window of its own would be a second place to look for what one node says about itself. The rule is there because the fields above it and the fields below go to different files, and nothing else would say so. The settings appear only for a payload and only where the session was given the simulator's file -- a computation, a log and the ground link have nothing standing in for them.

The names are the configuration's and are never invented here: the two files are joined by name, every payload needs settings in the simulator's file, and a name there that no payload has is an error rather than a line with no effect. So a file whose payloads were renamed shows the new names with nothing in them, which is the truth about it. The groups window offers what the simulator file declares and can make and remove one, a group being that file's own.

Both files are one session. They are edited together, checked together and must agree, so one Save writes both and one Undo takes back whichever of them the last edit touched. Every setting is optional -- what a payload does not state it takes from the group it names -- so nothing in either place is marked.

make runsimguiy and make runsimguix open a configuration and the simulator file beside it, in YAML and in XML; TCSGUI_SIM names the second file and defaults to the first's stem with sim on the end, which is what the shipped sets are called. The simulator file is YAML whichever spelling the configuration is in, that being the one spelling it has.

What a node says about itself

In a window of its own, one per node, opened by clicking the node and closed by a press of the Close button.

It was a panel down the right-hand side, which showed whichever node had last been pressed. That made the commonest thing somebody filling in a configuration does -- comparing two sensors on one bus -- a matter of pressing one, reading it, pressing the other and remembering. Several windows can be open at once, so the two can be read side by side, and closing takes a press of its own: a window that went away when something else was pressed would be the panel again.

Opened by a click and not by a press, because a press is how a drag starts. A gesture that moved the node is a move and one that moved nothing is a request to see what the node says -- told apart by the distance and not by the time, since a gesture that means one thing or another depending on how long it took is a gesture that does the wrong one under pressure. A click is therefore not an edit and leaves nothing in the history.

The window holds the attributes the node's type declares, whatever else the node happens to hold, its comment, what is wrong with it if anything, and the simulator's settings for it where there are any -- those with their labels in italic, which says on every row what the rule above them says once: a reader who has scrolled past the rule still knows which file they are typing into. Removing the panel also gave the canvas the 260 pixels it was using, which the three working panes now have.

Groups are edited in a window of their own, reached from File -> Edit payload groups. A group is not on the canvas and should not be: it is a set of payloads read together, and which payloads those are is written on the nodes rather than drawn between them. The window chooses which group, states its name, how often it is read, and which payloads are in it -- and below those, the attributes that all of those payloads declare. The chooser is in the window rather than in the bar, where it was: a list of what a configuration happens to contain is data, and a control holding data belongs with the form that edits it.

Typing a value into one of those writes it to every payload in the group, as a single edit. That is what a group is for beyond the schedule: a dozen sensors on one bus are told the bus once. One edit and not a dozen, because undo has to take back the gesture rather than the first of its consequences, and the edit carries what each payload had so that undoing it puts back exactly what was there -- including the payloads that had nothing, which have the attribute taken off again rather than emptied. A field whose payloads disagree says so, because typing into it will overwrite several different values with one.

Two attributes are never propagated. group is the group itself, and interval belongs to the group rather than to its members -- a member stating one is a fault the flight reader refuses -- so it is the group's own field and goes nowhere else. Renaming a group renames it on every node in it, as one edit, for the same reason: a rename that left the members behind would silently unschedule them.

A configuration that exists in two spellings is written in both. vehicle.yaml beside vehicle.xml is one configuration written twice, and two files meant to say the same thing drift the moment only one of them is written -- the shipped example is such a pair, and keeping it a pair by hand meant a command to remember after every session. Only where the sibling is already there: writing one that is not would be the editor deciding somebody wants their configuration in two languages, which is not a thing to decide for them.

Every edit is written as it is made, so saving is not something to remember. There is a Save button all the same. It writes every file of the session now, which covers the three things the ordinary case does not: retrying a write that failed once whatever stopped it has been put right, rewriting a set of files left half new by a failure between two of them, and answering somebody who has been taught by every other program to save their work. The way out of the program does the same for anything the files do not have, and says on the error output whether it got there -- leaving a session's work in a process that is ending is the one way this editor could lose anything.

Anything that goes wrong is said twice: in the red line along the bottom of the window, and in a small window of its own that has to be acknowledged. The line is the state -- saved, or what stopped the last write -- and the window is the moment it happened. Both, because either alone is wrong: a line is easy to carry on typing past without reading, and the thing it says is the one thing in this editor that silence makes worse -- a full device stays full while every edit after it is one more the files do not have. The window says what failed, and says that the session is still open and nothing has been lost, because the useful response is to put the cause right and press Save rather than to start again.

Something wrong with the configuration goes there too, the first time it is wrong. A fault -- two nodes of one name, two lines into one input, a value a rule refuses -- is an error: tcsverify fails on it and the flight software would refuse the file. The editor said so in red inside the node it is about and nowhere else, which is a sentence in a box forty-eight pixels tall, and on a canvas somebody is not looking at. So it is said in the window as well, with a heading of its own: "Something is wrong with the configuration" rather than "Something did not work", because the second is about a command that failed and there is nothing to press Save about. The red on the node stays, and so does the sentence in that node's own window; the window is the moment it appeared.

It appears once per thing that goes wrong, not once per edit. With a directory that cannot be written every edit fails with the same sentence, and a window that came back on each of them would be a window nobody could type past; the red line goes on saying it for as long as it is true. A failure that was put right and then happened again is a new thing, and is shown again. A fault is remembered by its own sentence and forgotten when it stops being true, which is what makes "put right and made again" the same as "new". The window is made at startup and hidden rather than made when it is needed: the moment it is needed is the moment something has already failed, and a window that could not be made then would leave nothing to say it with.

Undo and redo are a drawn arrow apiece beside the name of the edit they would take back or put again. The arrow is what the button does and the name is what it will do it to, which is the half worth keeping: "Undo" on a button is a word anybody could have guessed, where move "payload" is something only this session knows. Save is a floppy disk and no word at all -- the mark every program uses for it, where the other two need their names because what they would undo is a thing only this session knows. The icons are drawn here as SVG rather than taken from an icon set, for the reason every other mark in the window is drawn from primitives: there is nothing to fail to load.

TCSGUI_FILE is the same variable the editor takes, so checking what was just edited is the same name in both commands: make runguiy and make runguix open it as YAML and as XML, one target per spelling as the runmocy and runmocx pair is, and make verify checks the file the variable names.

It prints one line per problem -- the file, the line to look at, and what is wrong -- and says nothing at all about a configuration it accepts. A configuration made of several files has each problem reported in the file it is in, which is not the file that was named on the command line.

These are the checks:

  • every name a link mentions is a node that exists, and no two nodes share a name.
  • every input of every destination is bound exactly once. None missing, none twice. A node that takes any number of them -- a log -- is exempt from the count and not from the rest: it must have at least one source, and every source must suit its format.
  • no cycle, unless a node on it declares itself a loop and states what breaks the cycle -- a control loop is a cycle on purpose, and an accidental one is a configuration that would run forever on its own output.
  • every node that is opened says what opening it takes, and says nothing its type does not know. A device on a bus needs the bus and the address, a network link the protocol and the address and the port, a set of discrete lines the chip and the lines. The rules are the ones that have been tested against real buses, reached rather than restated, so a node is refused here for exactly what the flight software would refuse it for -- and refused on a desk rather than when the vehicle tries to open it. This is also what lets the editor be lazy about what has been filled in.
  • the configuration says what tcspecial itself is configured with. The tcspecial section -- where commands are taken, where beacons go, where the telemetry log is written -- is required of every configuration, and required rather than defaulted for the reason the attributes inside it are: a configuration that has not been asked the question has not answered it. It is the one section every configuration holds and is read by the one struct, so one mistake is one complaint wherever it is written. Only the top file of a configuration may state one: a configuration has one command interpreter, and a second section would be two answers to one question with the winner decided by the order the files happened to be read in.
  • a payload that is read is written to only if it says what asks it. What is written to a payload that is read is the question it answers, and the trigger is the bytes of that question -- so a payload stating none is one the configuration describes as only read, and a link delivering into it has nowhere to go. The usual cause is a link drawn to the wrong node, which nothing else in a configuration would reveal. A payload that is written and never read is exempt: a heater, a valve or a latch is driven rather than asked, and a trigger on one would be bytes written before a read that never happens.
  • direction agrees with the type. A set of discrete lines driven as outputs is not also read; a sensor buffer is not written; a node with no outbound link produces data nobody reads and is named as such.
  • every device in a group is readable on the group's interval, and no member states an interval of its own.
  • every command binding names an attribute its node will accept, and a attribute no binding mentions cannot be set from the ground.
  • every compute node names a computation that exists and says what that computation cannot run without -- a conversion's format, scale and offset, a thermostat's setpoint and band, a program's deadline -- and nothing else. An attribute belonging to another computation, or to none, is refused by name.
  • every mean is over the inputs the links bind, and says which they are. A mean over three sensors whose third link was deleted has two inputs, each bound exactly once, and the declaration is what makes that a fault instead of a mean over two.
  • every external process has a timeout. Whether it can be executed is the vehicle's answer and not this tool's -- the program is on the vehicle -- so that one is found when the configuration is loaded aboard.
  • every source of a log produces records the log's format can hold, which for fixed: <n> means a stated read_size of exactly n from every one of them. A source whose record length the configuration does not state -- every computation -- can feed a variable-length log and not a fixed one.

Example: a cabin thermostat

Three temperature sensors of one place, two on an I2C bus and one on a SPI link, each producing a different format. All three are read together ten times a second. Each reading is converted to kelvin, and each converted reading goes to two places: down to the MOC, and into a vote. The vote takes the middle of the three and goes to the MOC as well, and into a comparison against a setpoint the ground sets. More than two degrees above it, two sets of heater lines are unasserted; more than two below, they are asserted.

The trio is the point of the example. Three sensors of one place, separated only because two parts cannot occupy one point, so that the temperature survives one of them going wrong: the third is a different part on a different bus with a format and a scale of its own, so no one bus and no one part number can take the whole trio out, and the vote leaves the odd reading out of the answer rather than averaging it in.

This is the shape of the shipped examples/thermostat. Left out for room are its tcspecial section, which every configuration must have and without which this would be refused, its log node, and the editor's layout; its addresses are its own. What is here is checked as it stands, with a tcspecial section added back.

description: Cabin thermostat

payload_groups:
  - { name: cabin_temps, interval: "100 ms" }

moc:
  - { name: moc, type: moc, address: "10.0.0.1", port: "5000",
      ground_base: "10.0.0.2:5100", payload-in: true }

vehicle:
  - { name: k_1, type: compute, function: to_kelvin, format: i16_be,
      scale: "0.0625", offset: "273.15", payload-in: true,
      payload-out: [{ target: moc }, { target: cabin_temp, input: in0 }] }
  - { name: k_2, type: compute, function: to_kelvin, format: i16_be,
      scale: "0.0625", offset: "273.15", payload-in: true,
      payload-out: [{ target: moc }, { target: cabin_temp, input: in1 }] }
  - { name: k_3, type: compute, function: to_kelvin, format: u24_be,
      scale: "0.0078", offset: "255.37", payload-in: true,
      payload-out: [{ target: moc }, { target: cabin_temp, input: in2 }] }

  - { name: cabin_temp, type: compute, function: vote, algorithm: median,
      inputs: "in0,in1,in2", payload-in: true,
      payload-out: [{ target: moc }, { target: thermostat }] }
  - { name: thermostat, type: compute, function: hysteresis,
      setpoint: "293.15", half_band: "2.0", payload-in: true,
      payload-out: [{ target: moc }],
      moc-out: [{ target: heaters_1 }, { target: heaters_2 }] }

payloads:
  - { name: temp_1, type: i2c, group: cabin_temps, path: "/dev/i2c-1",
      address: "0x48", read_size: "2", payload-out: [{ target: k_1 }] }
  - { name: temp_2, type: i2c, group: cabin_temps, path: "/dev/i2c-1",
      address: "0x49", read_size: "2", payload-out: [{ target: k_2 }] }
  - { name: temp_3, type: spi, group: cabin_temps, path: "/dev/spidev0.0",
      max_speed: "1000000", spi_mode: "0", read_size: "3",
      payload-out: [{ target: k_3 }] }

  - { name: heaters_1, type: gpio, direction: output, path: "/dev/gpiochip0",
      lines: "3,4,5", moc-in: true }
  - { name: heaters_2, type: gpio, direction: output, path: "/dev/gpiochip0",
      lines: "6,7,8", moc-in: true }

commands:
  - { from: moc, sets: thermostat.setpoint }

A line is written once, on the node it leaves, under the terminal it leaves by: payload-out on each sensor names the conversion it feeds, and the conversions name the vote's input they bind. The thermostat's decision leaves by two terminals, which is the file saying two different things with it -- its payload-out sends it to the ground as telemetry, and its moc-out drives the two heaters.

Several things in that file are refusals waiting to happen, which is the point. Drop cabin_temp.in2 from k_3's links and the configuration is refused for a vote whose declared inputs are not the ones that arrive, rather than voting over two readings and calling it a trio. Give temp_2 an interval of its own and it is refused for disagreeing with the group that says when it is read. Remove direction: output from heaters_1 and it is refused for being driven as an input -- lines with no direction are inputs, a set of discretes being more often something read, so the omission reads as though it were right and the flight software would refuse every decision for that node for as long as it ran. None of those is discovered in flight.

What the cycle costs is also visible in the file rather than in the code. The group interval paces the readings and everything downstream of them runs on arrival rather than on a clock of its own, so a tenth of a second is a tenth of a second of sensing -- and three readings a tenth of a second is three votes and three decisions, the vote producing a value on every arrival so that a sensor which stops reporting is outvoted rather than waited for. The decision is asserted to the heaters each time; what reaches the ground and the log is the transitions.

Features

  • Centralized control

    • Statistics
      • Bytes transferred and I/O operations attempted and completed
      • Maintained on global and per payload basis
    • Memory allocation complete during initialization

  • TCSpecial code is comprised of:

    • Running on spacecraft
      • tcspecial: process running on spacecraft

  • Testing software is:

    • tcsmoc: Simple interface using tcslib for visualizing tcspecial operation and doing testing.
    • tcssim: Simulated payloads

  • There are two libraries and two configuration files -- the tcspecial configuration, which holds the payload configuration section, and the simulator configuration beside it:

    • tcslib: ground software library providing simple integration with mission control software
    • tcslibgs: software library containing the commands, the telemetry, and the configuration languages -- what tcspecial and tcslib share, and what the two GUIs share with them: every configuration file format is parsed here, including the simulator's, and a time or a sample of a transfer is formatted here so that both windows show it the same way
    • the configuration: the nodes tcspecial serves, tcsmoc watches and commands, and tcssim simulates the payloads of -- one file, named on each program's command line
    • the simulator file beside it: what simulating those payloads takes, which is not part of describing them
      • Network connections support Stream and datagram
      • Network and device
        • Support for <n> interfaces on Linux
        • Network devices are specified by node and server, like getaddrinfo(), along with the other getaddrinfo() hints.
        • Other devices are specified by a pathname.
        • Streams, both network streams, and devices, have a message length and a wait interval.
      • There are as many data handlers as are defined in this file.

  • Radio interfaces

    • UDP standard

  • Straight-forward extensibility for custom payload and uplink/downlink interfaces

  • Extensible in any language: a node may be a program of its own, in whatever language the vehicle can execute, which tcspecial starts from the configuration and connects into the graph

    • App nodes: three on the vehicle's side (app_payload_veh, app_moc_veh, app_all_veh) and two that are payloads (app_pl, app_all_pl), each naming its executable
    • The interface is file descriptors carrying length-prefixed packets, so a program links against nothing of tcspecial's -- tcsapp speaks it for Rust, and examples/simple-app has a payload in Python 3 with nothing outside its standard library
    • A process computation hands what arrives to any program a line at a time and takes back what it writes
    • A capability added this way needs no change to the flight software and no rebuild of it

  • Written in Rust:

    • Provides a high degree of portability
    • Take advantage of Rust memory ownership features to eliminate many errors
    • Leverage the many features Rust has to produce clean, well documented code, with many modern features to make it easy to write code.

High-Level View of TCSpecial

Tcspecial fits into the ground and space portions of the command and telemetry systems as follows:

High-Level View of TCSpecial

       GROUND              :                   SPACE
===========================:==================================================
                           :
+=======================+  :   +===========================+   +=============+
||  Ground Ops         ||  :   ||  Flight Software        ||   || Payload Bay||
+=======================+  :   +===========================+   +=============+
|  +---------+          |  :   |  tcspecial (tcslibgs)     |   |             |
|  | Mission |          |  :   |  +---------------------+  |   |             |
|  | Control |          |  :   |  | Command interpreter |  |   |             |
|  | S/W     |          |  :   |  +---------------------+  |   |             |
|  +---------+          |  :   |  | Beacon              |  |   |             |
|       ^               |  :   |  +---------------------+  |   |             |
|       |               |  :   |  | The graph           |  |   | +---------+ |
|       v               |  :   |  |  moc nodes          |  |   | |Payload 0| |
|  +-----------------+  |  :   |  |  vehicle nodes:     |  |   | +---------+ |
|  | tcslib          |<------->|  |   compute, log,     |  |   | +---------+ |
|  | tcslib-c        |  |  :   |  |   tee, null, ping,  |  |   | |Payload 1| |
|  | python/tcslib   |  |  :   |  |   apps              |  |   | +---------+ |
|  | (tcslibgs)      |  |  :   |  |  payload nodes  <------------->   .     | |
|  +-----------------+  |  :   |  +---------------------+  |   | +---------+ |
|  |  configuration  |  |  :   |  |  configuration      |  |   | |Payload n| |
|  +-----------------+  |  :   |  +---------------------+  |   | +---------+ |
+-----------------------+  :   +---------------------------+   +-------------+

On the ground, a mission's control software (such as YAMCS or MCT) is built on tcslib -- or on tcslib-c from C and C++, or on the Python tcslib -- to send commands, receive the vehicle's data and listen for its beacons. These are shown as a single communications link, though they could be using multiple frequencies or channels. Both ends read the same configuration, and a beacon carries its digest, so each can tell when the other is reading another one.

Over IP the link is several sockets, each saying by the port it is on what it carries -- see Where the Addresses Are. Commands about the spacecraft go to the command interpreter on one port and commands about a payload on another; the command interpreter answers each. The beacon is sent on a link of its own. What the ground sends into the graph arrives at a moc node, and what leaves the graph for the ground goes from each source to the moc node's ground_base, at a port of its own. On a space link the same division is by virtual channel.

Inside tcspecial the payloads are nodes of a graph the configuration draws: a payload node is the vehicle's end of an interface to one payload, a vehicle node is something the vehicle itself does -- a computation, a log, a tee or a null node, a ping, a program of its own -- and the links between them say where each datum goes. Every node runs in the one process, so a reading reaching two places is a link with two destinations rather than two programs talking to each other. See The four concepts.

The tcslibgs library contains definitions shared between the ground portion of the software, tcslib, and the space portion, tcspecial.

Commands and Telemetry

Both command and telemetry messages are subject to loss between the sender and the receiver. Thus, commands must be idempotent, generating the same resulting state and the same telemetry response

The formats of command and telemetry messages are as given in the document:

CCSDS 732.1-B-3 Unified Space Data Link Protocol (Blue Book, June 2024)

Commands and Response Telemetry

Commands cause generation of one or more telemetry responses. Every response contains a success failure and may contain additional parameter values

Every response carries a header before whatever else it says: the sequence number of the command it answers, which type of response it is, the status, and the time the spacecraft made it. The tables below list what each type adds to that.

Requirement
A timestamp is microseconds since the UNIX epoch: one number and one unit, wherever a time is carried -- a response's header, a handler's samples and statistics, a beacon. Two ends comparing times, or a log read long afterwards, then have nothing to agree about beyond the epoch.
Requirement
On the wire a timestamp is eight bytes, least significant byte first. Little-endian because both ends run on little-endian machines, so network order would be a byte swap at each of them to agree with nothing, and because the records a payload reads are already written that way.
Requirement
All ones -- FF FF FF FF FF FF FF FF -- is the one value that is not a time: it says the spacecraft could not tell what time it was. A clock nobody has set, one set to before the epoch, and one reading so far ahead that the count will not fit are all sent as that.

The ground is told the clock failed rather than given a number, because a time nobody can vouch for looks exactly like a time somebody can. A telemetry log full of 1970 reads as data from the epoch rather than as a clock that was never set, and every interval worked out from it reads as fifty years. All ones rather than nought for the same reason the other way round: nought is a real time, the epoch itself, and a field of them is a time the ground might believe.

Requirement
Nothing computes an interval from a timestamp the clock failed on, and nothing computes one from a later time minus an earlier one that comes out negative. Both are "cannot say", and a display shows what it shows for a reading it has not got. A clock that moved is not a reason to call a payload's data current.
Requirement
A time that cannot be shown is shown as --:--:--, which is what a panel already shows for a transfer that has not happened.

Eight bytes because four will not do. Four bytes of microseconds run out 4294.967295 seconds after the epoch -- 1970-01-01 01:11:34 UTC -- so a four-byte field would have overflowed before this software was first written. Eight reach 586524-01-19 08:01:49 UTC, one microsecond short of which is the last time that is not the clock fault.

Microseconds rather than the milliseconds this was: a payload that is triggered and read a fraction of a millisecond later is two transfers a millisecond clock stamps with one time, and the ground cannot then tell which happened first. Microseconds are finer than any interval here is written in and still leave the range above.

Before the milliseconds it was a pair, seconds and nanoseconds within the second, which is two fields to carry, two to serialize and two to get wrong: arithmetic had to borrow across the second, and nothing ever read the nanoseconds.

PING

Verify that TLSpecial is able to process commands.

Name Parameters
PING_CM Name Type Description
None
PING_TM Parameters
None, beyond the header every response carries

RESTART_ARM

Enable a restart of TCSpecial for as long as the tcspecial section's restart_arm_valid says, and for twenty minutes where it says nothing.

Name Parameters
RESTART_ARM_CM Name Type Description
arm_key ArmKey Value that must match the RESTART command key
RESTART_ARM_TM Parameters
Name Type Description
None

RESTART

Restart TCSpecial if the time is within restart_arm_valid -- twenty minutes where it is not stated -- of the last RESTART_ARM command and arm_key matches the value of arm_key from the last RESTART_ARM command. Otherwise it is answered NotArmed. See Restarting for what a restart does.

Name Parameters
RESTART_CM Name Type Description
arm_key ArmKey Value that must match the RESTART_ARM command key
RESTART_TM Parameters
Name Type Description
None

The rest of these need work done, but do list the applicatable commands

Configure

Configure various TCSpecial values

Name Parameters
CONFIG_CM Name Type Description
beacon_int BeaconInterval Interval at which BEACON telemetry is sent
CONFIG_TM Parameters
Name Type Description
None
Requirement
The interval is eight bytes of microseconds, least significant byte first: the unit and the order a timestamp uses, so that a number crossing this link means one thing whichever field it is in. There is no reserved value in it -- an interval, unlike a clock, cannot fail to be read -- and nought is refused where the sender is retimed rather than meaning anything here.
Requirement
A spacecraft asked for an interval longer than eight bytes of microseconds can state uses the longest it can state. The alternative is a very long interval arriving as a very short one, which is a spacecraft beaconing as fast as it can because the ground asked it to beacon once a century. Nothing will meet the limit: eight bytes of microseconds are 584,554 years.

It is called BeaconInterval because it is a length of time and not a time of day. It was BeaconTime, which read as the time a beacon was sent, so the one thing in the system named "beacon time" was the one thing that was not a time.

Asynchronous Telemetry

Beacon

The beacon is not JSON, as the answers to commands are. It is 34 bytes in a fixed layout, stated once, in XTCE, in tcslibgs/xtce/beacon.xml, and packed and unpacked by the code tcs-xtce generates from that file: Rust in tcslibgs::beacon, which tcspecial and tcslib use, and Python in python/tcslibgs/beacon.py. tcs-xtce is vendored in vendor/tcs-xtce until it is published.

Name Parameters
BEACON Name Type Description
name 10 bytes tcspecial and a space, as text
version 3 bytes Major, minor and patch version of the tcspecial build sending it, from its Cargo.toml, a byte each
separator 1 byte A space
digest 16 bytes MD5 of the configuration file this tcspecial read
sequence 4 bytes Which beacon this is: the number of beacons this process has sent, from one, most significant byte first
Requirement
A beacon is exactly 34 bytes, in the layout above, and the ground refuses a datagram of any other length or without the name and the space where they belong, showing what arrived rather than a beacon. Something else sending to the beacon port is then seen for what it is.
Requirement
The layout is stated once, in tcslibgs/xtce/beacon.xml, and the code that packs and unpacks it, in every language, is generated from that file by make beacon-packet in tcslibgs/ and never written by hand.
Requirement
Packing a beacon allocates nothing once the packer is made: tcspecial's beacon thread makes one BeaconPacker when it starts and packs every beacon into its buffer. Unpacking one allocates three times, because tcs-xtce's Rust decoder hands back the name, the space and the digest as a String, a String and a Vec; tcslib's tests hold a beacon pass to exactly those.
Requirement
A beacon carries no time. What the ground shows is when the last one arrived, by the ground's clock: a spacecraft whose clock had stopped would otherwise look as though its beacons had.
Requirement
A beacon carries the version and the digest a CONNECT is answered with. A beacon arrives whether or not anything has connected, so a ground station that is only listening can see which software is flying and which configuration it is serving rather than having to ask first.
Requirement
Neither a beacon nor the answer to a CONNECT carries a configuration version. A configuration states none, and the version of the language this software reads, tcslibgs::graph::VERSION, is taken into the digest: two builds that read the language differently announce different digests, which is the difference a version would have told.
Requirement
The MOC shows what the last beacon said as one line, each of the two labelled: tcspecial v0.1.0 md5 and the digest's sixteen bytes in hex with nothing between them. The answer to a CONNECT is written the same way; see What Each End Read.
Requirement
Both are settled when the process starts, not read when a beacon goes out. The digest is of the file this process read, and a file edited since would otherwise have the beacons claiming a configuration nothing is serving.
Requirement
Every telemetry response carries the time the spacecraft made it, in its header. Not the time the command was received, and not the time of whatever the answer is about: a QUERY_NODE_SAMPLE carries the times its samples moved, and this is the time it was asked. The two are worth telling apart -- a sample from an hour ago in an answer made a second ago is a node that has gone quiet, and without a time on the answer there is nothing to read that against.

Two of them used to carry a time of their own, PING and the beacon, and the other eight carried none; the header carries it for all of the answers now, and a header is built one way so that a type of telemetry added later cannot be the ninth. The beacon is not an answer and has no header: see Beacon.

Requirement
Every beacon carries its own number, counted by the sender from one. A beacon answers nothing, so the number it carries is not a command's as every other telemetry's is: it says which beacon this is.

A beacon says only that the spacecraft is alive, and without a number a ground station cannot tell a beacon it missed from one the spacecraft never sent -- which are a network to look at and a spacecraft to look at. The indicator says how long ago the last beacon was; the number says how many there should have been in between.

Requirement
The count is the sender's, so it starts again at one when tcspecial does. A number that does not go forward is therefore a restart rather than a gap, and the MOC says which it was: n beacons did not arrive, or the beacons start again at #1, so tcspecial has restarted.

TCSpecial

Configuration Files

Requirement
Every configuration file may be written in YAML or XML: the command interpreter's own settings, the payload configuration beside them, and a simulator configuration alike. Both formats describe the same settings and are read into the same Rust values, so which one a file uses is a matter of local preference and tooling and never of capability.
Requirement
The format is taken from the file's extension and from nothing else. .yaml and .yml are YAML, .xml is XML, and a file whose extension is neither is refused, naming the file and what a configuration file is called.

Refused rather than assumed. JSON was read as well and was what an extensionless or unfamiliar name was taken to be, so a misspelled extension was not an error but a file parsed as the wrong language -- a .yam file "describing no payloads" rather than a file whose name was wrong. Nothing was written in JSON that was not also written in YAML.

The structs the files parse into are the single description of the data, in tcslibgs; a format is a spelling of it. Adding a third would be a variant of ConfigFormat and no second set of structs. Every shipped configuration exists in both formats, so each parser is exercised by a file someone runs rather than only by a fixture, and a test loads both of every set and compares the payloads they produce.

How the two correspond

A section is a key in YAML and an element in XML; a value is a key in YAML and a child element in XML.

Requirement
In XML every value is a child element and never an attribute. The two formats then say the same thing in the same shape, and one reader of a configuration file learns one convention.
Requirement
A sequence in XML is repeated sibling elements named for the field, and the repeats are contiguous. There is no wrapper element around a list: two payloads are two <payloads> elements, which is how a sequence reaches serde from quick-xml.
Requirement
An optional value nothing states is an omitted element, not an empty one. An empty element is a value of no characters, which is a different thing from an absent one.

A value's own syntax is the same in both -- a number may be decimal or hexadecimal, a flag is true or false, a timeout carries its unit; see Value Syntax. The YAML of a shipped set is the one to read, because it carries the comments.

(The command link carries JSON between the ground and the spacecraft. That is not configuration and is no part of this.)

tcspecial

Tcspecial reads one configuration file, named on its command line. It describes the nodes, the links between them and the payloads among them -- see Payloads -- and it states what tcspecial itself is configured with in its tcspecial section: where commands are taken, where beacons go, the beacon interval, and where the telemetry log is written.

How many of the payloads it reaches may be stand-ins is said on the command line too, by --simulate, and is not part of the configuration; see Simulation Modes.

Which payload types it can reach at all is decided when it is built; see Which Node Types Are Built.

It read two. The second was its own, tcspecial/src/tcspecial.yaml, named by TCSPECIAL_CONFIG_PATH. A configuration must have the section, so a second file could only disagree with the one being flown.

Both choose their parser from their extension, as every configuration file in the project does, so either may be written in YAML or XML; see Configuration Files. The shipped one is YAML because that is what the rest of the project's configuration is written in.

This is the tcspecial section of the shipped tests/manual/thermostat.yaml:

tcspecial:
  beacon:
    interval: "5 s"
    recv: "127.0.0.1:5650"
    send: "0.0.0.0:5550"
  log:
    directory: "/var/log/tcspecial"
    segment_size: "65536"
  payload_command:
    dest_base: "0.0.0.0:4200"
    source_base: "127.0.0.1:4300"
  payload_telemetry:
    dest_base: "127.0.0.1:6100"
    source_base: "0.0.0.0:6000"
  tcs_cmd:
    recv: "0.0.0.0:4000"
    send: "127.0.0.1:4100"

tcs_cmd.recv is where the command interpreter binds for commands about the spacecraft, which is where the ground sends them; payload_command is the two ends of the link commands about nodes take, one socket for every node; and beacon.send and beacon.recv are the two ends of the beacon link. Every address tcspecial uses is in this section and nowhere else: see Where the Addresses Are. The quotes on the addresses are deliberate: unquoted, 0.0.0.0 is a string in YAML only because it happens not to be a number, which is a thin reason to rely on. log.directory holds the telemetry log's segment files and must already exist; logging is off when it is absent. Each segment that fills is handed to segsend: see Filled segments. log.segment_size is the payload bytes in one segment file, the header being added on top of that.

Tcspecial hass a command interpreter (CI) running on the spacecraft where the payloads are located. CI has one or more threads to handle OC communications, i.e. data exchanged with tcslib over a bi-directional communication link. It defaults to using datagram communication to the tcslib, though this is usually actually a link to the spacecraft radio. The radio may use a different protocol.

Tcspecial also has threads associated with each data handler (DH). Each DH communicates to payloads via a bi-direction channel. A key feature of tcspecial is that each DH may use a different protocol to communicate with its payload. This includes not only the core communication protocols supported by the operating system, such as stream or datagram protocols, or serial or parallel interfaces, but also stackable DH protocols that can be employed to build custom protocol stacks.

EndPoints

Endpoints handle the low level I/O and each one is associated with a thread.

The EndpointWaitable trait defines wait_for_event() as a function that waits for an event to occur on a Read or Write trait, similar to the Linux poll() system call.

Derived from the EndpointWaitable trait is the trait EndpointReadable, which defines read() for reading, and EndpointWritable, which defines write() for writing. Each is used after EndpointWaitable::wait_for_event() indicates I/O of the particular type is possible on a corresponding Read or Write trait.

Requirement
Each Endpoint has an I/O file descriptor
Requirement
Each Endpoint has a command file descriptor

In order for tcspecial to notify a given endpoint that it has some action to perform, it passes it a command file descriptor, which is a pipe interface, during endpoint initialization. When it wants an endpoint to perform some action, it writes a byte to that file descriptor. By waiting on the I/O file descriptor for a read or write, and the command file descriptor, for a read, the Endpoint will know that it has a command waiting or I/O waiting, or conceivably both, by using select(), epoll(), or a similar call.

Requirement
When an Endpoint needs to perform I/O, it first uses a select()-like interface to wait for the the I/O file descriptor to become ready for a read or write, depending on the operation, and a read of the command file descriptor.
Requirement
When the select()-like operation completes, the Endpoint will first check the command file descriptor
Requirement
If the command file descriptor has data ready, the Endpoint will read one byte from that file descriptor and call the command interpreter to handle the command.
Requirement
Regardless whether the command interpreter command handle is successful or not, the Endpoint will return the the select()-like call.
Requirement
If the select()-like call indicates an operation can be performed on the I/O file descriptor and one cannot be performed on the command file descriptor, it will issue the appropriate I/O with the NO_DELAY option.
Requirement
When the I/O file descriptor operation succeeds, the Endpoint will return a status of Ok(n) where n is a u32 indicate the number of bytes transferred.
Requirement
If the I/O file descriptor operation fails, it will be repeated after a delay up to a specific number of times
Requirement
The initial value of the delay is a configurable named EndpointDelayInit
Requirement
Each time the I/O file descriptor fails, the delay is set to twice its previous value up to a configurable named EndpointDelayMax.
Requirement
If the next value of the delay reaches EndpointDelayMax, the Endpoint will exit with an appropriate Err() value.

Stream and Datagram Endpoints "^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^ Stream data flows along a communication link as one or more bytes at a time with generally irregular timing and without any error checking. Datagrams differ in that all data in a datagram is either transferred in a complete block with no errors, or it is discarded.

Datagram Endpoints

A consequence of datagrams being transferred in a single block is that select()-like calls indicate that the entire datagram is present or none is. There is no need to read a piece at a time. It is possible to read the amount of data available and allocate a bigger buffer but tcspecial does not support this capability.

Stream Endpoints

Streams do not have embedded markers to indicate data boundaries, so select()-like calls indicate only that one or more bytes are available. Transferring one byte at a time will incur a significant amount of overhead, so tcspecial can delay for some time to collect more bytes. This configurable is known as StreamEPDelay.

When the select()-like operation indicates at least one byte is available, Stream Endpoints with values of StreamEPDelay, the Stream Endpoint will pause for StreamEPDelay and only afterwards perform a non-blocking read of up to the buffer size to get the data.

Network and Device Endpoints "^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^ Endpoints may correspond to a variety of file descriptor types. In a broad sense, these are network-related and device-related. While device-related file descriptors are generally stream devices, network-related file descriptors may behave like streams or datagrams.

Network Endpoints

The table below lists network protocols supported on Linux-based systems. The protocols below are generally available, see the man page for socket(2) and other, associated, documentation. The protocol family, socket type, and protocol information is provided to the Rust Socket::new() interface as follows:

use socket2::{Domain, Type, Protocol, Socket};

pub fn new(
    domain: Domain,
    type_: Type,
    protocol: Option<Protocol>,
) -> io::Result<Socket>

What a network node takes today is one of five protocols, named by its protocol attribute: tcp, udp, unix_stream, unix_dgram and raw -- AF_INET or AF_INET6 with SOCK_STREAM or SOCK_DGRAM, AF_UNIX with the same two, and AF_INET or AF_INET6 with SOCK_RAW and the number the node's ip_protocol states. Anything else is refused where the configuration is read, naming the five (see tcslibgs::graph::reach).

Requirement
A raw node states an address and an ip_protocol from 1 to 254, and no port: an IP datagram has none, and the number and the host are what say which datagrams are the node's. 0 is refused, being no protocol on a raw socket, and so is 255, IPPROTO_RAW, which can only send. ip_protocol on any other protocol is refused, naming the protocol.
Requirement
The raw socket is connected to the node's address, whichever way the node is used: a connected raw socket is handed only its protocol's datagrams from that address and sends to it, and there is no port for either end to wait at. What a read hands on is the payload, the IPv4 header taken off in the buffer it was read into; IPv6 delivers none.
Requirement
Two raw nodes reading one protocol from one host are refused as two claims on one thing, each being handed the other's datagrams.
Requirement
Without CAP_NET_RAW the node is refused as it opens, saying that the capability is what is missing and how to give it. The simulator does not stand in for a raw node: on one host every raw socket of a protocol hears every datagram of it, its own included, so a stand-in beside tcspecial would be each end reading its own words back.

The tables below are what Linux offers beyond them: the families and types a network node could be given, and the semantics each would have, so that one added later is added knowing which of the two kinds of endpoint above it is.

In the tables, every socket type but SOCK_STREAM has datagram semantics -- SOCK_SEQPACKET and SOCK_RDM keep each message's boundaries, which is what makes them datagrams here, though both are connection-oriented or reliable as well. SOCK_STREAM has stream semantics. "yes" under protocol is a family whose sockets must be told one -- an IPPROTO_, ETH_P_, NETLINK_, CAN_ or BTPROTO_ number -- and 0 one where the default will do.

Linux Networking Families and Types

domain type protocol Stream or Datagram
AF_UNIX or AF_LOCAL SOCK_STREAM 0 stream
SOCK_DGRAM 0 datagram
SOCK_SEQPACKET 0 datagram
AF_INET SOCK_STREAM 0 stream
SOCK_DGRAM 0 datagram
SOCK_RAW yes datagram
AF_AX25 SOCK_SEQPACKET 0 datagram
SOCK_DGRAM 0 datagram
SOCK_RAW yes datagram
AF_APPLETALK SOCK_DGRAM yes datagram
SOCK_RAW yes datagram
AF_X25 SOCK_SEQPACKET 0 datagram
AF_INET6 SOCK_STREAM 0 stream
SOCK_DGRAM 0 datagram
SOCK_RAW yes datagram
AF_NETLINK SOCK_DGRAM yes datagram
SOCK_RAW yes datagram
AF_PACKET SOCK_DGRAM yes datagram
SOCK_RAW yes datagram
AF_RDS SOCK_SEQPACKET 0 datagram
AF_LLC SOCK_STREAM 0 stream
SOCK_DGRAM 0 datagram
AF_CAN SOCK_RAW yes datagram
SOCK_DGRAM yes datagram
AF_TIPC SOCK_STREAM 0 stream
SOCK_SEQPACKET 0 datagram
SOCK_RDM 0 datagram
SOCK_DGRAM 0 datagram
AF_BLUETOOTH SOCK_STREAM yes stream
SOCK_SEQPACKET yes datagram
SOCK_DGRAM yes datagram
SOCK_RAW yes datagram
AF_VSOCK SOCK_STREAM 0 stream
SOCK_DGRAM 0 datagram
SOCK_SEQPACKET 0 datagram

Which protocol a type is given where the family needs one: AF_CAN's SOCK_RAW is CAN_RAW and its SOCK_DGRAM is CAN_BCM, CAN_ISOTP or CAN_J1939 -- though a CAN bus has a node type of its own, can, and is reached through that; AF_BLUETOOTH's SOCK_STREAM is RFCOMM, its SOCK_SEQPACKET L2CAP, SCO or ISO, its SOCK_DGRAM connectionless L2CAP and its SOCK_RAW HCI.

Unsupported Domains

Families that carry nothing a payload could send, or are gone from Linux, and which a network node will not be given.

domain why
AF_IPX removed from Linux in 4.18
AF_DECnet removed from Linux in 6.1
AF_KEY SOCK_RAW with PF_KEY_V2: IPsec key management, not a channel for data
AF_PPPOX sets up a PPP channel -- PPPoE, L2TP -- whose data then moves through /dev/ppp, not the socket
AF_IB an address family for the RDMA connection manager; Linux makes no socket of it
AF_MPLS configured over netlink; Linux makes no socket of it
AF_ALG SOCK_SEQPACKET: the kernel's cryptography, not a channel for data
AF_XDP SOCK_RAW: packets pass through rings shared with the kernel, not through reads and writes
AF_KCM SOCK_DGRAM or SOCK_SEQPACKET: messages framed by a BPF program over TCP connections it is handed, which is framing this software does itself

Protocol support may require configuring the Linux kernel to include protocol drivers. Of course, the hardware supporting the protocol must also be present. For more information on each of the address families, consult the Linux man page address_families(7).

Requirement
Tcspecial and tclib must translate the operating system dependent values, such as the address families, types, and protocols, to canonical values, and back again to avoid building in operating system dependent code. All values transmitted must use the canonical values.

Endpoints Opened Through Device Files "^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^ Most of the endpoint types are opened through an entry in /dev. Which type an entry is depends on what is at the end of it, and not on the entry's name: the name says where to look, and the type says what else must be said about it and which implementation opens it. The two that are not device files at all are network, which is a socket at an address, and the experimental can, which is a socket on an interface.

Endpoints Opened Through Device Files

Name What is there Endpoint type
/dev/ttyS0 Serial port, RS-232 or RS-422 serial: the line's rate and framing are configured with it
/dev/ttyUSB0 USB serial adapter, to RS-232 or RS-422 serial, as above
/dev/i2c-2 I2C bus, with several devices addressed on it i2c: the endpoint carries the address too
/dev/spidev0.1 SPI peripheral, bus and chip select named together spi: the clock, mode and word are configured with it
/dev/urandom A character device read as it comes device-file: only the path is needed

A device-file endpoint is the last of these and only that: a file opened and read as it comes. The other three are device files too, and were device endpoints until each was given a type of its own, which is what let their terms reach the handler that opens them.

Which Node Types Are Built

Every node type was built into every tcspecial, which assumed that every platform offers every interface: a GPIO character device, an IIO buffer, an rpmsg channel, SocketCAN. Not every platform does, and a vehicle that flies a serial line and an I2C bus has no use for the rest. So which payload types a tcspecial contains is chosen when it is built, and a configuration is held to what was chosen.

Requirement
Each payload type is a Cargo feature of the tcspecial crate, declared in tcspecial/Cargo.toml and named as the type is written in a configuration: device-file, serial, i2c, spi, can, iio, rpmsg, gpio and hdlc, with raw for the raw protocol of the network type. One word for a type in the configuration, the build and the refusal means none of them has to be translated into another. The words are tcslibgs::graph::built::OPTIONAL_TYPES, and a test holds the features in Cargo.toml to them.
Requirement
A feature includes or leaves out that type's module in tcspecial::node::payload and nothing else. The rules for the type in tcslibgs::node are built whatever tcspecial is built with: tcsverify and tcsgui run on the ground and check configurations for any vehicle, so what a configuration may say about a type cannot depend on how one vehicle's tcspecial was built. Tcssim is built with every type, since it runs on a development host and not on the target.
Requirement
Some of tcspecial is in every build. The network type over UDP, TCP and Unix-domain sockets is, because the link to the ground is UDP and a tcspecial that could not reach the ground would be no tcspecial at all; so are the nodes that reach no interface -- computations, logs, tees, null nodes, the ping node and the app nodes -- which run wherever tcspecial does.
Requirement

The default features are every type. cargo build --workspace, cargo test and the programs started by tcsmoc are then what they were before the types were made optional, and a build for a target says what it flies:

cargo build -p tcspecial --release --no-default-features \
    --features serial,i2c
Requirement
The types a build contains are listed in one place, made from those features: tcspecial::node::payload::BUILT. The open that reaches a payload, the check made as a configuration is loaded and the startup summary all read that list, so the three cannot disagree about what was built.
Requirement

A configuration with a node of a type the build does not contain is refused as it is loaded, before anything is opened, naming the node, its type and the feature that would build it. For example:

"temp_1" is an i2c node, and the tcspecial it is for is built without
i2c: build tcspecial with --features i2c, or take the node out of the
configuration

The rule and its sentence are tcslibgs::graph::built, which tcspecial asks through tcsverify::verify_built with the types it contains. An open of a type the build lacks says the same, though the load means none is ever reached.

A configuration refused on the ground costs a rebuild, where one that reached a node it could not open would have failed in flight.

Requirement
A type the platform cannot have is a compile error. GPIO character devices, IIO, rpmsg, spidev, i2c-dev, SocketCAN and generic HDLC are Linux interfaces, and enabling one of those features for another operating system stops the build at a compile_error! naming the type and the platform. A feature says what was chosen and the platform says what is possible, and a build that contained a type its platform lacked would fail only when a node of it was opened.
Requirement
Tcspecial says which types it contains. tcspecial --types prints them on one line, separated by commas, and exits; the startup summary says them as payload types built:, before the configuration is checked, so that a node refused for its type is read beside the list. What a build holds is then read off the binary that will fly rather than recalled from how it was built.
Requirement

Tcsverify can hold a configuration to a build: --types=serial,i2c checks it as a tcspecial containing only those types would, and reports each node of another type as that tcspecial would refuse it, in the same words. A configuration can then be checked against the vehicle it is for on the ground, without the vehicle. The words are the ones the features are named by, in the form tcspecial --types prints, so that one can be handed to the other:

tcsverify --types=$(tcspecial --types) x.yaml

A word that names no type is refused, listing those that do. Without --types, every type is taken to be built.

Requirement
A crate that uses tcspecial as a library depends on it with default-features = false and names the types it uses; tcsmoc uses none. Cargo turns on, for a crate built in one command, every feature any crate in that command asks for, so tcsmoc depending on tcspecial with its defaults would put every type back into a cargo build that named both. A build for a target is therefore made with -p tcspecial, and nothing in the workspace is to depend on a type it does not use.
Requirement
make check runs clippy over tcspecial with no optional types and with each type alone, taking the types from tcspecial --types of a build with the defaults rather than from a list of its own. A gate that leaves a reference to a type's module outside it -- the open in endpoint.rs naming the I2C endpoint whatever was built, say -- breaks only the builds without that type, which a workspace built with every type never makes.

Lunar Lander Payload Interfaces

The payloads tcspecial is for are the kind the Moon's landers were meant to carry, have carried, or are meant to carry, so the interfaces those landers give a payload are the interfaces tcspecial has to speak. This table is every such interface that a lander's published description states, whether tcspecial supports it, what Linux offers for it, and which landers offer it. Landers are named by series: Apollo 11 to 17 are "Apollo", Peregrine and Griffin are "Astrobotic".

interface tcspecial Linux driver landers
RS-422 asynchronous serial supported: serial built in: a UART behind an RS-422 transceiver is an ordinary tty Astrobotic, Chang'e, Intuitive Machines, Masten
RS-422 carrying SLIP and UDP supported: network, over UDP, with the line made an IP interface by slattach built in: SLIP Astrobotic
SpaceWire not supported not built in. Open source for Frontgrade Gaisler's GRSPW cores; for a fee from STAR-Dundee for its PCIe and USB bricks Astrobotic, Intuitive Machines, Masten
Ethernet supported: network built in Intuitive Machines
802.11 wireless LAN, to a payload deployed from the lander supported: network, once the link is up built in Astrobotic, Firefly
CAN supported: can built in: SocketCAN Intuitive Machines
MIL-STD-1553B not supported not built in; for a fee from the card vendors -- Data Device Corporation, Alta Data Technologies Chang'e
Time at the tone (a timing signal and the time it marks) not supported: the edge can be watched as gpio, but nothing sets a clock or stamps data by it built in: the PPS subsystem, pps-gpio Astrobotic
Analog telemetry channels partly: iio, where the converter is an IIO device built in: IIO, for the converters it has drivers for Apollo, Luna, Surveyor
Discrete commands and status lines supported: gpio built in: the GPIO character device Apollo, Luna, Surveyor
Serial data clocked out by the lander partly: spi, which clocks the same way and is a different protocol built in: spidev Apollo
Payload power switched by the lander, and release signals not supported: a service of the lander's, commanded through it rather than a link to the payload not applicable Astrobotic, Firefly. Intuitive Machines and Masten state 28 V power to payloads and not how it is switched

What is not in the table is not known to be absent. Blue Origin's Blue Moon, the Chandrayaan landers, Beresheet, SLIM, ispace's landers, Luna 25 and ESA's planned Argonaut publish no payload interface in a form found for this table: ispace and Intuitive Machines give their full payload user's guides on request. Firefly's public guide to Blue Ghost states the Wi-Fi it gives a deployed payload and not its wired interface. The historic landers' rows are from how their experiments were connected -- Apollo's ALSEP experiments to its central station by analog channels, discrete commands and a bit stream the station clocked out -- rather than from a payload user's guide, which they did not have.

The sources: Astrobotic's Lunar Landers Payload User's Guide (2021), for Peregrine and Griffin; Masten's Lunar Delivery Service Payload User's Guide (Rev 1.0, 2019), for XL-1; Firefly's Blue Ghost Lunar Lander Payload User's Guide (condensed, 2021); Intuitive Machines and Space Applications' Lunar Payload Delivery & Mobility Service (ESA BSGN, 2024); the Chang'e-4 lander's payload electrical control box test system (Chinese Journal of Space Science, 2021); and the Apollo 12 ALSEP Systems Handbook.

The gaps that matter most are SpaceWire, which three landers offer and Linux has no driver for, and time at the tone, which a payload that stamps its data needs. A new interface is a new node type, and its stand-in is a UDP stand_in where the operating system has nothing to stand in for it: see Saying Which Payloads Are Simulated.

The Visual Window

A configuration is a drawing in tcsgui and a running process in tcspecial, and nothing showed the one as the other: what a vehicle on a desk was doing could be read only from its logs and from the ground station's panels. The visual window draws the running graph as the editor draws the configuration and lights each link as data crosses it.

Requirement
tcspecial --visual opens the window, in a tcspecial built with the Cargo feature visual. A tcspecial built without it refuses the flag before anything is read or opened, saying that the window is only in a build with the feature and how to make one. The feature is not a default and is never in a build that flies: it brings a window toolkit into the flight software, and a build that has it says so as a warning every time it starts, window or not.
Requirement
--visual is refused under --simulate=none, in every build and before anything is read or opened, saying to give --simulate=some or --simulate=all with it. none is the flight setting and what a run that leaves --simulate out gets (see Simulation Modes), so a window there would be a window opened in flight; a run with no stand-ins that is not flight is a mistake about which run it is. The refusal comes before the build's own, so that what a flight launch meets does not depend on how the binary was built.
Requirement
The window shows the three working panes as tcsgui shows them, with every node of the top file where tcsgui would place it and every link along the route tcsgui gives it. Where the panes are divided is the person's own tcsgui setting, read and never written, and the nodes are settled into their panes by the rule the editor settles a file it opens with. A line to a node in an included file is drawn to a stub, as the editor draws it.
Requirement
The drawing is one drawing. What a node, a link and the sheet look like -- the shapes, the routes, the colours, the panes -- is the crate tcsdiagram, Rust that produces paths, sizes and colours and the markup tcsdiagram/ui/diagram.slint that paints them, and both tcsgui and tcspecial draw with it. Two copies of the drawing would be two answers to where a node is.
Requirement
A link is lit while data crosses it. Each datum sent over a link draws, under the link's ordinary line, a second line three times as wide and a shade lighter than the ordinary line's colour -- half way to white, so the colour still says which way the data is travelling -- and the wide line is taken away LINE_BLINK after the datum was sent: 0.25 seconds, tcspecial::visual::LINE_BLINK. The ordinary line is drawn whether or not the wide one is, on top of it, exactly as tcsgui draws it.
Requirement
The window takes no press: a node is not dragged, a terminal draws no link, a menu does not open. It keeps what the window manager gives every window -- raising, lowering and closing -- and closing it stops tcspecial, as an interrupt would: the pass finished, the links closed, the log flushed. A tcspecial that stops, or that never starts, closes the window.
Requirement
What the vehicle does to be shown is one atomic store per datum sent, into a slot of a table made before the vehicle starts. It allocates nothing, takes no lock and waits for nothing, so a window that is slow to draw cannot slow the vehicle. The window runs on the main thread, which a window has to, and the vehicle on a thread of its own beside it; the window looks at the table every 40 milliseconds, well inside LINE_BLINK. A delivery says which line of the drawing it travelled by the line's place among the configuration's links counted one per destination in the file's order, which is the order tcsgui draws them in.

tcslib

The TCSpecial library has a set of operations for global control and status and a set of per-payload interface operations. It is used for building control applications using mission control software such as YAMCS or MCT.

Those operations are tcslib::client::TcsClient. A control application drives one of those rather than speaking the command and telemetry protocol itself: each operation sends its command, waits for the answering telemetry, and hands back what that telemetry carried. The spacecraft's own operations are PING, CONNECT, RESTART_ARM, RESTART and CONFIG; the per-node ones are START_NODE, STOP_NODE, SET_NODE, QUERY_NODE and QUERY_NODE_SAMPLE.

Requirement
An operation that is not answered within the client's timeout is an error. A control application on a link that has gone quiet is told so rather than left waiting.

The transport is a Connection. The link to the CI is UDP, so a client is built on a UdpConnection; the trait is what lets the client's tests stand a canned spacecraft in its place. Tcsmoc is built on this client, which is why it is here rather than in tcsmoc: a second copy of it would be a second thing to keep correct, and the one in tcsmoc was the only one that existed.

tcslibgs

The TCSpecial ground/space library contains definitions used by both tcslib and tcspecial.

What Each End Read

Requirement
A link begins with each end saying what it read. The ground sends a CONNECT carrying its software version and a digest of its configuration, and the spacecraft answers with its own.
Requirement
The version is three bytes -- major, minor and patch, each in binary -- and the digest is an MD5.
Requirement
The answer is shown as one line, each of the two labelled: tcspecial v0.1.0 md5 and the digest in hex. The same two facts a beacon carries, written the same way by the same function, because the two are read against each other.
Requirement
The ground compares and the spacecraft says. Tcsmoc is the end that knows which file it read and can name it, so it reports the difference; tcspecial answers with what it read and logs a disagreement it can see.
Requirement
Each end says its own version and the digest before it makes the socket the link runs over. A socket that cannot be made, or a far end that is not answering, takes the exchange with it -- and that is exactly when those facts are wanted. In tcspecial this also puts an address already in use under the configuration it was going to serve rather than instead of it.
Requirement

The two ends write that line the same way, each naming itself:

tcspecial v0.1.0 md5 8d908f54a3d72d0704213113b92d958b

Two ends reading one configuration therefore print the same line twice, which is what makes a difference in any part of it worth reading. The MOC's line used to be written differently -- Version 0.1.0 and a bare md5 -- so the two could only be compared by the one fact they spelled alike.

Nothing else in the protocol makes the two ends prove they are talking about the same configuration, and when they were not, the only sign was a command answered NotFound for a payload the operator could see on the screen: a tcspecial left running from an earlier set, which tcsmoc attaches to rather than replacing. The two ends also say what they read on their own consoles as they start, so the question can be answered without a link at all.

Requirement
The digest is of what a file says, not of its bytes. Two ends reading different spellings of one configuration have read the same thing and must agree, so the two formats a set is written in digest alike, and so do two files differing only in comments, indentation, the order of their sections or the order of one payload's attributes.
Requirement
The order of the payloads does change the digest. Ids will be assigned in the order the payloads appear, so that order is configuration rather than spelling.
Requirement
Each payload is given a sequence number as the file is read: the first is 0. A file may not state it -- the number is what this program read, not something a file gets to assert.
Requirement
The digest takes the payloads in order of their sequence numbers. Two ends that read the same file then agree about which came first whatever either has since done with its own list.

The numbering rests on one assumption: that a parser hands entries to the code above it in the order the file gave them. Both formats here do, and a format that did not would be unusable for a language whose payload order means something.

The payloads are numbered as the list is deserialized, which is the one place every format arrives through, so nothing can hold a configuration whose payloads do not know which ones they are. A file stating a number of its own is refused by name.

Carried by the data rather than left to the order a list happens to keep. The order used to live only in that, so anything sorting the payloads -- for a lookup, for a display -- would have changed what the configuration said about which payload came first, and the only thing in the way was that nothing did it yet. The two halves of the check are then independent: walking by sequence number is what makes a list in some other order digest the same, and hashing the numbers themselves is what makes a file in some other order digest differently.

A sequence number and not a line number. A line is not something these parsers hand to the code that builds a document, and it would be a different number in each of the two formats one set is written in, where the sequence number is the same in both -- which is what lets them digest alike.

How it is reached: a file is parsed into the document it describes and that document is written back out as a tree of values, which is where the format stops showing through -- a number is a number rather than the text that spelled it, a list is a list rather than XML's repeated sibling elements, and an attribute nobody stated is absent rather than missing. The tree is then written to bytes with every map's keys in order and every list in the order it was given, and those bytes are what the MD5 is of.

A map's keys are put in order twice over, deliberately.
