The document is a set of Rust structs, and a struct writes its fields in the order it declares them however the file gave them, so the order a file used is already gone; sorting is what keeps the rule true of anything that is a map rather than a struct. What a file's own order does reach the digest through is the sequence number each payload was given as it was read, which is the one ordering that means something.

Payloads

The payloads section of a configuration describes the payloads themselves: which there are, how tcspecial reaches each one, and what one read of it asks for. What it deliberately does not describe is how a simulated payload behaves; see Simulator Configuration Files for that.

This chapter is about reaching a payload, which is the same question whatever draws the rest of the configuration: a line, a bus, a peripheral, a socket and a set of discrete lines each have terms of their own, and each is checked against the rules of its kind. Where those terms are written is in The Node Architecture; what they mean is here.

A payload is a node, and What a device node says is the section a reader of a configuration wants beside this one.

How a Payload Is Made to Send

Payloads are generally one of two types, and which type a payload is decides where the timing of it is written down.

Requirement
A payload says which type it is by stating a trigger or by stating none. There is no mode attribute: it said in a word what the trigger already says, so a file could state periodic and then give a trigger, and something had to decide which half of it to believe. A file stating one is refused by name and told that the trigger is what says it.
Requirement
A periodic payload sends of its own accord and tcspecial reads what arrives. It states no trigger and no interval in the payload configuration: there is nothing to send it, and how fast a simulated one produces data is a property of the simulation, stated in the simulator configuration file.
Requirement
A triggered payload answers a request. It states the trigger to send, which is flight behaviour: tcspecial does the sending, and what to send comes from the payload's interface document. Its simulator configuration states no interval at all, packet or segment, and takes none from the group it names: it sends when it is asked, so a rate there would govern nothing and the segments of one answer go as fast as they can.
Requirement
How often a triggered payload is asked is the interval of the payload group it is in, or, for a payload in no group, its own trigger_interval. Payloads read on one schedule are what a group is, so a payload in a group that states a trigger_interval beside it is refused, and told that the rate belongs to its group.
Requirement
A name this language no longer has is refused with the name it has now, rather than reported as a word nothing knows. The value commonly changes with the name -- a bare count became a length of time -- so a reader told only "unknown" would write the number again under the new name and be wrong by whatever the unit was.

The two attributes are exclusive, and a file giving both is reported rather than run: either guess about what was meant would operate the payload in a way nobody asked for. The exclusion is also structural in the Rust the file parses into -- a periodic handler has nowhere to put a trigger -- so it cannot be lost downstream of the check.

Requirement
A datagram payload may be triggered. One that is asked is spoken to first, so the vehicle sends its trigger to the address the node states and reads the answer on a socket of its own, as A payload that is spoken to first says. There is somewhere to send a trigger before the payload has said anything, which there would not be were the vehicle to wait at that address to learn where the payload is.

A trigger is bytes rather than text: a payload's interface document asks for what it asks for, and that is as likely to be 55 as "READ\r".

Requirement
A trigger is written as one or more parts, one after another, and is the bytes the parts make together. A part is a string in C's notation, in quotes, or bytes in hexadecimal, in square brackets: "READ\r", [55 AA], "AT" [0D 0A]. Parts may stand with space between them or none. One notation for text and one for bytes, and either may say any run of bytes, so a value whose bytes are mostly text and partly not is written as what each part is.
Requirement
Inside brackets each byte is one or two hexadecimal digits, in either case, with space between one byte and the next: 5 and 05 are the same byte, a datasheet writing either. A run of digits longer than two is refused rather than split -- [55AA] is not two bytes -- so that how many bytes a value means never depends on counting its digits. There is no 0x inside brackets: everything there is hexadecimal, and a prefix saying so would be saying it for a notation that has no alternative.
Requirement
Which notation a part is in follows from its first character and nothing else: a quote opens text and a bracket opens bytes. 52 is a plausible byte and a plausible pair of digits of something longer, so something has to say which, and a file that meant one and got the other would send something nobody asked for. Anything else where a part should begin is refused, naming the character.
Requirement
A value written bare -- 55, 0x55, 55 AA, which is how one byte was written before brackets -- is refused, and told the brackets it wants: [55]. A terminator was a byte in decimal unless it said 0x, and is told the byte it meant: 13 is [0D].
Requirement
The marks are part of the value rather than part of the way the file is written. So a YAML file writes them inside its own quotes -- '"READ\r"', '[55]' -- and an XML one as <trigger>&quot;READ\r&quot;</trigger> or <trigger>[55]</trigger>. A quote or a bracket that opens and does not close is refused.
Requirement
A C string's escapes are C's own -- \\ \" \' \n \r \t \0 \a \b \f \v and \xNN -- and an escape C does not have is refused rather than passed through. A file that wrote \q meant something, and a backslash and a q is unlikely to be it. This is also what lets an XML configuration state a trigger that a YAML one states with the format's own escapes.
Requirement
A trigger is one byte or more. A payload that answers requests has to be asked something, and nought bytes asks nothing.
Requirement
A trigger's bytes go out as the file wrote them, and are shown as a file could have written them: text as a C string, and anything else as bytes in brackets. See tcslibgs::trigger, which is the one reader of the notation for triggers, inits and terminators alike.
Requirement
Tcspecial sends a trigger on the conduit that writes the payload, and sends the first one as soon as the handler starts. A payload that answers requests has nothing to say until it has been asked, so waiting out an interval first would be a handler that begins by doing nothing.
Requirement
Triggers are counted separately from the data a handler carries. A handler's received and sent are the ground's data in each direction, so the payload side of each conduit is deliberately left out of both -- those are the same bytes seen twice -- and a trigger is a write with no read behind it. Without a count of its own it would appear in no statistic, and a payload that had stopped answering would look exactly like a handler that had stopped asking.
Requirement
A handler goes on asking its payload however the asking goes. A trigger that will not go is the payload's silence and not the handler's: the payload may come back, and a node is stopped by a STOP_NODE rather than by a payload that has gone away. It used to give up three different ways -- the write failing, the payload's end of a stream reading as end of file, and the wait on the endpoint reporting POLLERR or POLLHUP -- and in two of them it spun through the interval it was no longer asking in.
Requirement
Triggers that failed are counted apart from triggers that were taken, and apart from the writes that carry data to the ground. A payload that is answering, one that has gone away, and a handler that has given up asking are three states, and one count of attempts could not tell them apart. A failed write to the ground is data the ground did not get; a failed trigger is a payload that would not take a request.
Requirement
A conduit with nothing to read and nothing to wait on pauses before going round again, and spends the pause watching its command pipe rather than asleep. An endpoint in error or at its end is ready the instant it is polled and yields nothing every time, so a conduit that only went round held a core down for as long as its handler ran; and one that slept the pause out would answer a STOP_NODE only when it woke. A conduit that asks pauses until its next trigger is due, so the asking keeps its interval.

The format has no packet interval. How fast a payload produces packets is a property of a simulation rather than of the payload, and belongs to a simulator configuration file.

Requirement
A tcspecial configuration file states no packet interval, and one stated here is an error naming the file it belongs in. How fast a payload sends is a property of the simulation, so a tcspecial configuration that states an interval is describing a simulation. It was ignored for a while, which was the same silence the mode rules exist to prevent: a file that states an interval has said something about the payload's timing, and reading it as though it had not is reading a different file than the one that was written. An interval a payload takes from its group is refused as one it states itself is.
Requirement
A name belongs to a data handler and never to a group. It is what tells one handler of a group from another, and what every command about a handler addresses it by.
Requirement
A data handler has no number. It was addressed by a dh_id the file stated and found by its name, which meant a file could state two identities for one payload and keep them consistent by hand -- and a command naming an id the ground had and the spacecraft had not was reported against a number nobody reading the file would recognise. The name was already required, already unique and already what the panels and the simulator configuration use, so it is the identity.
Requirement
A data handler takes each attribute it does not state from the group it names, and overrides any the group does state. A group therefore holds what its handlers share without keeping one of them from differing.
Requirement
A data handler has a type and a packet size, from itself or from its group. A handler with no packet size is an error rather than a handler whose packets are zero bytes long.
Requirement
A data handler names a group the file defines. A name matching no group is an error, because the handler would otherwise be left with none of the attributes the group was to supply, which is a more confusing failure than the misspelling that really caused it.
Requirement
A group name is defined once in a file. Two groups sharing a name is an error rather than one silently shadowing the other.
Requirement
A payload name is defined once in a file. It picks out one payload, and a repeat parses cleanly and then loses one: tcspecial keeps its handlers by name, so a repeated name has one silently replace the other, and a simulator configuration is joined to the payloads by name, so it would drive two payloads from one entry.
Requirement
Each of these names is unique within its file and nowhere wider. Two payload sets are two descriptions of what a mission flies, and a set that had to rename its payloads because another set in the same directory had used the names would be renaming them for no reason: a set is read on its own, with the simulator file beside it, and nothing compares the names in one file with the names in another. The shipped sets rely on it: set 3 is set 2's first payload over a stream and carries the same name, auto-send.
Requirement
An attribute of another type of payload is an error. A device file payload stating a protocol, an address or a port, or a network payload stating a path, has written something nothing will read -- and a payload that looks configured and is not is worse than one that is refused. A link's own terms are refused the same way, by the rules for its type: see Link Terms.
Requirement
The check is made after a group has been laid under a payload, because an attribute inherited from a group reaches the payload exactly as one it states itself does. A payload of one type joining a group written for another is the case this catches.
Requirement
A word that is not an attribute is an error. A misspelled attribute is otherwise ignored, which is silence about something the file plainly meant: a payload whose oc_address is misspelled has nowhere to send its data and nothing says so. packet_interval on a payload and mode are the words this language still knows without accepting: each is named so that a file stating one is told where that figure belongs, or what says what it said, rather than told the word is unknown.
Requirement

No two nodes want the same thing. Two at one address cannot both be started; two on one device file, one serial line or one SPI peripheral is worse, each taking part of the one stream and reporting it as though it were the whole. Every node that is opened is compared with every other -- the payloads and the MOC node, a port being a port whatever claims it -- and the node whose claim came second is the one named, since the first claim is the one that stands and a reader given both names has to change one of them. The claim is per kind, because what a kind shares by design differs: a path is claimed whole, an I2C bus is shared and an address on it is not, a CAN interface is shared and an identifier on it is not, and a GPIO chip is shared while a line of it is given to one request at a time.

An app node is not compared, having no interface to claim: a payload that is a program is reached by running it, which is why it names a program and no bus. A node whose terms will not parse is left out as well, so that the complaint about what is actually wrong with it is not buried under a second one built on half-read terms.

Requirement
Hosts that certainly mean this host -- localhost, 127.0.0.1 and ::1 -- are one host, so naming a port's host differently does not make it a different port. Two names for some other host are left as written: resolving them is a question for the network rather than for a file.
Requirement
A Unix socket collides by its path. It is named by a file rather than by a port, so its port means nothing here, and a device handler opening that same path is the same collision from the other side. Every path is one type of claim for that reason, whichever type of handler opens it.
Requirement
A bus is shared and a place on it is not. Several devices on one I2C bus is what a bus is for, so a claim is on the bus and the address together rather than on the bus device.
Requirement
The rule is applied in one place, over the handlers a file produced rather than over the words that produced them. The hazard belongs to the handlers: two of them opening one device file collide whatever either was called in the file.
Requirement
A group is named by a data handler. A group no handler names is an error rather than a section with no effect, because that is also what a group whose name a handler misspelled looks like.

A misspelled group name leaves the group unnamed and the name undefined at once. The handler's end is where the misspelling actually is, so that is the error reported.

Link Terms

Three types of payload are on a link of their own -- a serial line, a device on an I2C bus, a peripheral on a SPI bus -- and each has terms that say how that link is driven: a data rate, an address on a bus, the shape of a clock. A node states them among its attributes, beside everything else it states.

The rules are in tcslibgs::endpoint_config and the per-type modules beside it, and are asked of a node by graph::reach, through types::kind_of_terms.

Requirement
A term that belongs to another type of link is an error rather than something ignored, so that a misspelled or misplaced term is reported where it was written. pec on a line, cs_active on a bus, and datarate on a peripheral are each refused.
Requirement

A payload documents and takes only keywords whose values it can set through the interface it uses. A value a file states and nothing applies is worse than no keyword at all: the file looks configured, a reader believes what it says, and the equipment is doing something else. Four words went for that reason -- bus_speed, bitrate, channels and sample_rate. Each said something true about the equipment and none of them could be set by a program that opens the interface: a bus clock belongs to the controller the platform configures, a CAN bitrate to the interface as it is brought up, and a sensor's channels and rate to the sysfs entries whoever brought the sensor up wrote. A file that states one is refused by name and told why, as a file writing any other name this language has dropped is told what to say instead.

A synchronous serial line keeps it too, through the interface the kernel gives such a line rather than through a device file: standard, clock_type, clock_rate, loopback, encoding and frame_check are every field of the two structures SIOCWANDEV takes for a raw HDLC line, and each is set and read back. See HDLC Payloads: a Synchronous Line.

Requirement
A term of a link is refused on the two types that are not on one: a network address and a plain device file. Every other type refuses what belongs to another type as its own rules are asked, and these two have no rules to ask -- so a term written on one of them was read and dropped. A device file payload could state a data rate, or a sensor's channels, and be read as a device file payload that had said nothing of the sort: a misspelling that configured nothing and reported nothing. A network payload's protocol is a term of its own type and is allowed.
Requirement
Such a term is refused whether the payload stated it or took it from its group, and the error says which. The two are fixed differently: a line is deleted from the payload, or a payload is in a group that was not written for it.
Requirement
A term a payload does not state is taken from the group it names, exactly as every other attribute of a payload is. A term reaches the link the same way whichever of the two stated it, so both are checked the same way.
Requirement
The SPI mode is written spi_mode. A payload already has a mode -- whether it sends of its own accord or on a trigger -- and one word cannot mean both.

What Is Experimental

Some of what follows is experimental and not yet supported: RS-485 on a serial line, and the can, iio, rpmsg, gpio and hdlc types of payload. Each is written, parses, reaches a handler and has tests of its rules; none has been run against the hardware it describes, and no shipped configuration uses one. hdlc is the furthest out of them: every other has something in the kernel to be exercised against, and a synchronous line has nothing but a card.

The requirements for them are written the way every other requirement here is, because that is what they are for: a statement of what the configuration will be, settled before there is equipment to try it against. They may change, where the five supported types -- network, device-file, serial, i2c and spi -- will not.

GPIO Payloads: Discrete Lines

Experimental, not yet supported.

Not every payload is a stream of data. A deployment switch, an arming line, a thermostat's output: each is one wire that is high or low, and what the ground wants to know is when it changed. Linux offers those through a chip's character device, where a program asks for a set of lines with edge detection and then reads events.

The rules are in tcslibgs::endpoint_config_gpio, the endpoint in tcspecial::endpoint_gpio and the stand-in in tcssim::payload_gpio.

Requirement
A GPIO payload states a path and the lines it watches, and may state the edge, the bias, a debounce and a state_interval. Offsets are separated by commas or spaces and written in decimal or hexadecimal, because a file written for a board copies them out of a schematic in whichever form the schematic used.

Two ways of watching, and a set of lines may ask for either or both

edge says when a line changed; state_interval says what the lines are, every so often. They answer different questions, so a payload may ask for one, the other, or both:

edge: both                         changes, which is the default
edge: rising                       changes, one way only
state_interval: 5 s                what they are, every five seconds
edge: both, state_interval: 5 s    both
edge: none                         refused: nothing would be sent
Requirement
Edges are the default. A payload that says nothing about watching is watched for both of them, which is what every file written before the report existed asked for and still means.
Requirement
A report is made with one ioctl on the line request the payload already holds -- GPIO_V2_LINE_GET_VALUES_IOCTL -- and not with a second request. The kernel gives a line to one request at a time, so a second request for the same lines is a payload that could not start.
Requirement
A report makes the same sixteen-byte record an event does, one per line. The ground reads the two alike, which is the point: both say that this line had this value at this time. The difference is whose time it is -- an event carries the moment the line changed, a report the moment the vehicle looked -- and anything measuring how long an edge took to arrive reads the events.
Requirement
The report exists for the two things edges cannot do. A line that has not changed since the vehicle started tells the ground nothing about itself; and the kernel's event queue is finite, so a burst can leave the ground holding a value that is wrong with no way to know it. A report every so often ends both: after one interval at most, what the ground has is what the lines are.
Requirement
The report's clock runs from when a report was due and not from when it was made, and a report missed by more than an interval is not made up for. What the ground wants is what the lines are now, so a vehicle that was busy sends one report late rather than several together.
Requirement
A payload that asks for neither is refused: edge: none with no state_interval is a set of lines watched for no changes and reported at no interval, which would send nothing at all. Saying it takes writing edge: none, so it is not a thing a file does by leaving something out.
Requirement
poll_interval is not a GPIO word. It is the least time between reads of a descriptor whose data appears because the vehicle reads -- see How a node is driven -- and a line request is read when there are events in it. What a set of lines states instead is state_interval, which is a cadence and says so.
Requirement
A payload names at least one line, names none of them twice, and names no more than the kernel's limit of 64 on one request. A payload watching no lines has nothing to report, and one watching a line twice would report every change of it twice.
Requirement
This is the one type whose payload data is made rather than carried, so its shape is settled here: each event becomes a record of sixteen bytes -- eight of time, four of line offset, four of value. The time is a timestamp as every other time here is: microseconds since the UNIX epoch, least significant byte first, and all ones for a clock that could not be read. A line's change and a telemetry response can then be read against each other, which is the whole reason the field is a time since the epoch at all; the kernel's own timestamp is nanoseconds on a monotonic clock, which says nothing to a ground station.
Requirement
A packet_size that is not a whole number of records is refused. A packet that was part of a record would be a reading nobody could use.
Requirement
A chip is shared and a line is not. Several payloads commonly watch lines of one chip, as several devices sit on one bus, and the kernel gives a line to one request at a time -- so two payloads naming one line are two handlers of which the second could not start.
Requirement
A set of lines states its direction, and the default is input. A set of discretes is more often something read -- a deployment switch, an arming line -- than something driven, and a line requested as an input is one the kernel will not write, so the direction decides the request and cannot be deduced from anything else the file says.
Requirement
edge, bias and debounce are refused on an output rather than ignored. They are about watching a line, an output has no edges to report and nothing to debounce, and a file that states one of them for a driven line is saying something it does not mean -- commonly a set of lines whose direction was changed and whose other terms were left as they were. The refusal names the term that was missed.
Requirement
A GPIO payload cannot be triggered, and only one of a read and a write is open to it. An input refuses a write -- there is nothing to write a request to, and driving one would be commanding a payload rather than reading it, as the IIO endpoint says of its own. An output refuses a read: a driven line reports nothing, so reading one would be reporting what this node itself asked for, and a configuration that said output where it meant input is told so rather than left waiting for events that never come. Each refusal names the direction that would allow what was tried.
Requirement
Driven lines are driven together, to the one state, by a decision on a link -- the text 1 or 0, and nothing else, a decision being the one thing on a link that is text rather than a value. There is nothing in the configuration to say which line of a set is which, so a pattern would have no meaning; and three heater circuits switched one at a time would be three thermal states of a vehicle on the way to the one asked for. A datum that is neither 1 nor 0 is refused and not guessed at, because what is on the other end of an output is a heater or a valve.
Requirement
The bits and the mask of a set-values call are indexed by a line's place in the request and not by its offset on the chip, which is how the v2 interface counts. Every line of the request appears in the mask whichever way they are going: a mask of zero is a call that says nothing.
Requirement
The request names the handler as its consumer. A line held by something unnamed is the hardest type of conflict to find, and gpioinfo shows the name beside the line.
Requirement
The line request's descriptor is read without waiting. A read gathers every event that is waiting, so a blocking descriptor would have it wait for the next one after collecting the last -- a conduit stopped until a line happened to change again, holding the events it had already gathered.
Requirement
The kernel's structures are declared in the endpoint and their sizes asserted against the kernel's own. libc has no GPIO character-device definitions, and a structure of the wrong size is an ioctl that reads past what it was handed -- which nothing else in a test would notice.
Requirement
The simulator moves lines through gpio-sim, which it finds rather than makes. Nothing in user space can drive a wire, so what stands in is a chip whose lines exist in memory with a file per line saying what it is pulled to; loading the module and making a chip need root, so a payload whose chip is not there says how to make one -- as the I2C stand-in does.
Requirement
A simulated chip is found by the node it became and not by its number. A gpio-sim chip's node is numbered as chips appear, exactly as an i2c-stub adapter's bus is, so the number a payload set names is not an identity.
Requirement
The packet and segment sizes and the faults mean nothing to a simulated set of discretes, and it says so rather than appearing to obey them. There are no bytes to size, drop, corrupt or truncate: what it produces is an edge on each of its lines every packet interval.

rpmsg Payloads: Another Core

Experimental, not yet supported.

A flight computer is commonly one package with cores of more than one type in it, and the payload is then software on another core rather than anything on a wire. Linux talks to it over rpmsg: the remote announces a named channel, Linux makes a character device for it, and the two ends exchange whole messages through that device.

The rules are in tcslibgs::endpoint_config_rpmsg, the endpoint in tcspecial::endpoint_rpmsg and the stand-in in tcssim::payload_rpmsg.

Requirement
An rpmsg payload states a path and a service, and the service is required. A node's number is not an identity: it is assigned as channels announce themselves, so the node a file names can be another core's channel after a change in boot order -- the hazard an I2C stub adapter has with its bus number.
Requirement
The handler holds the node it opened against that service, where the kernel will say. /sys/class/rpmsg/<node>/name is the name the remote announced; a node that is not the channel the file asked for is refused rather than exchanged with, because the alternative is a handler trading telemetry with the wrong software.
Requirement
Where the kernel publishes no name, nothing is refused. A stand-in has no sysfs entry and no node number to move, and the check exists to catch a number that moved.
Requirement
A packet_size larger than one message is refused where the file is read. rpmsg messages go in buffers whose size the transport fixes -- 496 bytes after the header on the usual virtio transport -- and rpmsg does not fragment, so the remote would receive the first part of a packet with no way of knowing the rest was coming. A platform with other buffers states max_message_bytes.
Requirement
An rpmsg payload has no stream rule: a read hands over one whole message and never part of one.
Requirement
An rpmsg payload may be triggered. The channel carries both directions, so the handler writes a request and the remote answers -- which is what an IIO payload cannot do and a CAN payload can.
Requirement
The stand-in is a pseudo-terminal, which carries the bytes both ways and keeps no message boundaries. A payload that writes faster than the handler reads will therefore have two messages arrive as one read, where the real driver would hand over one per read; said here because it is the one way the stand-in differs from the thing.

IIO Payloads: a Sensor's Buffer

Experimental, not yet supported.

The kernel's Industrial I/O subsystem is what a sensor looks like on Linux. A program that wants a stream of samples enables the channels it cares about and a buffer, both through sysfs, and then reads /dev/iio:deviceN, which hands over packed sample sets as they become ready.

That is a device file a handler reads, which is nearly a device-file payload. What makes it a type of its own is that it is read-only. The rules are in tcslibgs::node::iio, the endpoint in tcspecial::node::iio and the stand-in in tcssim::node::iio.

Requirement
An IIO payload is located by a path and states nothing else. It took two terms once -- a number of channels and a sample_rate -- and both were recorded and never applied: channels and buffers are enabled through sysfs by whoever brought the sensor up, and a handler that wrote there would be reconfiguring a sensor other programs may be reading. A payload documents and takes only what it can set, so a file that states either is refused and told which word and why.
Requirement
An IIO payload has no stream rule. A buffer read hands over whole sample sets and never part of one, so there is nothing for a rule about where a read ends to say.
Requirement
An IIO payload cannot be triggered, and the refusal is in the payload configuration rather than in the simulator. It is not that a stand-in cannot hear a request: a sample buffer is a stream out of the sensor, so there is nothing on that descriptor to write and nowhere for a trigger to go. A datagram payload is refused in the same place for the same type of reason.
Requirement
The endpoint refuses a write rather than attempting one, and says what an IIO payload is. The write would fail anyway -- the device is opened for reading -- but a driver's error says nothing about why, where this says that a sensor's settings are in sysfs and not on this descriptor.
Requirement
The stand-in is a pseudo-terminal, as a serial line's is, and stands in for the data and not the sysfs around it. The kernel's iio_dummy module would be a sensor and is the wrong shape for this: it generates values of its own, so a simulator file would decide nothing about what the payload produced. What is missing from the stand-in is missing from the handler too, which touches none of those attributes.

CAN Payloads: a Bus of Peers

Experimental, not yet supported.

A CAN controller is a network interface rather than a device file, so a CAN payload is located by an interface and by the identifier its frames carry. Both ends of the link are sockets on that interface, which is what makes this the one experimental type whose stand-in is as faithful as a socket: a bus carries every member's frames to every other member, so the simulator joins the bus rather than standing in for something on it.

The rules are in tcslibgs::endpoint_config_can, the endpoint in tcspecial::endpoint_can and the stand-in in tcssim::payload_can.

Requirement
A CAN payload states an interface and a can_id, and neither is optional. The interface is where the bus is; the identifier is what tells one payload on a shared bus from another, as an address tells one device on an I2C bus from another.
Requirement
A CAN payload states no path, payload_address, payload_interface or protocol, and an interface or a can_id on any other type is refused. A controller is not a device file and not a host and port, and a line that named an interface would be a line written for a bus it is not on.
Requirement
Two payloads may share an interface and may not share an identifier. A bus is shared by design -- the reason an I2C payload claims its address rather than its bus -- and two handlers filtering on one identifier would each read the other's frames and count them as its own.
Requirement
A packet_size a frame cannot carry is refused where the file is read. A classic frame carries eight bytes and a CAN FD frame sixty-four, both exactly, and CAN does not fragment: a payload whose packets are larger would be a handler that could never send one, discovered a frame at a time at run time rather than when the file was read.
Requirement
The identifier's format follows from its value. The base range is eleven bits and the extended range twenty-nine, and the two do not overlap, so a file stating both a value and a format would have two answers to one question. An identifier above the extended range is refused.
Requirement
A handler's socket is filtered to its payload's identifier. A bus carries everybody's traffic, and a handler that read the lot would count another payload's frames as its own -- which is the one thing a bus makes easy to get wrong.
Requirement
A CAN payload states no bitrate. It is set where the interface is brought up, by the platform or by ip link, and a handler that opens a socket on it cannot change it -- so a file stating one would be stating a value nothing applies. What a socket can set it does state: the frame format and the loopback.
Requirement
A CAN payload may be triggered. Both ends are peers on the bus, so a simulated payload really does hear the handler ask -- which a payload on an I2C bus cannot, the request there being the master's own read. The trigger goes out with the payload's own identifier, and the socket does not receive its own frames, so a handler never reads back the trigger it just sent.
Requirement
The simulator finds the bus and does not make one. Creating a virtual bus and bringing it up need root, so a simulated payload whose interface is not there says so and names the two commands that would make one -- as the I2C stand-in does for its kernel module.

Serial Payloads: a Line

A start-stop line: each byte delimited by its own start and stop bits, which is what termios drives. A line whose bits are carried on a clock is not one of these -- Linux drives such a line as a network interface rather than as a device file -- and is an HDLC Payloads: a Synchronous Line instead.

Requirement
This type is start-stop framing and nothing else. It used to be both, reached by saying asynchronous: true or false, and almost nothing about the two is the same: one is located by a path and the other by an interface, one is a stream and the other frames, one is set with termios on a file descriptor and the other with an ioctl on a socket. Two types with one word between them was the arrangement that let a file state a clock nothing applied.
Requirement
stop_bits is required. A stop bit is what start-stop framing uses in place of a clock, so a line that states none is not a line of this type.
Requirement
A clocked line's terms are refused by name on one of these: standard, clock_type, clock_rate, encoding, frame_check and loopback belong to the hdlc type, and a start-stop line shares no clock.
Requirement
parity is what termios sets per character -- none, even, odd, mark or space -- and a frame check is not one of them. A file writing a CRC here is refused with the five a character can have.
Requirement
A line is read back after it is set, and a parity the port did not take is an error. A pseudo-terminal -- which is what a simulated line is -- rejects even parity outright and quietly drops the rest, so a handler that did not look would be checking nothing while its configuration said it was checking every character. The message names the parity and says the port is what will not have it.

RS-485: a Two-Wire Bus

Experimental, not yet supported.

A start-stop line may be half of an RS-485 bus rather than one end of a point-to-point link: one pair of wires, several transmitters, and each of them obliged to stop driving the pair the instant it has finished. The turnaround is a few bit times -- a write returns before the last bit has left the shift register -- so Linux does it in the UART driver, told through TIOCSRS485.

RS-485 is not a type of link. It is the electrical layer under a start-stop line: the same termios terms, the same framing, the same device file. So these are terms of the serial type, in tcslibgs::endpoint_config_rs485, and a payload on a four-wire line states none of them and is unchanged.

Requirement
A line states rs485: true to be a bus, and says nothing to be a plain line. Absent is what every payload set written before this says, and what an RS-422 line says.
Requirement
A line that states rs485: true states rs485_rts_on_send. Which level of RTS drives the transceiver is a property of the board rather than of the protocol -- the signal commonly reaches the enable pin through an inverter -- so a line given a default would have an even chance of transmitting exactly when it should be listening.
Requirement
The turnaround delays default to nought and are refused above a second. A turnaround is measured in bit times, so a file asking for seconds of it has mistaken the unit, and a line that held the bus that long would look like one that had failed.
Requirement
A turnaround stated without rs485: true is refused rather than kept against the day it is turned on. A file that set one and left the mode off has described a bus whose driver is never released, and reading it as a plain line would be reading a different file from the one that was written.
Requirement
None of it applies to an hdlc payload, and there is no word for it there. The kernel's RS-485 support is part of its start-stop drivers, where a synchronous line is driven through generic HDLC and a network interface instead.
Requirement
The mode is applied and read back, and a line that did not take it is refused rather than started. A driver that does not support the mode may take the call and clear the flag, and a line left in point-to-point mode on a two-wire bus transmits into every other transmitter on it -- including the payload this handler is trying to read.
Requirement
A simulated line cannot be an RS-485 bus and says so. A pseudo-terminal has no transceiver and no RTS to drive, so it refuses the mode, and a set meant to be run against tcssim states no rs485 -- as it states parity: none for the same reason.

A line's terms

Name Required Description
datarate Yes Line rate in bits per second
stop_bits Yes Stop bits after each byte: 1, 1.5, 2
parity No none, even, odd, mark or space, defaulting to none
byte_length Yes Data bits in each byte, from 5 to 8
stream Yes Stream payload protocol attributes, below
packet_size No Bytes in one packet. Shared by every type of payload, and listed with the rest above
Requirement
A serial payload has a stream rule, because a serial port is a stream and so there is always a rule deciding where one read ends.

HDLC Payloads: a Synchronous Line

Experimental, not yet supported, and the one type with nothing to be run against.

A synchronous serial line: bits carried on a clock, framed as HDLC. Linux calls the mechanism generic HDLC, and what it presents is a network interface -- hdlc0 -- not a device file. The terms of the line are set with one ioctl on it, SIOCWANDEV, which carries whichever of the structures in linux/hdlc/ioctl.h its type field names; the data is frames on that interface. The rules are in tcslibgs::node::hdlc, the endpoint in tcspecial::node::hdlc, and there is no stand-in at all.

Requirement
An hdlc payload is located by an interface and nothing else. The kernel drives such a line as a network interface, so there is no file to open; a payload stating a path is refused and told which word to use.
Requirement
One interface is one payload. A raw HDLC frame carries no address, so there is nothing to tell two payloads on one line apart -- which is what makes this a claim on the whole interface, where a CAN payload's claim is on an identifier and the bus is shared by design.
Requirement
The terms are the kernel's own two structures, and nothing beyond them. standard, clock_type, clock_rate and loopback are sync_serial_settings -- the port's wiring -- and encoding and frame_check are raw_hdlc_proto, which is what the bits mean once the port is clocked. The values are the kernel's, word for word.
Requirement
standard and clock_type are required. A line carries no negotiation, so both ends must be told the same thing, and a line read with the wrong one of either delivers noise rather than nothing. The encoding and the frame check default to the kernel's own plain values -- nrz and none -- a short cable commonly wanting neither clock recovery nor a CRC.
Requirement
t1 and e1 are not standards this takes. They are telco framings rather than a port's wiring, and the kernel takes a different structure for them -- te1_settings, with a map of which timeslots the link owns -- which nothing here states. A file asking for one is told that rather than being given a port set up as something else.
Requirement
clock_rate belongs to whoever generates the clock: required with internal or tx_internal, where this end drives it, and refused with external or tx_from_rx, where the far end does. A rate this end cannot set is a number it has no business stating, which is the rule the whole type was rewritten under.
Requirement
The frame check is called frame_check and not parity. The kernel's field is named parity for both types of line, and on a synchronous one it is a CRC over a frame rather than a bit per character: one word for the two was what made a start-stop line's parity and a frame check look like one term.
Requirement
Both halves are read back after they are set, and a line that reports something else is refused. IF_GET_IFACE and IF_GET_PROTO exist for exactly this: a card may take the call and keep less than it was given, and a line whose frame check is not the one the configuration asked for is a line checking nothing while the file says it checks every frame. It is the rule a start-stop line already follows with its parity.
Requirement
The port's wiring is set before the framing. The framing is attached to a port that is already clocked, which is the order sethdlc uses and the order the drivers expect.
Requirement
The frames move over an AF_PACKET socket of type SOCK_DGRAM bound to the interface. SOCK_DGRAM rather than SOCK_RAW because a raw HDLC frame has no link header worth seeing: a read hands over one frame's payload and a write puts one payload into one frame, as on a CAN bus.
Requirement
An hdlc payload has no stream rule. A frame is its own length, so there is nothing for a rule about where a read ends to say.
Requirement
An hdlc payload may be triggered. Both ends are peers on the line, so a far end really does hear the handler ask -- as on a CAN bus, and unlike on an I2C bus where the request is the master's own read.
Requirement
Bringing the interface up is not this software's business, as it is not for a CAN controller: ip link set dev hdlc0 up, from whatever starts the vehicle. What this does is set the line's terms and move its frames.

A line's terms

Name Required Description
interface Yes The interface the card presents: hdlc0
standard Yes How the port is wired: v35, v24, x21 or x21d
clock_type Yes Whose clock the bits are on: external, internal, tx_internal, tx_from_rx
clock_rate If ours Bits per second, where this end generates the clock. Refused where the far end does
encoding No How the bits are carried: nrz (the default), nrzi, fm_mark, fm_space, manchester
frame_check No none (the default), crc16_pr0, crc16_pr1, crc16_pr0_ccitt, crc16_pr1_ccitt, crc32_pr0_ccitt or crc32_pr1_ccitt
loopback No true to put the port in loopback, for proving a card with no line attached. Default false
packet_size No Bytes in one packet. Shared by every type of payload, and listed with the rest above

I2C Payloads: a Bus

A serial port is configured by describing how a byte is framed on the wire. I2C needs none of that, because the protocol fixes it: every byte is eight data bits, most significant bit first, followed by an acknowledge bit. What a payload does carry is how the master addresses the device and how far it will go to complete a transfer.

A bus's terms

Name Required Description
ten_bit No Address the device with 10 address bits rather than 7. Default false
pec No Append an SMBus packet error check, a CRC-8, to each transfer. Default false
retries No Times a transfer is retried after a lost arbitration or an unexpected NAK. Default 0
timeout No How long a transfer waits before it fails
packet_size No Bytes in one packet. Shared by every type of payload, and listed with the rest above
Requirement
An I2C payload specifies no byte length, parity, or stop bits, because the protocol fixes the framing of a byte and leaves nothing to configure.
Requirement
An I2C payload has no stream rule, because the master clocks exactly as many bytes as it asks for and a transfer is therefore already bounded.
Requirement
With 7-bit addressing, an address names a device only in the range 0x08 to 0x77. The specification keeps 0x00 to 0x07 for the general call, the CBUS address, and the high-speed master code, and 0x78 to 0x7F for the 10-bit addressing prefix and for future use. A payload given one of those is reported as an error rather than accepted as a device that will never answer.
Requirement
With 10-bit addressing there is no reserved block, and the whole range 0x000 to 0x3FF is available. A 10-bit transfer is introduced by the 0x78 prefix and carries its address in the bytes that follow, so the reservations that apply to a 7-bit address do not apply to it.
Requirement
A timeout takes effect rounded up to a multiple of 10 ms, that being the resolution the bus driver keeps it in. The value the file gave is kept as it was written, so that what a reader of the file sees and what the bus was asked for can both be reported.
Requirement
An I2C payload states no bus_speed. The bus clock belongs to the controller, which the platform configures from the device tree or from ACPI, and a program holding an endpoint open cannot change it -- so a file stating one would be stating a value nothing applies. It was a term once, recorded and never applied, and is refused by name now.

SPI Payloads: a Peripheral

SPI is clocked and full duplex, so, as with I2C, there is no parity and there are no stop bits; a transfer is delimited by the chip select rather than by framing bits. What a payload carries is the shape of the clock and of a word.

A peripheral's terms

Name Required Description
max_speed Yes Greatest clock rate the peripheral accepts, in Hz
spi_mode Yes Clock polarity and phase: 0, 1, 2, 3
bits_per_word No Bits in each word. Default 8
bit_order No Bit sent first: msb or lsb. Default msb
cs_active No Chip select asserted low or high. Default low
packet_size No Bytes in one packet. Shared by every type of payload, and listed with the rest above
Requirement
A SPI payload specifies spi_mode, because the controller has no default that is right for every peripheral and a mismatched mode fails the way a mismatched data rate fails on a serial line.
Requirement
A SPI payload has no stream rule, because the master clocks exactly as many words as it asks for and a transfer is therefore already bounded.
Requirement
The clock rate is an upper bound. A controller divides its own clock down and so runs at the greatest rate it can produce that does not exceed the rate the payload gives.

Where a Read of a Line Ends

A stream delivers bytes with no frame boundaries of its own, so a node on one needs to be told where one read ends. Four attributes say so, written flat beside the node's other terms. A stream is a line or a stream socket: a network node whose protocol is tcp or unix_stream.

Stream attributes

Name Required Description
max_length Yes The most one read may hold, in bytes
read_timeout See below How long after its first byte a read ends with what has arrived, or none
terminators See below A list of byte sequences, each in the notation a trigger is written in, any of which ends a read
length_prefix See below What a read's first bytes say its length is: u8, u16be or u16le
Requirement
A line specifies the maximum read length, and it is at most a datum's, tcslibgs::DATUM_BYTES: a read is delivered whole, and one longer than a datum would arrive cut short, as pieces nothing knows how to put back together.
Requirement
A line specifies the timeout, the terminator list, the length prefix, or the timeout beside either of the other two.
Requirement
Specifying both a timeout and a terminator list is valid. A read then ends on whichever condition occurs first.
Requirement
A terminator list contains at least one terminator. An empty list is an error, because it states a terminator list without naming a terminator.
Requirement
A terminator is a run of one or more bytes, in the notation a trigger is written in, so that a record ending "\r\n" ends a read only when both have arrived, in order. A read ends where the first terminator to arrive whole ends -- the earliest to end, not the earliest to begin -- which is where it would have ended had the bytes come one at a time. A terminator longer than max_length is an error: no read could hold it, so it would end none.
Requirement
Terminators are a list: a YAML sequence, or one XML element for each. One may be written on its own, as a list of one. Several written as one value with commas between them are an error -- each is an entry of its own -- so a list has the one shape in a file. A node holds the list as one value with its entries joined by commas, which is what the editor shows; a comma inside a string is a character of it, so the join is exact. See tcslibgs::graph::LISTED.
Requirement
A stream socket -- tcp or unix_stream -- specifies these attributes as a line does, max_length among them, because it is a stream of bytes with no packets of its own: without them a pass would take whatever bytes happened to be waiting. A datagram socket -- udp, unix_dgram, raw -- that specifies them is an error: a datagram is a packet already, and a rule on one would be a rule nothing applies.
Requirement
To read a fixed number of bytes at a time, with no timeout and no terminator, a line specifies a timeout of none.
Requirement
A length prefix is u8, one byte; u16be, two bytes, the more significant first; or u16le, two bytes, the less significant first. The length it gives counts the bytes after the prefix. A read ends when it holds the prefix and that many bytes, and is delivered prefix and all, as a read ended by a terminator keeps its terminator.
Requirement
A line with a length prefix specifies a timeout, which may be none. One lost byte puts every later prefix in the wrong place and nothing in the bytes says so: the timeout is what ends a frame that never finishes, so that the next byte is read as a prefix and the reads come back into step. none says there is to be no such recovery, and saying it is what tells that intent from a timeout forgotten.
Requirement
A line with a length prefix does not specify terminators. A terminator's byte can appear anywhere in the body of a frame whose length was given, and would end a read inside it, at a place that depends on the data.
Requirement
A line's max_length holds at least its length prefix.
Requirement
On a prefixed line the timeout does not deliver what has arrived. A frame shorter than its own prefix says is known to be broken, and is let go rather than handed to the ground as though it were a reading. A frame whose prefix gives more than max_length is let go as it arrives, without being held, so that the frame after it is read from its own prefix; a timeout ends the letting go of one that stops arriving. The vehicle logs a warning naming the node for each frame let go.
Requirement
A prefixed read asks the line for its prefix and then for no more than the frame the prefix gives, so the next frame's prefix stays in the line's buffer; both asks are made in one pass, so that a frame waiting on the line is not delayed a group's interval between its prefix and its body.
Requirement
The vehicle acts on the rule: each read of a line is held until it ends, and is then one datum. A terminator is the read's last bytes, and what arrived after it is the start of the next read; max_length ends a read that has filled; the timeout counts from the read's first byte and ends it with what has arrived. Without this a pass would take whatever bytes happened to be waiting, which is half a sentence as often as a whole one -- and a rule that is checked and then ignored is worse than no rule, because it reads as though it were applied. See tcspecial::runtime::frame.
Requirement
No more is held than max_length, and no more is asked of the line than would fill the read under way. A line that sends faster than it is read is held back by its own driver's buffer, not by a queue aboard that grows.
Requirement
Holding a read never blocks a pass. A timeout is therefore noticed by the first pass after it, and a read that ends by its timeout is late by as much as its group's interval; a read already ended is handed over before anything more is read.

The fixed-length requirement deserves a word, because it is the one case where the format asks for something to be stated that could have been left implicit. A line giving only a maximum length would be ambiguous: it reads either as "hand me this many bytes at a time", which is a real and useful intent, or as a file whose author forgot to say when a read should end. These have different consequences on a link that goes quiet, so the format does not guess. Writing read_timeout: none says the first explicitly, and a line with neither attribute is reported as an error rather than being taken for it.

Value Syntax

XML carries every attribute value as text, while YAML distinguishes numbers from strings. Both are accepted everywhere a value is expected, so the same value may be written 8 or "8" without changing its meaning.

Value syntax

Type Syntax
Whole number Decimal, or hexadecimal with an 0x prefix
Bytes Parts one after another, each a C string in quotes or bytes in hexadecimal in square brackets: "AT" [0D 0A]
Timeout A whole number with a unit of us, ms, or s, or the word none
List A YAML sequence, or one XML element for each entry
Flag true or false
Bus address Decimal or 0x hexadecimal, within the width the payload's addressing allows, and not one the bus reserves
Requirement
A timeout carries an explicit unit. A bare number is an error, so that a file cannot silently mean milliseconds where seconds were intended.
Requirement
A timeout of zero is an error. A read that is not to wait is written none.
Requirement
A hexadecimal value may be written as a number or as a string: 0x48 or "0x48". Both formats accept either spelling and both mean the same value.
Requirement
An attribute has one spelling. A file that writes another -- hyphens where the name has underscores, or underscores where it has hyphens -- is refused like any word nothing reads, and is told the spelling: max-length is answered it is spelled max_length. Two spellings would be twice the words for the editor, the XML and the YAML to agree about, and a node that wrote both would say one thing twice with nothing to choose between them. See tcslibgs::graph::spelled.
Requirement
The rule and its answer are the same wherever a name is refused: a node's attribute, an attribute of the tcspecial section -- log.segment-size is answered it is spelled log.segment_size -- and the attribute a SET_NODE names, which is told the spelling a command binding gives it. A refused SET_NODE's hint reaches the ground in the answer's reason, and the vehicle's log.

Simulator Configuration Files

The tcspecial configuration describes payloads. Simulating one takes more than that description holds, and the extra is not payload configuration: how often a payload produces a packet, and how it divides a packet into segments, are choices about a simulation. They are stated in a separate file, which only tcssim acts on. A shipped pair is examples/minimal/minimal.yaml and minimal-sim.yaml beside it; the parser is tcslibgs::sim_config.

Requirement
The parser lives with the other three configuration languages rather than inside tcssim. Both GUIs read this file now -- one to simulate payloads and one to show what a payload set says about them -- and a configuration language read by two programs cannot live inside one of them.

The format is chosen from the extension exactly as every other configuration file's is, so a simulator configuration may be written in YAML or XML.

File Structure

A simulator configuration file has a description of its own, a tcssim section, which is required, and two sections that are optional.

tcssim

What the simulator itself is configured with. Today that is one address:

tcssim:
  ready: "127.0.0.1:4800"

ready is the UDP address of the readiness socket, where the simulator says whether it has made its stand-ins; see Waiting for the Simulator. It is required, and every shipped simulator file states one.

simulated payload groups
Named groups of settings. A group carries what several simulated payloads have in common, and may say whether they are simulated, with simulate.
simulated payloads
The simulated payloads themselves. Each names the data handler it stands in for, may name a group, and may state settings of its own. Each has simulate, from itself or from its group but not both: see Saying Which Payloads Are Simulated.

Settings

Attribute Type Meaning
simulate bool Whether the payload is simulated: true for tcssim to stand in for it, false for the real device. Required, from the payload or its group and not both
packet_interval length of time How long between packets, as a length of time -- 1 s, 500 ms -- or continuous for as fast as the payload can be driven
segment_interval length of time How long between the segments of one packet, continuous included. Defaults to the packet interval
segment_size number Bytes in one segment. Defaults to the whole packet, whose size the payload configuration gives

What it stands in for. Neither of these is a setting of the simulation. The payload configuration decides both, and they are stated here again only so that the two files can be checked against each other.

Attribute Type Meaning
type string The type of payload this stands in for, in the payload configuration's own word: network, device-file, serial, i2c, or spi
protocol string Which transport, for a network payload. Refused for every other type

The payload's own end of the link, which nothing else states:

Attribute Type Meaning
payload_address string The socket the payload answers from: a host for a UDP payload, a path for a Unix datagram one. Optional, and only for those
payload_port number The port it answers from. Optional, and refused for a Unix datagram payload

Fault injection settings, each defaulting to no fault at all:

Attribute Type Meaning
drop_percent number Packets in a hundred that are never sent
corrupt_percent number Packets in a hundred with one byte altered
truncate_percent number Packets in a hundred sent short of their size
jitter length of time How late a packet may be, as a length of time, chosen afresh for each one
silent_after number Packets after which the payload sends no more, the link still open. 0 is never
close_after number Packets after which the payload hangs up. 0 is never
ignore_trigger_percent number Requests in a hundred a triggered payload leaves unanswered
Requirement
A simulated payload takes each setting it does not state from the group it names.
Requirement
A simulated periodic payload has a packet interval, from itself or from its group. One with none is an error rather than a payload driven at a rate nobody chose.
Requirement
A simulated triggered payload has no packet interval, and stating one is an error. It sends when tcspecial asks and at no rate of its own, so an interval here would govern nothing -- and a file that gave one has not understood which type of payload it is simulating. The segment settings still apply: how a payload divides an answer is its own business whichever way it was prompted.
Requirement
A simulated triggered payload sends one packet for each request it is sent, and nothing otherwise. A payload that answered on a clock as well would be two types at once, and no file could say which rate was which.
Requirement
The simulator refuses a triggered payload it cannot hear a request on. Tcspecial opens a device and reads it, so there is nothing for the simulator to be written to; and an I2C master's own read is the trigger, which a second master on the bus cannot see. A payload that answered nothing would look exactly like a handler whose polling had stopped working.

What a Simulated Payload Stands In For

The two files are joined by handler name and by nothing else, and a name is exactly what survives a payload set copied from another one. Two sets that differ only in how their payload is reached -- one a datagram socket, one a stream -- therefore have simulator files that each parse perfectly well against the other's payloads, and the run that follows looks like a payload that never sends. So each simulated payload says what it stands in for, and the statement is compared rather than used.

Requirement
A simulated payload states the type of payload it stands in for, and a network payload the transport as well. Neither is taken from this file: the payload configuration decides both, and a payload that stated neither could not be checked against anything.
Requirement
The two files cannot disagree about a payload's type or its transport. A disagreement is an error naming the payload and what each file says of it, rather than one file overriding the other: both describe the one payload, so a disagreement means one of them is not the file that was meant.
Requirement
A transport is stated by a network payload and refused for every other type. A line, a bus or a peripheral is not reached by a transport, so one stated for it would govern nothing.
Requirement
The type and the transport are written in the same words the payload configuration uses, and the error that refuses a word that is neither lists the words that would have worked. The spellings are one table read from both files, so the two cannot come to disagree about what a type is called.
Requirement
Where the payload is is not stated here. The simulator takes it from the payload configuration, which decides it, so this file stating it again would be a second place to keep in step and nothing gained: payload_interface written here -- or address and port, which that file spelled it before -- is refused with the file it belongs in, and with the pair this file does have.

What this file may say about an address is the other end of the same link: the socket the simulated payload answers from.

Requirement
A simulated payload may be given the socket it binds for itself, as payload_address and payload_port. Nothing in the link needs it -- a handler learns where its payload is from the first packet it gets -- but something outside the simulation may: a rule on a firewall, or a capture being read afterwards. Stated by neither file otherwise, so there is nothing for this to disagree with.
Requirement
Only a datagram payload has an address of its own to bind. A stream payload is the end that waits, so it binds the address the payload configuration gives it, and a second address stated here would be two answers to where it is; a device, a line, a bus or a peripheral has no address at all.
Requirement
A Unix datagram payload answers from a path, so it takes a payload_address and no payload_port.
Requirement
Neither end may be the other. A payload told to bind its handler's own socket is refused here rather than left to fail at the bind, which says nothing about which file was wrong: two sockets cannot be one, and the two ends of a link are two sockets.
Requirement
A file that states neither gets what every simulator file asked for before this existed: any interface and a port the system chooses, or for a Unix datagram payload a path beside the handler's.
Requirement
A group may carry the type and the transport, like every other setting a payload does not state. A group whose payloads are not all reached the same way leaves them with the payloads: the thermostat's sensors share a rate and a thermal mass, and two of the three are on a different kind of bus from the third.

Thermal Masses

Every other simulated payload makes its bytes up. A buffer filled from a random number generator exercises a link and nothing at all of a control loop: three sensors sending noise never agree, so the vote over them produces nothing anybody can check, and a thermostat downstream of it cannot be watched holding a band.

A thermal mass is what makes them agree. The sensors attached to one report its temperature, each in its own encoding, and the heaters attached to it warm it -- so what the vehicle reads is a consequence of what the vehicle drove, and the whole loop can be watched closing.

Requirement
The simulator file may declare thermal masses. Each has a name, the temperature of the environment it sits in, and a time constant: how long it takes to lose a given fraction of the difference between itself and that environment.
Requirement
A simulated payload may be attached to a mass, either as something that reads it or as something that heats it. A heater states what it adds, in kelvin a second.
Requirement
A mass nothing is attached to is refused. It would be a state nothing reads and nothing changes, which is a file saying something it cannot mean.

The arithmetic is Newton's cooling with a constant heat,

dT/dt = -(T - environment) / time_constant + heat

where heat is the sum of the rates of the heaters that are on. Between two changes of that sum it is constant, so the equation has a closed form, and the mass is stepped by that rather than integrated:

T(t + dt) = rest + (T - rest) * exp(-dt / time_constant)
rest      = environment + heat * time_constant
Requirement
The mass is stepped exactly, whenever it is read and whenever a heater changes. A method whose error grew with the step would make the simulated temperature depend on how often somebody looked at it.

rest is where the mass would settle with that heat on for ever, and it is the figure that says whether the heaters can win at all: with a fifty second constant and six kelvin a second of heat, it is three hundred kelvin above the environment.

Requirement
A mass starts at its environment, with its heaters off. A mass that started at the setpoint would hide the one part of the loop worth watching, and a vehicle coming up cold is the case a thermostat exists for.

What the bytes are

A sensor has to send bytes the vehicle will read back as the temperature, and the format, the scale and the offset are not the sensor's own: they are on the to_kelvin node its readings go to.

Requirement
The encoding is taken from that node and not stated here. A scale written in both files is a scale that can disagree with itself, which would show up as a simulated temperature the vehicle reads back as a different one, with both files looking right.
Requirement
A sensor attached to a mass whose readings reach no conversion is refused, and so is one whose readings reach two. The first has no encoding to borrow and the second has no single answer.
Requirement
A temperature the format cannot hold is clamped and not wrapped. A wrapped count is a sensor reporting the other end of its range, which is a fault the vehicle has no way to tell from a reading.

What a heater is

Requirement
A heater sends nothing. Being driven is the whole of what it is for, so a packet of its own would be bytes nothing reads.
Requirement
A heater's packet interval is how often it looks for what the vehicle decided. It is the one thing pacing still means for a payload that sends nothing, and it is part of how promptly the loop responds: the vehicle cannot see a band crossed until its next sample, and the heaters cannot hear about it until they next look, so the overshoot is their rate over the sum of the two.
Requirement
A heater acts on the last thing the vehicle said and not on the next thing in the queue. A decision is not a reading -- it says what the device should be now -- so a vehicle deciding faster than the heater looks must not leave it acting on a decision that is older every pass.
Requirement
A heater must be a kind of payload the simulator can be driven through, which today means a socket. A heater on discrete lines is driven through gpio-sim, which the simulator drives and cannot yet be driven through, so the shipped thermostat example attaches its sensors to a mass and its heaters to nothing: what that example shows is the vehicle's half of the loop.

Fault Injection

A payload that always works exercises only the path where everything works. The settings above have a simulated payload misbehave in the ways a real one does, so that a handler's own counting, framing, timeouts and reconnection can be made to happen on purpose rather than waited for.

Requirement
Fault injection settings appear in the simulator configuration file only. A tcspecial configuration file describes the payloads a mission flies, and nothing flying is configured to drop a tenth of its packets; a fault stated there would also be read by tcspecial, which must be told what the payload is meant to do rather than what the simulator is doing to it.
Requirement
A fault setting is inherited from a group exactly as every other setting is. A group of flaky payloads is as much a thing several payloads share as a rate is.
Requirement
A percentage is nought to a hundred. A larger number is an error naming the setting and the value, because it is a misunderstanding of the unit rather than a request for a severer fault.
Requirement
A dropped packet is not counted as sent. The simulator's counters and the handler's are what tell a dropped packet from a lost one, and counters that agreed about a packet that never existed could not.
Requirement
A corrupted byte is never the byte it was, and a truncated packet is always shorter than its size and never empty. A fault that left the data as it found it would have a file asking for a tenth of its packets corrupted quietly get fewer. A packet of one byte is the exception to truncation: there is no length both shorter than one and not empty, so it is sent whole, an empty packet being the dropping fault instead.
Requirement
Jitter is waited out before a packet is sent and inside its interval, never added to it. A payload told to send every second still sends every second, with the packet late within that second. Late and never early: early would be the simulator sending sooner than its own configuration asked for.
Requirement
A payload that has gone silent keeps its link open. That is what makes it worth simulating separately from one that closed: a wedged instrument looks to the handler exactly like a healthy link carrying nothing.
Requirement
A payload that hangs up listens again. The handler has to reconnect, which is the path being exercised, and a payload gone for the rest of the run would exercise it once at most.
Requirement
The simulator refuses a fault the payload could not have. Only a stream has a connection to close, so close_after is refused for every other type; only a triggered payload is asked for anything, so ignore_trigger_percent is refused for a periodic one. Either would otherwise sit in the file looking as though it governed something.
Requirement
A payload starting with any fault set says so once, in a line of log. A run whose payload was dropping a tenth of its packets should not have to be guessed at from the results afterwards.

Matching

The two files are joined by name, not by position: the order of one file has no bearing on the other, and a data handler's id is not a row number.

Requirement
A simulated payload names a data handler of the tcspecial configuration file. A name matching no data handler is an error, because a line that matched nothing would otherwise be indistinguishable from a misspelled name.
Requirement
Every data handler of the tcspecial configuration file has a simulated payload naming it. A handler nothing names is an error, because the simulator would otherwise start with a payload that never produces anything.
Requirement
A name is defined once in a file. Two groups or two simulated payloads sharing a name is an error rather than one silently shadowing the other, and as in the payload configuration the rule reaches no further than the file: a simulator file names the payloads of the set it belongs to, and two sets may name their payloads alike.
Requirement
A word that is not a setting is an error, in a payload, in a group, and in the file's own sections. A misspelled setting that was ignored would have the simulator do something other than what the file asked for and say nothing: a payload asked through a misspelling to drop a tenth of its packets drops none, and looks exactly like one that was never asked. A group is where such a word hides best, being read once and applying to every payload that names it.
Requirement
A group is named by a simulated payload. A group no payload names is an error rather than a group with no effect, for the same reason a payload configuration's unnamed group is: it is also what a misspelled group name looks like. As there, the error is reported from the payload's end.

Example

This is the shipped examples/thermostat/thermostat-sim.yaml, which simulates the three sensors of thermostat.yaml. All three are read together and so share a group, which carries their rate and the thermal mass they report; the mass is what makes three sensors agree about a temperature. The heaters are not simulated: they are discrete lines, which the simulator drives rather than being driven through.

description: Simulator settings for the payloads of thermostat.yaml

thermal_mass:
  - name: cabin
    environment: "283.15"
    time_constant: "50 s"

simulated_payload_groups:
  - name: cabin_temps
    packet_interval: 100 ms
    reads: cabin

simulated_payloads:
  - name: temp_1
    type: i2c
    group: cabin_temps

  - name: temp_2
    type: i2c
    group: cabin_temps

  - name: temp_3
    type: spi
    group: cabin_temps

Testing

There are two components of testing software. Tcssim is a GUI used to simulate payloads and tcsmoc is used to simulate the MOC. Both allow user interaction to change parameters and see what result the changes produce. A third program, tcsverify, runs none of it: it reads the files the other three read and says what is wrong with them.

High-Level View of Test Configuration

       GROUND              :                     SPACE
===========================:===================================================
                           :
+=======================+  :    +=========================+     +=================+
||  Simulated MOC      ||  :    ||  Flight Software      ||     ||  Simulated    ||
||                     ||  :    ||                       ||     || Payloads      ||
+=======================+  :    +=========================+     +=================+
|  +---------+          |  :    |    +=================+  |     |                 |
|  | tcsmoc  |          |  :  ----+->| tcspecial      |  |     |                 |
|  | Control |          |  : |  | |  | (tcslibgs)      |  |     |                 |
|  | S/W     |          |  : |  | |  +-----------------+  |     |                 |
|  +---------+          |  : |  | |  | Command         |  |     |                 |
|       ^               |  : |  | +->| Interpreter     |  |     |                 |
|       |               |  : |  | |  +-----------------+  |     |  +-----------+  |
|       v               |  : |  | |  | Data            |<-------|->| Payload 0 |  |
|  +-----------------+  |  : |  | +->| Handler 0       |  |     |  +-----------+  |
|  | tcslib          |<------   | |  +-----------------+  |     |  +-----------+  |
|  | (tcslibgs)      |  |  :    | |  | Data            |<--------->| Payload 1 |  |
|  +-----------------+  |  :    | |  | Handler 1       |  |     |  +-----------+  |
|  |  configuration  |  |  :    | |  |      .          |  |     |                 |
|  +-----------------+  |  :    |           .             |     |                 |
+-----------------------+  :    | |  |      .          |  |     |                 |
                           :    | |  +-----------------+  |     |  +-----------+  |
                           :    | +->| Data            |<--------->| Payload n |  |
                           :    |    | Handler n       |  |     |  +-----------+  |
                           :    |    +-----------------+  |     |                 |
                           :    |    |  configuration  |  |     |                 |
                           :    |    +-----------------+  |     |                 |
                           :    +-------------------------+     +-----------------+

Simulation Modes

Implemented, except that the mode and the judgements are not yet written to the telemetry log: they are said in the startup summary only. The mode is read by tcslibgs::simulate::take_mode, a payload is judged by tcslibgs::simulate::judge and its type's judged in tcslibgs::node::payload, and the mode is checked by tcslibgs::simulate::refusals, which tcspecial and tcssim both call.
