Tcspecial reaches each payload through whatever is at the end of its endpoint, and nothing in its configuration says whether that is the payload or a stand-in for it. /dev/i2c-7 is an i2c-stub on one host and a real bus on another, and a configuration that names the wrong bus number drives real hardware while every file involved reads as a simulation. So two things are said, and each is checked against what is found rather than believed: the simulator file says which payloads are meant to be simulated, with simulate (see Saying Which Payloads Are Simulated), and a simulation mode says which kind of run a run is meant to be.

Requirement

Tcspecial, tcssim and tcsmoc each take --simulate=<mode>, and the mode is one of three:

  • none: no payload may be a stand-in. This is the flight setting.
  • some: at least one payload is a stand-in and at least one is not. This is for debugging a configuration against part of its hardware.
  • all: every payload is a stand-in. This is for a run that must not exercise hardware at all, because a device switched on when nobody meant it to be can do damage that nothing afterwards undoes.
Requirement
The mode is none when the flag is not given, in all three programs. A flight launch that leaves the flag out then gets the flight setting, and a test run that leaves it out is refused at its first stand-in, saying which flag it wants. Of the two mistakes, that is the cheap one.
Requirement
The mode is a flag and not part of the configuration. The configuration flown is then the configuration tested, byte for byte, and its digest is the same under every mode, so a CONNECT agrees about it whichever mode the ground station was started in.
Requirement
A mode that is not one of the three, or the flag given twice, is refused, naming the three modes. A second --simulate is more likely a mistake about which run this is than something meant to override the first.
Requirement
A payload, for the mode, is a node of an interface type other than the gateway: what the configuration reaches over a socket, a line, a bus or a device file. Computations, logs, tees and app nodes are counted under no mode, because none of them has hardware behind it to stand in for -- an app node's program is the thing itself. The gateway is not counted either: it is the link the run is commanded over, which is set up by whoever started the ground station, and not one of the payloads the configuration serves.

How a Stand-in Is Recognised

Requirement
Tcspecial decides for itself whether each payload is a stand-in, from the endpoint it is configured to reach, and does not take the simulator file's word for it. The simulator file says what tcssim will stand in for. It cannot say what a path, a bus number or an address in the configuration actually reaches, and that is where the mistake this exists to catch is made.
Requirement
Each payload is judged before its endpoint is opened, from what is at the path, the bus, the interface or the address, and the run is refused before anything is opened if the mode is not met. Opening is not always harmless: a serial port raises its modem lines as it is opened, and a GPIO line requested as an output is driven the moment the request is granted. A check made after opening would already have done what all exists to prevent.
Requirement
A path with nothing at it is not a stand-in. The simulator makes its stand-ins before tcspecial starts and says when it has, so a stand-in tcspecial does not find is one that was not made; see Waiting for the Simulator.
Requirement

A payload is a stand-in when its endpoint is one of the things the simulator, or the stub scripts in bin/, make:

network      the address is a loopback address or a Unix-domain path,
             so the far end is on this host
i2c          the bus's adapter is the kernel's i2c-stub
gpio         the chip belongs to the kernel's gpio-sim
can          the interface is a virtual CAN interface, a vcan
iio          the device belongs to the kernel's iio_dummy, or the path
             resolves to a pseudo-terminal
serial, spi,
rpmsg,
device-file  the path resolves to a pseudo-terminal's slave, which is
             what tcssim makes
hdlc         never at its interface: nothing stands in for a
             synchronous line
hdlc, spi,
rpmsg, iio   reached at a stand_in, under some or all: the stand_in
             is a loopback address, so the far end is on this host

Tcssim does not yet make a pseudo-terminal for a device-file payload: what it stands in for there is the device producing data, which tcspecial reads from whatever is at the path. So a device-file payload is a stand-in only where something else has made its path a pseudo-terminal, and until tcssim makes one a run with a device-file payload cannot be all.

Requirement
What cannot be told apart is real. Under all it is refused, and the refusal says that tcspecial could not tell, which is a different thing from having found hardware. Failing that way round is the point of the mode: a stand-in mistaken for hardware costs a refused run, and hardware mistaken for a stand-in costs the hardware.
Requirement
The network judgement is the weakest of them, and is said to be here and in the manual. An address on this host says only that the far end is on this host, so a local program in front of real hardware passes it. A datagram payload is judged by the address the handler binds, which under all must be a loopback address, so that nothing off the host can be the one that answers.
Requirement
Each type's judgement is in that type's own module, tcslibgs::node, beside the type's other rules, and tcspecial and tcssim both call it. A type cannot then be added without saying how its stand-in is told apart, and the two programs cannot come to disagree about what a stand-in is.
Requirement

A refusal names the node, what it reaches, what was found there and the mode that forbids it, and says what would satisfy the mode. For example:

temp_1: /dev/i2c-1 is a real adapter (i915 gmbus), and --simulate=all
allows only stand-ins: point path at the i2c-stub bus, which
bin/mki2c makes.
Requirement
Tcspecial says the mode, and what it judged each payload to be, in its startup summary and in the telemetry log. The summary is for whoever is watching the vehicle start, and the log is the record of the run, which should say what kind of run it was.

Saying Which Payloads Are Simulated

Implemented: simulate is read by tcslibgs::sim_config::SimConfigFile::simulate_of, tcssim's comparison is tcslibgs::simulate::disagreement, and a stand-in reached over UDP is tcslibgs::graph::reach::Reach::opened.

Which payloads a run means to simulate is said in the simulator file, payload by payload, and checked against what is at each endpoint before tcssim stands in for anything. The file says what was meant; the check says whether the configuration points where the file thinks it does.

Requirement
simulate is a boolean, true or false, stated in a simulated payload or in a simulated payload group, and nowhere else: not in the tcspecial configuration, which is flown as it was tested, byte for byte.
Requirement
Every simulated payload has simulate, stated in the payload itself or taken from the group it names, and from exactly one of the two. A payload with neither is refused, naming it: whether a device is simulated is not left to a default. A payload that states it and names a group that states it too is refused, whether or not the two agree: a payload does not override its group's simulate, so that the group says it for every payload in it and a reader of the group has read the answer. A group that does not state it is allowed, and every payload in it then states its own.
Requirement
A payload whose simulate is true is simulated: tcssim stands in for it. One whose simulate is false is not: tcssim makes nothing for it, and tcspecial reaches the real device at the endpoint its configuration gives. A payload of the configuration the simulator file does not name is not simulated either.
Requirement

Tcssim compares each payload's simulate with what is at its endpoint, judged by the rules of How a Stand-in Is Recognised, before it stands in for anything, and refuses to start on a disagreement, naming the payload, its endpoint, what was found there and what the file says:

  • simulate: true where the endpoint is real hardware is refused. A stand-in tcssim makes for itself -- a pseudo-terminal, a socket -- has nothing at its path yet, and that is not a disagreement; what is refused is a path, a bus or an interface that is already something other than a stand-in, which is a configuration pointing at hardware in a file that says it is simulated. A stand-in the kernel makes -- i2c-stub, gpio-sim, vcan, iio_dummy -- must be there already, made by the scripts in bin/, and one that is not is refused, saying which script makes it.
  • simulate: false where the endpoint is a stand-in is refused: a payload meant to be real that the run would in fact simulate. Under the network judgement's rule this refuses a real payload reached at a loopback address, which cannot be told from a stand-in.
Requirement

A node of a type the operating system has nothing faithful to stand in for may state stand_in, a loopback address:port, in the tcspecial configuration. The types are hdlc, which has no virtual interface at all, and spi, rpmsg and iio, whose pseudo-terminal stand-ins carry the bytes but not the clock, the channel or the sysfs around them. Under --simulate=some or all, tcspecial reaches such a node over UDP at its stand_in instead of opening the device, by the rule of which end speaks first that a UDP payload has, and tcssim answers there as a UDP payload. Under none, the flight setting, stand_in is not used and the device is opened: the configuration that flies is the one that was tested, byte for byte.

stand_in is refused on every other type, whose stand-in is at its own endpoint and is the better test, the code that runs being the code that flies; and it is refused off this host, since a stand-in reached off the host is one nothing here can tell from the hardware. A node is judged where it will be reached: at its stand_in in a run with stand-ins. One rule says which, Reach::opened, and tcspecial's opening, its judgement and tcssim's all ask it.

Requirement
Tcssim controls every simulated payload and shows each the same way: a panel with the one button, which has it take part or not, and the counts of what it sent and what reached it. So a payload whose endpoint is a stand-in and that the simulator file does not name is refused, naming it, whatever made the stand-in -- a gpio-sim chip from bin/mkgpio the file leaves out would otherwise be a simulated device nothing controls or shows.
Requirement

Where the operating system makes the stand-in, tcssim watches it, and tcspecial is not told: what tcssim knows of a payload it learns from the stand-in, never from a link to the flight software. It is the same panel for every kind:

  • A pseudo-terminal, a socket or a vcan interface carries what each end sends, so tcssim counts both ways exactly.
  • A gpio-sim chip the vehicle drives is watched through each line's value, and each move is a datum received; a chip the vehicle reads is one tcssim moves.
  • An i2c-stub chip's register bank is read back each pass, and a change tcssim did not make is the vehicle writing -- an init, a trigger, a decision -- counted as received. A read of the vehicle's changes nothing and cannot be seen.
  • An iio_dummy sensor fills its own buffer from the kernel's trigger; tcssim finds it rather than making it, and its panel counts nothing, the samples going from the kernel to the vehicle past it.

A payload's button off is the device switched off: it sends nothing, answers nothing and acts on nothing -- a heater warms no mass -- and what reaches it is still counted, as what the vehicle did.

Requirement
A payload that is only driven -- a heater, a valve -- is simulated as any other: its stand-in sends nothing and counts what reaches it, and its simulator entry gives no packet interval, there being nothing to send. One that heats a thermal mass warms it as well.
Requirement
The comparison is tcssim's, and tcspecial does not read the simulator file. The flight software's inputs are its configuration and its flags, and a simulator's file among them would be a file a flight could be wrong about. Tcspecial's own check, of each endpoint against --simulate, is unchanged and stays the last one: only tcspecial opens the hardware.

What Each Program Does With the Mode

Tcspecial checks what it finds; tcssim checks what it has been asked to do. The two catch different mistakes, and only the first protects the hardware.

Requirement

Tcssim checks the mode before it stands in for anything:

  • Under none it refuses to start. There is nothing for it to do in a run that may have no stand-ins, and a simulator answering in place of a missing device is exactly what such a run must not have.
  • Under some, at least one payload is a stand-in and at least one is not. A payload counts as a stand-in when its simulate is true.
  • Under all, every payload is a stand-in.

A payload whose simulate is false, or that the simulator file does not name, is counted as real, which the comparison above has already held its endpoint to.

Requirement
Tcssim's check does not replace tcspecial's. It catches a simulator file that forgot a payload before anything runs. Only tcspecial's check protects the hardware, because only tcspecial opens it.
Requirement
Tcsmoc takes the mode and gives the same one to both programs it starts, so that the two cannot disagree in a run tcsmoc started. Each program run by hand is given the flag by whoever runs it, as each is given its files.
Requirement
Under none, tcsmoc starts tcspecial and not tcssim. A run that is like a flight then looks like one, with no simulator present to answer for a device that is missing.
Requirement
Tcspecial does not start tcssim, and so does not pass the mode on to it. Flight software that cannot start a simulator cannot start one by mistake, and the code for finding and launching one would be flown although it served only testing.

Waiting for the Simulator

Implemented: the messages are tcslibgs::ready, tcssim's end is tcssim/src/ready.rs, a harness waits through tcslib::simulator or python/tcslib/simulator.py, and tcsmoc and tcssim --wait are harnesses.

A stand-in has to exist before tcspecial looks for it. Tcssim makes a pseudo-terminal and links the payload's path to it, binds a socket, or loads the bank of an i2c-stub chip, and until it has, the path, the socket or the register is either missing or something that is not the stand-in. A tcspecial started first judges a payload by what is there before the simulator has put anything there, and a run that worked yesterday fails today because the two programs started in the other order. So the simulator is started first, and says when it is ready.

Requirement
When a run has stand-ins that tcssim makes, tcssim is started before tcspecial, and tcspecial is not started until tcssim has said it is ready. Whatever starts the two programs -- the test harness -- is what waits, and not tcspecial: flight software that knows nothing of a simulator cannot wait for the wrong one, or be held up by one that is not there.
Requirement
Tcssim answers on a readiness socket: a UDP address its simulator file states, in the tcssim section as ready. The address is required and has no default, so that the harness and the simulator read it from the one place and cannot each assume a different one.
Requirement
The exchange is a request and an answer, and nothing is held between exchanges. The harness sends a request carrying a sequence number; tcssim answers to the address the request came from, carrying the same number. A harness can therefore be stopped and started again, and asked as often as it likes, with nothing to reset: there is no connection to be left half open, and no reconnection to race the noticing that the old one has gone. A request that is lost is sent again, and a repeated one does no harm.
Requirement
The messages are defined in tcslibgs, beside the commands and telemetry, and a ground station waits through one function in tcslib, with the same function in the Python ground station's library. Every harness then waits the same way, and there is one statement of what the messages are.
Requirement
Tcssim makes every stand-in as it starts and keeps it until it stops. A panel's button decides only whether its payload sends: a stand-in that existed only while its panel was sending would be one tcspecial found or not by what somebody had pressed, and one tcssim could not say was ready until somebody had pressed every button.
Requirement
Tcssim binds the readiness socket before it makes any stand-in, and keeps it bound for as long as it runs. A harness that asks while the simulator is still starting is told so, rather than told nothing; and an address already taken refuses tcssim's start, naming the address. That is how a simulator left running from an earlier run is found: it holds the address, and a second simulator started beside it is refused rather than left to fight it for the payloads' paths.
Requirement

The answer is the simulator's state now, which is one of three:

  • starting: it is still making stand-ins.
  • ready: every stand-in it will provide exists and answers -- each link is made, each socket is bound or listening, and each i2c-stub chip is found and loaded. Ready says the stand-ins are there, not that any packet has gone over them.
  • failed: it could not make a stand-in, with the payload and the reason. A simulator that cannot start would otherwise look exactly like one that is slow, and the harness would learn which only when it gave up.

The state is the current one, so a stand-in that fails after the simulator was ready changes the answer to failed, and a harness that asks an hour later is told the truth.

Requirement
The answer also says what the simulator is set up for: the digest of the tcspecial configuration it read, the digest of its simulator file, and its --simulate mode. The harness compares the three with its own before it starts tcspecial, and refuses to go on when any differs, saying which and what each side has. A simulator started yesterday on another configuration, or under another mode, then stops the run before tcspecial reaches a single payload, which is the same reason CONNECT compares digests with the vehicle.
Requirement
The answer is read and written in fixed-size buffers. The reason a failed answer carries is cut short where it would not fit, as every other text the protocol carries is, so that answering is no allocation in tcssim's running loop.
Requirement
A harness waits for a time it is given and then gives up, saying what it last heard: nothing at all, which is a simulator not running or an address that is wrong, or starting, which is a simulator that has not finished. The two are different faults with different remedies.
Requirement
Tcsmoc is a harness. Under some or all, it first asks at the readiness address. When a simulator answers with the same configuration, simulator file and mode, tcsmoc uses that simulator, starts none, and leaves it running when it exits, since it did not start it; when one answers with something different, tcsmoc refuses, saying what differs; and when nothing answers, it starts tcssim and waits for it to be ready. In every case tcspecial is started only after a ready. A simulator started on its own can then outlive any number of ground station runs, and a ground station started twice by mistake does not start a second simulator.
Requirement

tcssim --wait takes the same files and the same mode as the simulator it waits for, and exits successfully once that simulator answers ready with the same digests and mode. It exits with a failure, saying why, on failed, on a difference, or when its time runs out. This is the harness for a run started by hand:

tcssim --simulate=all x.yaml x-sim.yaml &
tcssim --wait --simulate=all x.yaml x-sim.yaml &&
    tcspecial --simulate=all x.yaml
Requirement
Under none there is nothing to wait for: tcssim refuses to start, and a harness neither starts one nor asks.

tcssim

Tcssim is a GUI simulating the payloads. It reads two files. The payload definition comes from the configuration, named by the SIM_PAYLOAD_CONFIG_PATH environment variable when that is set, and says which payloads exist, how each is reached, and how big its packets are. What simulating them takes comes from the simulator file beside it, named by PAYLOAD_SIM_YAML; see Simulator Configuration Files.

Requirement
A tcspecial configuration file describes payloads and says nothing about simulating them. How often a payload produces a packet, and how a packet is divided into segments, are properties of a simulation rather than of a payload, and are stated in the simulator configuration file.

The two are joined by name: a simulated payload names the data handler it stands in for. Neither file is read in the order of the other.

Requirement
Every simulated payload names a data handler of the tcspecial configuration. A name that matches none is reported rather than simulated around, because a misspelled name is otherwise indistinguishable from a payload deliberately left out.
Requirement
A data handler the simulator file does not name is not simulated, and whether a run may have one is for the simulation mode to say: under all every payload must be a stand-in, and under none tcssim does not run. See Simulation Modes. Every handler was once required to be named, which left no way to write a configuration that is part hardware and part simulation.

Which Types Can Be Simulated

A simulated payload has to be whatever is at the far end of its handler's payload endpoint, and the types of endpoint are not equally easy to be. The experimental types are covered where each of them is: a CAN bus of peers, a sensor's buffer, a channel to another core and a set of discrete lines.

Requirement
A synchronous line is the one type nothing stands in for at its own endpoint. Every other type has something in the kernel to be -- vcan, i2c-stub, gpio-sim, iio_dummy, or a pseudo-terminal -- and generic HDLC has nothing: there is no virtual HDLC interface, so such a payload needs a card. One whose node gives a stand_in is simulated over UDP there (see Saying Which Payloads Are Simulated); one that gives none is refused where the simulator is asked to stand in for it, naming the interface and what a card would be, rather than becoming a payload that looks simulated and is not.
Requirement
A simulated payload of a network endpoint is a socket of the configured protocol, for all four of them. A stream payload listens; a datagram payload sends to where the handler waits.

The four divide in two, and which end binds divides with them. A TCP or Unix-stream payload listens at the configured address and the handler connects to it. A UDP or Unix-datagram payload that is read and not asked sends first, so the handler binds that address and the payload reaches out to it; one that is asked or driven is spoken to first, so it binds the address and the handler reaches out.

Requirement
A Unix-domain socket's address is a path, which outlives the process that bound it, so what is already at that path is looked at before binding. A socket nothing answers was left by a run that was killed rather than stopped and is replaced; a socket something answers belongs to something running and is left alone, with what is there reported; anything that is not a socket is left alone too.
Requirement
A simulated Unix-datagram payload binds a path of its own, beside the handler's, so that the handler can answer it. A datagram socket with no path of its own cannot be sent to, where a UDP one gets an address for nothing from its port. The path is derived from the handler's rather than configured, since nothing need name it: the handler learns where its payload is from the first datagram, as it does over UDP.
Requirement
A simulated payload of a device endpoint produces data and counts it. Tcspecial opens and reads the device itself, so there is nothing for the simulator to write to and the pacing is the whole of the behaviour.
Requirement
A simulated payload of a serial endpoint is a pseudo-terminal. Its slave is a device file with a line discipline behind it, so the handler opens it, sets it to the rate and framing its configuration gives, and reads and writes it as it would a real port; the simulator holds the master.

A pty is a line in every respect but the one it cannot be: the kernel accepts a data rate and ignores it, so bytes cross at memory speed whatever the configuration says. A simulated line therefore exercises the handler's reading, its writing and the terms it sets, and says nothing about whether the rate is one the cable would carry.

Requirement
The path the handler was told to open is made a symbolic link to the pty's slave for as long as the simulated payload runs, the slave's own name being the kernel's to choose. The link is removed when the payload stops.
Requirement
A path that is anything other than a link the simulator itself made is left alone, and what is there is reported. A simulator that unlinked /dev/ttyS0 to stand in for it would be worse than one that could not, and a tcspecial configuration meant for simulation can name a path the simulator may create.
Requirement
A simulated payload of an I2C endpoint is a chip of the kernel's i2c-stub. That adapter's chips are a bank of registers in memory and every master on the bus reads and writes the same bank, so the bank is the payload: the simulator writes packets into it and the handler reads them out.

Nothing in user space can answer an address on a bus, which is why this is the one type of simulated payload that is not the far end of anything. It has two consequences worth stating.

The first is that a bus is a register and not a stream. Bytes do not queue: a handler reads whatever is in the register now, so it may read one packet twice or miss one altogether, depending on how its reading falls against the packet interval. That is also true of a real sensor read over a bus, and is why a payload link over a bus is a different thing from one over a line.

The second is that the simulator does not create the bus. i2c-stub is a kernel module, loading it needs root, and the addresses its chips answer at are fixed when it is loaded.

Requirement
The simulator finds a stub bus by name among the kernel's adapters, since their numbers are assigned as they are found and a tcspecial configuration cannot know which number the stub will have. With none loaded, or with no chip at the configured address, what is reported is the command that would put it there.
Requirement
A handler asks the adapter behind its bus what transfers it can do, and uses raw I2C where that is offered and SMBus block transfers to a single register where it is not. i2c-stub has no raw transfer at all -- a plain read of it fails -- so a handler that assumed raw transfers could talk to real hardware and never to a simulated bus.

A block transfer carries at most thirty-two bytes, so a segment of a packet on a bus is capped at that: the packet size is a property of the payload and the thirty-two is a property of SMBus, and the smaller of the two wins without the file being refused.

Requirement
A simulated payload of a SPI endpoint is a pseudo-terminal, as a serial one is, and carries a peripheral's bytes and none of its clocking.

This is the weakest of the stand-ins and the one place the simulator is openly not the thing it stands for. Nothing emulates a SPI peripheral: a peripheral is what a controller clocks, and nothing in user space can be clocked. What a pty offers is the bytes -- a packet, in its segments, at its pace -- and no clock, no mode, no chip select and no word width.

Requirement
A handler takes a terminal at the end of a SPI path as a stand-in for a peripheral: it carries bytes on it, says once in the log that it is doing so, and does not apply the configured clocking to it, there being nothing to apply it to. Anything else that is not a spidev node is refused.

The narrowness is the point. A terminal is what a stand-in is, and a handler that accepted any node it could open would open a wrongly configured path confidently -- which is the fault the types were separated to prevent.

What such a link tests is a handler's reading and writing of a peripheral and the pacing of a payload that produces data in blocks. What it cannot test is whether the peripheral would stand being clocked that way, which is a question for the peripheral.

So every type of endpoint, and every protocol of the network type, can be simulated. The stand-ins are not all of one quality, and that is the thing to keep in view rather than the coverage: a socket is a socket and a pty is a line in all but its rate, while a bus is a register bank that every master shares and a SPI peripheral is a pty with no clock behind it. Each type's own file says which it is.

Requirement
A handler opens a payload device with O_NOCTTY. A device that is a terminal -- a real serial port, or a pty standing in for one -- otherwise becomes the controlling terminal of the process that opened it, and then a read from a background process group stops the handler with SIGTTIN and a hangup kills it with SIGHUP, for reasons that have nothing to do with the payload.

The window is built from the two files. Nothing in the GUI names a payload or fixes how many there are, so a payload added to or removed from the files adds or removes a panel.

Each payload occupies a portion of the window, displaying its name, configuration, and statistics. It also displays when a packet last went each way.

Requirement
A panel shows the time of the last packet sent and the time of the last packet received, and the head of each. A count says how much has moved since the payload started, and only a time says whether any of it is moving now: a payload stopped ten minutes ago and one sending every second look identical by their counts a moment after either is looked at. The bytes are what say the traffic is the traffic that was expected rather than merely traffic.
Requirement
The time is taken when the packet moves, not when the window next looks. A time stamped by the refresh would creep forward on a payload that had stopped sending, which is the fault tcsmoc's samples avoid for the same reason.
Requirement
A payload counts a packet and times it in one operation, so a count cannot move without the time moving with it.
Requirement
A direction nothing has gone in shows --:--:--, as tcsmoc's panels do. A payload that has sent nothing has no time to show, which is a different thing from one that sent something at midnight.
Requirement
A panel shows a line for a direction only where that direction can carry something. A simulated payload that sends of its own accord is never spoken to, so tcssim shows it no received line; a handler whose payload sends of its own accord is never asked anything, so tcsmoc shows it no sent line. Either would otherwise read the same from the first packet of a run to the last, which is a row of a panel spent saying that nothing can happen there.
Requirement
The line stays and the items on it go. Every panel is then the same shape, and the rows below do not move from one panel to the next -- a grid of ragged panels is harder to read across than a grid with a blank line in it.
Requirement
Every panel of a row is the same width, whatever type of payload it is for.

The items go by their text going empty rather than by each of them being conditional, and that is the whole of why. A layout whose children come and go is measured differently from one whose children are always there: with the three texts of tcsmoc's sent line behind an if, the two payloads of set 2 came out 196 and 458 pixels wide -- the triggered one taking every pixel of slack in the row and the other sitting at its minimum, which says nothing about either payload and reads as a window that has gone wrong. Empty text draws nothing, so the line still shows no label and no reading. A test measures the widths of a mixed row and of a row of each type alone.

Requirement
A payload keeps the same sample of a transfer that a data handler keeps: the time, the head of the data, and the whole length. The two programs show the one transfer from opposite ends of a link, so a payload keeping something else would have the two panels disagree about what moved.
Requirement
Both programs show a time and a sample the same way: the time of day, in UTC, with the date and the fraction of a second dropped, and the bytes as hex pairs with an ellipsis where the transfer was longer than the sample. Tcsmoc's panels and tcssim's are read side by side while a link is being watched, so one of them showing local time would make a transfer look an hour old, and a byte written differently would look like a difference in the data. The formatting is one function both call.

The packet size and interval can be changed, as can the segment size and interval. Each starts at what the files gave it: the packet size from the tcspecial configuration, the rest from the simulator file.

Requirement
A segment is a piece of a packet, so a segment larger than the packet is refused. Equal is the ordinary case -- a packet sent in one segment, which is what a simulator file stating no segment size asks for -- so only a segment strictly larger than the packet is wrong.
Requirement
A pair of sizes that cannot be sent is said at once and the button that would start the payload is taken away until they can be. Either alone would be worse: a message with the button still there invites a press that does something nobody asked for, and a button that had quietly stopped working would leave someone pressing it and wondering.
Requirement
What is said names both sizes and both ways out of it. The two spin boxes are side by side and either can be the one that was meant to change, so a message naming only one of them answers half the question.
Requirement
The message is taken back by the edit that makes the sizes sendable again. An open popup still saying the sizes cannot be sent, after they can, is the wrong answer left on the screen.
Requirement
The two sizes are checked when the files are read, not only when a spin box is touched. A simulator file may state a segment larger than the packet the tcspecial configuration gives, and a panel that said nothing until it was edited would have the payload refuse to start for no stated reason.
Requirement
The window decides neither. It asks for the two sizes to be judged and obeys the answer -- showing what it says and disabling the button while it says anything -- so there is one rule rather than one in each place that acts on it.

Each panel has two buttons: one that starts and stops the payload, and one that shows the whole of what the two files said about it.

Requirement
The button that starts and stops a payload is labelled with what pressing it will do. A payload that is sending offers Disable, which stops it; a payload that is not offers Enable, which starts it. There were two buttons, Start and Stop, and either was pressable whatever the payload was doing: a Stop on a payload that was not sending did nothing and read as though it had.
Requirement
One button here where the MOC's panels have two, because a press in this window is the act itself: it starts or stops a payload in this same program, so it cannot be lost and there is never a reason to make it twice. The status the label is built from is this program's own knowledge of its own payload, so the press it offers is the press that is wanted. The MOC presses across a link to a spacecraft, where a command may not arrive and may have to be sent again; see the MOC's panels for why that takes two buttons.
Requirement
Both windows label those two presses the same way. Enable and Disable in the MOC's panels and in the simulator's, so that one state is said one way in the two windows an operator reads side by side. The simulator said Send and Silent -- the payload's own words, the payload being the thing that sends -- which read well in one window alone and left the reader of both translating.
Requirement
Which way a press goes is decided by the status the label was made from. The window builds the label and the program decides what to do, so the two are checked against each other: a button that offered one thing and did the other would be worse than either button alone.
Requirement
A payload that failed to start offers Enable again. It is not sending, which is what its status says, and the useful thing to offer is the start that failed rather than a stop of something that never began.
Requirement
Each panel has a button that shows every parameter read from the two configuration files for that payload, in both programs. What a panel has room for is a name, a size and what has moved; a payload set says a good deal more than that -- a trigger, a line rate, a slave address, a clock mode, a fault -- and until this there was nowhere to see it but the files themselves, which is no use for telling what the program actually read.
Requirement
That button is labelled Configuration in both programs and sits to the right of the button that starts and stops the payload. The press a panel is opened to make comes first, and one word for one button means an operator reading the two windows side by side is not told that a Config and a Configuration are the same thing. The MOC's was abbreviated while its panel was narrower than the word; the panel is wider now, which is what that cost.
Requirement
Both halves are shown and each is said to come from the file it came from. One is flight configuration, which tcspecial serves and the ground controls; the other is how a stand-in behaves and reaches no spacecraft at all. A list that mixed them would have someone reading a drop percentage as something a payload does.
Requirement
Nothing shown is a measurement. What a payload has done is on the panel itself, and a list that mixed the two would leave no way to tell a configured value from an observed one.
Requirement
Only the attributes of the payload's own type are shown, and only the faults that were asked for. A device has no protocol and a bus no port; six faults of nought say less about a payload than the one that was set.
Requirement
A value the files did not state is shown as what the program will use instead, said to be that rather than passed off as a statement. A handler with no OC address says it cannot be started, since that is what the absence means.
Requirement
Both programs describe a payload with one function. They show the same payload set from opposite ends of a link, so two descriptions would be two things to keep in step and a difference between them would read as a difference in the configuration.
Requirement
Tcsmoc reads the simulator configuration to show it, and does not require it. The MOC controls payloads and simulates nothing, so a simulator file that is missing, unreadable, or written for another tcspecial configuration costs it that half of what a panel can show and nothing else; it says so once on the way past, and the panel says so where the parameters are shown.

The panels are laid out in a grid whose shape follows from how many there are. A row is filled before another is started under it, three panels across, which is the rule tcsmoc follows and for the same reasons given there.

It replaced one that kept the window wider than tall and chose the shape nearest square among those that were. The claim here used to be that the two rules came to the same thing for the counts the shipped sets have. They did not: five payloads went into two columns and three rows, because a 680x620 window is squarer than a 1062x476 one, so the set went down the window instead of across it. Squareness was never the thing wanted either -- it was a proxy for a window that fits a screen, and a column count says that directly.

How many columns there are is worked out in the window rather than handed over by Rust, from the width the window has at the time, so dragging a window wider reflows the grid into the room that appeared. Rust opens the window wide enough for the columns the payload count calls for, and because it opens it at exactly that many panels' width plus the chrome, the window arrives at the same count.

The five sources of the thermostat example give a row of three and a row of two in a 1206x668 window. The size is the one window_size gives, which a test reads out of this document so that the number here cannot quietly stop being the one the program opens at.

tcsmoc

The tcsmoc is a GUI program used to control simulated payloads interacting with tcspecial using the tcslib library over a datagram connection to tcspecial. For testing purposes, tcsmoc uses tcslib, along with simulated payloads, to support a simple GUI.

Tcsmoc gets the payload definition from a file named on its command line, and reads the shipped fixture when the command line names none. It takes an argument rather than an environment variable because it starts tcspecial and tcssim as subprocesses and they inherit its environment: a variable naming tcsmoc's file would name theirs too, and so could not point tcsmoc at one file and its children at another. An argument belongs to tcsmoc alone.

Requirement
Tcsmoc accepts one tcspecial configuration file. A second path is an error rather than an argument with no effect, because it is more likely a mistake about which file is being read than something meant to be ignored.

The beacon box carries the indicator and, beside it, the time the last beacon arrived.

Requirement
The time the last beacon arrived is shown, and is the time it arrived at the MOC rather than the spacecraft time the beacon carries. The line is read to find out whether beacons are still coming, so a spacecraft whose clock had stopped must not look as though its beacons had. It is shown the way every other time in the window is, by the one function that shows them.
Requirement
The time survives the passes that receive nothing, and a socket error as well. The colour beside it is what ages; the beacon arrived when it arrived, and blanking the line on a timeout would say that none ever had.
Requirement
Until a beacon has arrived the line says so, in the words a panel uses for a direction that has carried nothing. It was this and nothing else for the life of the program before the time was ever set: the window declared the line, the receiver set the colour beside it, and the line itself said no beacon had arrived while the colour said they were arriving steadily.
Requirement
The beacons also say whether the spacecraft is being heard from at all, which the panels act on. Contact is held for as long as the last beacon's age is within the leading run of stages that count as being heard from -- blinking or steady, an indicator that has begun to blink being beacons that are arriving and aging rather than gone -- and is lost past that run, when no beacon has ever arrived, and when the system clock has moved so that the last one is in the future. One reading serves both the indicator and the panels, so the two cannot say different things about one beacon.
Requirement
The indicator is grey before any beacon has arrived, and never again after one has. Wherever it shows no colour once a beacon has arrived -- the dark half of a blink, a last beacon a moved clock puts in the future -- it is white. Grey says nothing has been heard; an indicator that went from green back to grey would say the spacecraft had never been heard from, and look like a window that had just opened. tcsui::indicator is the rule, and the Python panel says it again with the same tests.

Where the beacon's code lives

Not here. A beacon is the one thing a ground station knows without asking, so every ground station wants it, and what it means does not depend on the window it is shown in.

Requirement
What a beacon is and what has become of it is a library's: the socket, the datagram, the digest comparison, the beacons that did not arrive and how stale the last one is. No toolkit is linked into it, because a ground station with no screen -- a logger, a test, a program that only watches -- needs the facts and not the look. It is tcslib::beacon.
Requirement
How stale the last beacon is, is a named stage and not a colour. A stage is what is true of the spacecraft; a colour is one way of saying it, and the stages are what both the indicator and the panels are worked out from. The stages and their timings are one schedule, which is the library's, so two ground stations watching one spacecraft cannot differ about how late a beacon is.
Requirement
The panel is shared markup, imported by every window that shows it, with what to show on it worked out beside it: tcsui. Two windows show it -- this one, beside its command link, and the beacon example, which shows it and nothing else -- and an operator who has read one has read the other, where an indicator in a different place or a label in different words would be two things to learn about one spacecraft.
Requirement
What is left in a ground program is the wiring: read the configuration to find where the beacons are, run the loop on a thread, put each pass in the window. The beacon example is that and nothing else, which is what makes it worth reading.
Requirement
A panel starts unknown and stays unknown until somebody asks. A MOC that has just opened has been told nothing about any handler, so a panel claiming anything is a panel claiming what nobody said: it claimed Stopped, and was commonly wrong, since the MOC attaches to a tcspecial that is already serving the set rather than starting a second one, and that one's handlers may have been moving data for hours. Enable, Disable and Query all are what ask.
Requirement
A panel's status is what a START_NODE or STOP_NODE was answered, and the beacons are what can take it away. Beacons stopping makes every panel's status unknown: the status is the answer to a command and is worth as much as the ground's hearing from the spacecraft, where a node may since have stopped or gone with a process that restarted, and a panel still claiming a state would be read as a reading. It is said once, when contact is lost, rather than every pass of the silence.
Requirement
The panels are made unknown whether or not there is a command link. A MOC that has been disconnected cannot find out either, and the beacon is what it has to go on.
Requirement
The spacecraft is asked what its handlers are doing at two moments, and only two: when the first beacon arrives, and when a beacon outage ends. Both are a MOC that has just learnt the spacecraft is there and knows nothing about its payloads -- it may have been serving them for hours, or have restarted during the silence with none of them running.
Requirement
A payload whose last press was Disable is not asked about. The operator said to stop it; asking the spacecraft would spend a command to be told what the ground already knows, and a panel saying Stopped because somebody stopped it is not a panel with a question hanging over it. An Enable clears that, a Disable sets it, and a payload nobody has pressed anything about is asked.
Requirement
The asking happens off the event loop, and what comes back is painted by the next pass of the refresh. Each question waits on the spacecraft for as long as the timeout allows, and a burst of them on the event loop would stop the window repainting at exactly the moment an operator is watching it.

Nothing is asked while the command link is down, and the question is not held until it comes up: the panels keep saying unknown until contact is made again or somebody presses Query all. Contact returning while the link is down is rare -- the beacon and the link come back together -- and a question owed indefinitely is a question that goes out at a moment nobody chose.

Requirement

The ground does not ask about its payloads on a timer. It asks at the two moments above and when somebody presses Query all, and otherwise watches what arrives.

An enabled payload sends on its own -- that is what enabling it does -- and every datagram it sends arrives on that payload's own telemetry socket, where the ground times it. So the ground has what it needs to say whether a payload is still delivering without asking anybody.

There was a poller: a thread that asked every payload what it had moved once a second and painted the panels from the answers. It cost a command and a response per payload per second -- for forty payloads, eighty datagrams a second of housekeeping on the link the spacecraft is also commanded over -- and most of what it learnt the arrivals had already said. It was also the MOC's second sender of payload commands, which is why the ground's end of that link could not be a socket the configuration names.

Requirement
A panel says when its source's data last arrived and nothing more about whether that was recent enough. Nothing in a configuration states how often a node's data should arrive -- a thermostat's heaters hear from it when a decision changes, which may be hours apart -- so an indicator that judged it would be an indicator whose colour means nothing, and one that never changes is worse than none.
Requirement
Query all still asks the spacecraft directly, and sets both the status and the counters from what it answers. It is the one path by which the spacecraft's own account of a handler -- its state, its byte counters, the triggers it has sent -- reaches a panel, and somebody pressing it is somebody who wants exactly that.

Every program is pointed at its tcspecial configuration the same way: as a command line argument, failing that the program's own environment variable -- PAYLOAD_CONFIG_PATH for tcspecial and SIM_PAYLOAD_CONFIG_PATH for tcssim, while tcsmoc has none for the reason below -- and failing that the shipped fixture. Tcssim names its simulator configuration separately again, through PAYLOAD_SIM_YAML.

Requirement
An argument naming the tcspecial configuration takes precedence over any environment variable naming one. The argument is the unambiguous of the two: tcsmoc starts the other programs and they inherit its environment, so a variable can reach further than it was meant to, where an argument reaches exactly the program it is given to.
Requirement
One configuration file is named, and no other file. A further path is an error rather than one with no effect, because it is more likely a mistake about which file is being read than something meant to be ignored. A flag is not a path: tcspecial's --quiet and --types (see Which Node Types Are Built), and the --simulate all three programs take (see Simulation Modes), say how a program runs and not what it reads.

Where the Addresses Are

Requirement
No address appears on tcspecial's command line. The configuration file is the only thing it names, and its flags say how it runs, not where anything is.
Requirement
The address and ports the command interpreter binds are in the configuration's tcspecial section, so one file says where commands are taken and what is flown.
Requirement

The command interpreter takes commands on two links: one for the spacecraft and one for every node. A command about the spacecraft -- PING, RESTART_ARM, RESTART, CONFIGURE -- arrives on tcs_cmd.recv; a command about a node -- START_NODE, STOP_NODE, SET_NODE, QUERY_NODE, QUERY_NODE_SAMPLE -- arrives on payload_command.dest_base, with nothing added to it, and is answered there. CONNECT is the spacecraft's: it asks whether there is a connection to the vehicle, which is what that link is, and a ground station without one has nothing to ask a node. A CONNECT that arrives on the node link is refused like any other command on the wrong one.

One socket for every node: a node command carries the node's id, so the socket does not have to say which node a command is about.

Requirement
Each payload's link is served by a loop in a thread of its own, and every socket is bound before any loop runs -- so a port already in use is reported at startup, naming the payload whose link wanted it.

Separate links because what the ground needs in a hurry must never be queued behind what it asked for at leisure. A RESTART waiting behind a payload's statistics is a spacecraft that cannot be rescued while it is busy, and the two types of command are unlike in every way that matters: one is rare, urgent, and about the vehicle, the other routine, bulky, and about an instrument. Missions divide them for the same reason, often between different operators with different authority. One link per payload divides them again, for a smaller version of the same reason: a payload that takes a second to start should not hold up a question about one that would answer at once.

On a space link the division is by virtual channel, which is where the priority between them is really decided and where a command link gets COP-1's sequence control. Over IP there is no such thing, so it is two ports -- what an IP network has to divide traffic with. A payload set that is flown rather than tested would map tcs_cmd.recv to the command virtual channel and the payload links to payload ones.

Requirement
One table says which link a command belongs on, read by the ground when it sends and by the spacecraft when it answers: CommandType::link. The two ends cannot come to disagree about where a command belongs, which is a disagreement nothing could report except as a command that went unanswered.
Requirement
The table is a match over every type of command, not a rule with a default. A command added later is placed deliberately, by someone who has to decide, rather than by whichever side of a default it fell on.
Requirement
Both ports are required of every command interpreter configuration, with no default, as the beacon attributes are: where commands are taken is not a question to be answered by whatever a file was silently given.
Requirement
The two ports are two ports. A configuration giving both the same number describes a split it has not made, and the half of it that bound second would fail at startup with the other half's address.
Requirement
No payload may bind a port a command link binds. It is the one collision that does not stop a payload from working -- it stops the commanding that would have started it -- and which of the two fails depends on the order the programs happened to start in. A command link bound to every interface, which 0.0.0.0 is, takes its port on every address, so a payload at localhost:4000 collides with a link at 0.0.0.0:4000 without either naming the other's host.
Requirement
Each link is served by a loop of its own, in a thread of its own. Two sockets read by one loop are two queues into one bottleneck: a payload command that takes its time -- a START_NODE opens the node, which may be a device -- would still be ahead of whatever the ground sent next. The split is only worth having if what it separates cannot wait for the other.
Requirement
Both sockets are bound as the interpreter is created, before either loop runs, so a port already in use is reported where every other configuration error is -- at startup, naming itself -- rather than when the first command of that type arrives.
Requirement
Every link takes what arrives only from where the configuration says it comes from, and ignores the rest. tcspecial takes a spacecraft command only from tcs_cmd.send, which is the socket the MOC binds to send them; the MOC takes a response only from the socket its command went to, which is tcs_cmd.recv; it takes a beacon only from beacon.send; and it takes a payload's telemetry only from payload_telemetry.source_base counted on by that payload's id. Where the configuration names every interface -- 0.0.0.0, which the vehicle's side of every link usually is -- the port is all there is to compare, the address a datagram carries then being whichever interface the route chose.

This is what writing both ends of every link down buys. A command is a command to restart a spacecraft, and a response is what the ground believes about one: before this, either would be acted on whoever sent it, which a bound port invites -- another program on the host, a stale process from an earlier run, a packet from the wrong mission on a shared network. Nothing is sent back to such a sender, either; an answer would tell it that something is listening.

The dropped datagram is said on the console, with where it came from and where the configuration expected it from. A link that is deaf because two files disagree looks exactly like a link that is deaf because nothing is sending, and the two have nothing in common to do about them.

Requirement
A command that arrives on the link it does not belong on is refused with InvalidCommand, and the refusal is of the telemetry type the command asks for. The type is how the ground pairs an answer with what it asked, so a refusal it cannot place reads as a command that went unanswered -- which is the one thing a refusal exists to deny.

Serving it instead would be worse than refusing it. A command on the wrong link means one end is not reading the configuration the other is, and that is exactly the fault this split must not hide: the link the ground restarts the spacecraft over is the last place to learn that the two ends disagree about where commands go.

Requirement
The ground sends each command on the link the table names. A client holds a connection to each, and which one a command goes on is read from the same CommandType::link the spacecraft refuses it by, so the ground cannot spend a round trip learning what it already knows.
Requirement
A client has both links or neither. One would send only half of what there is to send, and the half it could not send is the half the panels use.
Requirement

The ground asks CONNECT on the spacecraft's link alone. It is the question "is there a connection to the vehicle", and the vehicle's own link is where that is asked; a payload's link carries what is asked of a payload.

What this gives up is a check: two links pointed at two spacecraft is the one way this arrangement can be wrong that nothing else catches -- each link works, each answer looks right, and the payloads being commanded are not the ones being restarted. It was caught by asking both links and comparing the answers. Catching it again means a payload command's answer carrying what that end read, which is where to put it if it is wanted: on the link whose correctness is in question, in an answer the ground already waits for.

Requirement
The MOC takes the payload port from the command interpreter's own configuration file, which is where tcspecial takes it from. The host is the operator's -- it is what the address box says, and a bind address of 0.0.0.0 is no destination -- so moving the link moves both of them by typing one address.
Requirement
tcsverify reports a missing or colliding port from the line that states it.
Requirement
A beacon is sent from one stated socket to another. Tcspecial makes the socket it sends beacons on out of beacon.send -- that is the address and port it binds, so every beacon leaves from there -- and sends to beacon.recv. Tcsmoc makes the socket it receives beacons on out of beacon.recv, binding the same address and port. Each is an address and a port.

A beacon was sent to a multicast group, with a second attribute naming the local interface to join it on. That bought something real: a beacon is an announcement to whoever is listening, the spacecraft does not know how many ground stations there are, and multicast is the primitive for one-to-many with no knowledge of the many. A unicast beacon reaches one listener, chosen in advance by whoever wrote the configuration, a port being bindable once.

What it cost was two attributes that had to be right and said nothing when they were not. The group address is nobody's, so neither end could be reached at it to see whether the other was there. The interface attribute decided delivery and could not be defaulted: a sender bound to every interface sends a multicast datagram out the default route, a listener that joins with INADDR_ANY joins on the default route, and on a host where those differ -- one with a second interface, or a tunnel -- not one beacon arrives with nothing anywhere to say so. Measured on a development machine with three interfaces: of the four combinations of naming and not naming, only naming the same interface at both ends delivered anything. A mission whose beacons stop is a mission that cannot tell a silent spacecraft from a misjoined socket.

Two stated sockets trade the many for the saying. Each end is an address and a port somebody wrote down, which means a firewall rule, a capture filter or a colleague can describe the link beforehand, and either end can be probed. More than one ground station listening is then something configured -- a relay that fans the beacons out -- rather than something the network did silently.

Requirement
Every configuration states where beacons come from, where they are received, and how often: beacon.send, beacon.recv and beacon.interval, in its tcspecial section. None has a default and none may be left out.
Requirement
How often is a length of time, like every other length of time in these files: 5 s rather than 5000. The protocol still counts milliseconds, the command that changes the interval carrying four bytes of them, so the conversion happens where the file is read and in one place: CIConfigJson::to_ci_config.

Beacons are how the ground knows the spacecraft is alive. A configuration that has not been asked where to send them has not answered, and a default is a way of finding that out later -- by the beacons arriving somewhere nobody is listening, which looks from the ground exactly like a spacecraft that has stopped.

Requirement

Tcsmoc reads both beacon addresses from the configuration tcspecial reads, through the one function that answers it: graph::parameters::beacon_of. The two programs read one file, so they cannot name different addresses.

One file states them, so there is no second answer to resolve against and no rule about which wins.

That is what makes the earlier drift impossible rather than merely unlikely. The MOC used to bind a compiled-in address and read neither file, so a mission that moved the beacon moved it for tcspecial alone and the MOC said only that nothing was arriving. A MOC that cannot work out where to listen says so and runs without beacons, as it does without a simulator file: controlling payloads does not depend on it.

Requirement
A beacon.send or beacon.recv that is not an address and a port is refused where it is read, naming the attribute.

Refused there rather than where a beacon is sent, because a beacon goes out on a timer with nobody to report to: the address used to be a constant parsed with unwrap, which could only ever have panicked, and a configured one has to be caught while someone is still reading errors.

The command address was an argument for a while, and tcsmoc passed the address it was about to send to, so that the end that listens and the end that sends could not differ. One address in one place is the simpler rule, and it is the rule now; what it gives up is that agreement. The MOC and a tcspecial it starts agree about the command address by reading the same default, and about the beacon address the same way, so a tcspecial.yaml that moves either moves it for tcspecial alone -- commands then go to a port nothing is bound to, which no error on either end reports, since a UDP datagram sent where nobody is listening is not refused. What the two ends do check on connecting is each other's configuration; see What Each End Read.

Requirement
Tcspecial says both addresses as it starts, on stderr, whether or not anything was asked of the log, and names the file they came from.

Said on the way past rather than only when something fails, and said before either socket is made, so that a failure to bind follows the address it was trying to bind. They are the addresses the ground has to be pointed at, and until this was printed, finding out what a running tcspecial had bound meant asking the operating system.

Requirement
Tcsmoc passes its own configuration file to each program it starts, as that program's argument. Tcsmoc builds its panels from that file, so a child reading a different one would serve or simulate payloads the panels do not describe: handlers that never connect, with nothing on screen to say why.
Requirement
Tcsmoc does not start a tcspecial when one is already running. It asks by PING, and attaches to the one that answers instead of starting a second.

Starting a second never worked: the command interpreter's address can be bound once, so the second exits and tcsmoc comes up with a window, no spacecraft behind it, and the reason on a terminal nobody is reading. Asking by PING rather than by looking for a process or a bound port is deliberate -- what matters is whether a command interpreter answers, where a bound port might be anything and a process of that name might be wedged -- and it finds a tcspecial that is not a local process at all.

Requirement
Tcsmoc stops only what it started. A tcspecial it attached to rather than started is left running when tcsmoc closes: something else started it, something else may still be using it, and shutting down a command interpreter because a ground display was closed is not the display's decision.
Requirement
A START_NODE for a node tcspecial does not have says which id came in, how many nodes this tcspecial does serve, and that the two ends are reading different configurations.

Attaching is what makes this worth saying. A tcspecial left running from an earlier payload set is attached to rather than replaced, and it goes on serving the set it was started with while the panels in front of the operator are the new set's -- so a payload the configuration plainly contains is answered NotFound. The command carries the payload's name, which put the name in the answer and made it read as a name that had been looked up and missed; nothing is looked up by name, the id is what addresses a handler, and the file tcspecial read twenty hours ago is the fact that explains it. A status cannot carry that, so the log says it.

An argument is what makes this possible. Tcsmoc has no variable of its own because its children inherit its environment: a variable naming tcsmoc's file would name theirs as well, and could not point one at a different file from the other. An argument reaches exactly the program it is given to, and beats anything that program would otherwise have taken from the environment, so for the run of a payload set tcsmoc's file is the one that counts.

Tcsmoc builds each child's command without running it, so what a child would be started with is checked by a test rather than by starting it: the programs open windows and bind fixed ports, which makes running them a poor way to test anything.

The one file tcsmoc does not pass on is tcssim's simulator configuration. Tcssim takes it from PAYLOAD_SIM_YAML, inherited from tcsmoc's environment like any other variable, and so is already looking at the file tcsmoc was pointed at. Tcsmoc reads that file as well, but only to show what it says; it acts on none of it and so has nothing to say about which one is right.

The Makefile names a set once for this reason, and names it as a stem: TCSPECIAL_CONFIG_FILE is the set, $(TCSPECIAL_CONFIG_FILE).yaml the tcspecial configuration that reaches every program as its argument, and $(TCSPECIAL_CONFIG_FILE)sim.yaml the simulator file that reaches tcssim in the environment. One target then runs a whole set, and its last letter is the spelling of the configuration the MOC reads -- runmocy the YAML and runmocx the XML -- both adding the suffix to the same stem, so they are one set read two ways:

$ make runmocy
$ make runmocy TCSPECIAL_CONFIG_FILE=tests/manual/thermostat
$ make runmocy TCSPECIAL_CONFIG_FILE=tests/manual/tcspecial2
Requirement
The two files of a set are named by one variable rather than two. Naming one of a pair is how the two come to describe different sets, which is reported rather than run -- every data handler of the tcspecial configuration must have a simulated payload naming it -- but it is reported by tcssim, after tcsmoc has already come up, which makes it a confusing way to find out.

The sets live under tests/manual rather than at the repository root because running one is something a person does at a terminal: each opens sockets or device nodes, so none of them belongs in a test that runs on every build. The tests that read them only parse them.

make run runs tcspecial alone and make runsim the simulator alone, both from the same stem -- tcssim run by itself needs the tcspecial configuration as well as the simulator file, and one variable is what keeps the two from being given for different sets:

$ make run TCSPECIAL_CONFIG_FILE=tests/manual/thermostat
$ make runsim TCSPECIAL_CONFIG_FILE=tests/manual/thermostat
$ make runmocy TCSPECIAL_CONFIG_FILE=tests/manual/thermostat

Started in that order, the three run as separately as they can: tcsmoc finds the tcspecial already there, attaches to it rather than starting a second, and leaves it running when its window closes. Which is what one program at a time is for -- a debugger on the one being worked on, and the others left alone.

make help lists the targets, and RUST_LOG reaches every one of them:

$ make runmocy RUST_LOG=debug
$ make run TCSPECIAL_CONFIG_FILE=tests/manual/thermostat RUST_LOG=debug

The manual says the same thing from the other end -- what to type to run a set -- under "Running the Programs".

The window is built from that file. Nothing in the GUI names a data handler or fixes how many there are, so a source added to or removed from the configuration adds or removes a rectangle.

Requirement
Panels are laid out across the window before they are laid out down it, three to a row, and a fourth starts a row under the first.
Requirement
The MOC opens the payload data path of every payload that has a ground link: one socket per payload, bound where payload_telemetry.dest_base counted on by that payload's id says the ground's end is, which reads what arrives and shows the latest in the panel with the time it arrived. It sends nothing.
Requirement
tcspecial sends a payload's telemetry to the end the configuration names -- payload_telemetry.dest_base counted on by the payload's id -- and not to wherever that socket was last spoken to from. A payload's own socket still learns its peer, because the payload is the end that speaks first over UDP and no configuration says where it will speak from; the ground link is the one whose far end is written down.

Before the ground's end was named there was a hello: an empty datagram from the MOC to every payload's handler, once a second, because the handler learnt where to answer from what arrived and with nobody there it had no peer at all -- every write to the ground failed, and a payload's data went into a handler and no further. Once a second because starting or stopping a payload rebound that socket and the learnt peer went with it, so a ground station that said hello once was silently cut off by the next Enable, with nothing anywhere to say so.

Naming both ends removed all of it: the hello, that silence, and the last traffic the MOC sent without being asked. What remains of the reason the listener exists is the other half of it -- every reading in a panel used to be the spacecraft's own account of itself, which is worth having and is not evidence that anything reached the ground.

Requirement
The window asks to be put against the top of the display, and tcssim's against the bottom. The two are read together -- what the ground sees of a payload and what the payload is doing about it -- and left where a window manager puts them they land on top of each other.
Requirement
It is a request and nothing depends on it. X11 honours a client's position; Wayland leaves placement to the compositor and may ignore it, and a tiling window manager places windows as it sees fit. A window that lands elsewhere is a window in the wrong place, not a program that failed.
Requirement
The display's height is read from /sys/class/drm: the tallest connected output's first mode. Nothing can know which display the windows opened on, and the tallest is the one that makes a window asking for the bottom land on a display rather than above it. With no height to be had, neither window asks for anything.
Requirement
The position is asked for once the event loop is running, because that is when a window has a size. Before it is shown it reports what was requested rather than what it was given, and the bottom of the display is the display's height less the window's.
Requirement
The window opens wide enough for the row.

It already did. Three columns come to 798 pixels including the margins, which is wider than the window's own width floor: the command interpreter's controls need 700, and the floor is at least what two rows of panels stand, so that a two-row grid is wider than it is tall. That was not obvious before the panels were measured: the constant the window width was worked out from said a panel was 320 wide, with a comment claiming the layout stretched them to fill it, and a panel is as wide as its widest row and does not stretch. Three columns of 320 would have opened a 960-wide window with a third of it empty to the right of the panels.

A panel's widest row is its three buttons, which is why spelling Config out as Configuration moved the window: 206 pixels became 256, and three columns 644 became 798. A row of buttons that does not fit keeps them at their own width and hangs the last one over the panel's edge onto the panel beside it, so a test holds every button to the panel that contains it and not merely to the window.

So the shape follows from the count alone: three handlers are one row of three, and the five of the shipped thermostat are a row of three and a row of one, in a 977x1068 window. A test reads that size out of this document and checks it against the one the program opens at, as one does for tcssim.

This replaced a rule that chose the shape nearest square among those at least as wide as they were tall. It read well and laid the panels out badly: two panels came out as one column of two, because a 739x739 window is squarer than a 739x549 one, so the set the Makefile runs went down the window instead of across it. Which row and column each panel is given was row-major all along -- the shape was the whole of the fault. Squareness was never the thing wanted either; it was a proxy for a window that fits a screen, and a column count says that directly.

What is given up is that the grid is no longer always wider than it is tall. Seven panels or more take a third row, which is 929 against a 798-wide window; they scroll, as too many panels always have. Up to six it still is, and a test says so. The window the MOC opens has been taller than wide since it gained its three-row floor, which is a different thing: the floor is room to grow into.

A test measures where the panels land in a laid-out window rather than reading the arithmetic back, because the arithmetic was not the part that was wrong.

The GUI has a section at the top of its single window for commanding the spacecraft and reading what it answers, with the beacon's panel beside it. Below that is a rectangle for every source the configuration sends to the ground, each showing the node's name, what it is, where its data arrives and what has arrived -- whether or not anything has, because what a panel is for is comparing a node against the file that describes it.

It shows no packet interval: that is a simulator setting, stated in the simulator file and shown by tcssim.

The two lines of what arrived are the ground's own reading: when something last came from that source and the head of it, counted from the moment the MOC started. A source that has sent nothing shows --:--:-- and no data, which is what a panel says before anything arrives.

What a panel shows is what reached the ground, which is the thing an operator is watching the window for.

Requirement
A panel says which payload of the configuration it is, beside the name: auto-send (dev id 0). The number is the payload's place in the payload configuration, counted from nought in the order the file describes them, and nothing in the file states it -- a payload stated a dh_id once and that was a second identity to keep consistent by hand. Both windows count the same way, which is the use of it: the two are read side by side, and a payload is then the same number in each.

Each panel has three buttons: Enable, Disable, and Configuration, which shows what the configuration files said about the handler and is described under tcssim where the same button is. It read Config while the panel was narrower than the word.

Requirement
A panel has a button per command, each labelled with the command it sends. Enable sends START_NODE and Disable sends STOP_NODE, with Enable first: that is the press a panel is opened to make, and the status beside the name is what says which of the two has taken effect.
Requirement
Both are pressable whatever the node is doing. tcspecial answers a START_NODE for a node already running and a STOP_NODE for one already stopped without harm, and what an operator wants when a panel looks wrong is what the spacecraft says to the command -- which a button that cannot be pressed does not say.

There was one button, labelled with the one thing worth doing next: Discard for a handler that was moving data and Receive for one that was not. It had to be read both ways -- Slint built the label from the status and the program decided the command from the same status -- so the two had to be held to one rule or they would drift into offering one thing and doing the other. Two buttons have nothing to drift: a press is the command on the label. What a panel gives up is that it no longer suggests which press to make, and what it gains is that either can be made without first getting the handler into the state that offers it.

Requirement
The MOC has a button per command where the simulator has one button for both, and the difference is the link each press crosses. Pressing a button in tcssim starts or stops a payload in that same program: the press is the act, it cannot be lost, and one button labelled with what it will do says everything there is to say. A press in the MOC sends a datagram to a spacecraft, which may not arrive -- so the same START_NODE or STOP_NODE may have to be sent again, and again, before tcspecial has it. A single button offering the press that suits the status cannot be pressed twice: the status it reads is the ground's own guess about a handler it may not have reached, and the press worth making is exactly the one such a button refuses. Two buttons, both always pressable, are a command an operator can repeat.
Requirement
The panels are refreshed without being asked. A handler moving data must not look like one doing nothing, which is what a panel that changed only when a button was pressed showed.
Requirement
Asking a handler what to show does not happen on the window's own thread. Each command waits on the spacecraft for as long as the client's timeout allows, and a window that stops repainting while tcspecial is slow to answer is a worse fault than a panel a second out of date.
Requirement
