A panel shows what arrived from its payload, the time it arrived, and how many packets have arrived -- and not the spacecraft's account of what it last sent and last received, nor its count of the bytes it sent to the payload. Three rows and a counter carried that account and are gone: a panel is watched for what reached the ground, and the claim about it took a third of the panel's height to say nearly the same thing. The spacecraft's own sample is not asked for any more either -- a command a second per node whose answer nothing showed.
Requirement
All three of those readings are kept by the end that received them, which is the ground. The count was taken from the spacecraft's statistics instead, and from the wrong half of them: what a handler had received was what it read from the ground, which is the direction nobody sends in, so a panel beside a payload sending steadily read 0 bytes. The listener that already kept the time and the data counts the packets as it receives them.
Requirement
The window says when the last command went out and when its answer came back, under Last Response. A response that is slow to come and one that never comes read alike until those two times are beside it.
Requirement
Both are the ground's own clock, and they are the window link's own commands -- what the operator pressed -- not the panels' polling. The times are read from the client that sends them, which is the one place every command passes, so a press that forgot to record one cannot happen.

Testing requires starting up tcssim before other operations and shutting it down when tcsmoc is halted.

tcsverify

Tcsverify reads a configuration -- the file itself, and the simulator file beside it when one is given -- and prints what is wrong with it. It opens no socket and no device node, so it can be run anywhere, by a person editing a file or by a recipe: it is given the files to read and says nothing when they are good.

Requirement
Tcsverify takes one file or two. The first is the tcspecial configuration file and the second, if there is one, the simulator configuration. Which they are follows from the order they are given in and not from their names, a set being free to name its files as it likes.
Requirement
Every problem is printed as the file it is in, the line of that file, which kind it is, and what is wrong: file:line: error: message or file:line: warning: message. The shape an editor jumps to and a build log is read with. Both kinds say which they are, rather than only the warnings: a report where an unlabelled line had to be worked out would be a report read wrongly.
Requirement

A problem is an error where the configuration says something wrong -- two nodes of one name, a link to a node that is not there, a link carrying one stream into the other, a node of a type that does not exist -- and a warning where it has not finished saying it: a node whose attributes are not all in yet, a node nothing is linked to. One statement of which is which, tcslibgs::graph::check::Problem::severity, so that the verifier, the editor and the flight software agree about what a half-built graph is.

A type that does not exist is an error and not an unfinished attribute, because no amount of finishing makes it work: it is most likely a misspelling, and the flight software would have nothing to open it as. The types are tcslibgs::graph::types_in, one list per section, which the configuration schemas beside tcsgui are tested against.

The split changes no rule. The flight software refuses a configuration with any problem in it, warnings included: a sensor whose device nobody named cannot be opened, and that is not a matter of degree aboard. What it is for is the person editing. A configuration is unfinished for as long as somebody is drawing it, and a verifier that failed on that would fail on every save -- burying the mistakes that matter under the fields nobody has typed yet.

Requirement
Tcsverify prints every error it finds unless --error-limit=<n> asks for a limit -- any whole number, nought included -- and then stops as soon as it has printed that many. --error-limit=unlimited is the default said aloud. Nothing is looked for after the last one the limit allows. --warnings=on, the default, prints warnings without counting them; --warnings=off prints none; --warnings=errors prints them and counts them against the limit as errors are. What is found is printed in the order it was found, so that a run stopped by its limit ends on the finding that stopped it. Every one by default, because a report cut short by a limit nobody asked for hides errors from somebody who believes they have seen them all; a limit is for whoever wants the first few on the screen.
Requirement
--digest prints each configuration's digest as well, one line per file and before the problems: the same digest tcspecial takes of the graph it loaded, prints as it starts and puts in every beacon, so that the question the digest exists to answer -- is the vehicle flying the configuration on this desk -- can be asked of a file without running anything. It is of the graph a file parses into and not of the file's bytes, so the two spellings of one configuration answer alike.
Requirement
--types=<types> checks a configuration as a tcspecial built with only those payload types would; see Which Node Types Are Built.
Requirement
The digest is taken of the configurations among the pathnames and of nothing else: the first of two and the first two of three, the last being the payload simulator file. A simulator file has no digest of this kind. It is not a graph to take one over, and it does not fly, so there is nothing at the other end to compare one with.
Requirement
A configuration with problems still has a digest, having said something for the digest to be taken of. One that cannot be read has none, and that is a failed run: a digest asked for and not given must not end as a silent success, which it otherwise would under --error-limit=0.
Requirement
The exit status is about what was printed: failure if an error was, and success if none was -- a run stopped before it reached one included, and a run whose only findings are warnings, whatever --warnings says. A digest asked for and not given fails as well, being the one failure that is not about a file's contents. The library's verify takes the same two settings and its report passes and fails as tcsverify does.
Requirement
The programs that check a file before running it -- tcspecial, tcsmoc, tcssim and tcsgui -- set no limit and keep warnings on, so they look at everything, and show all of it in the order found, as tcsverify prints it: a configuration is small, and a problem left out of what is shown is a problem somebody has to run something else to find.
Requirement
Tcsverify exits with a status of nought when it printed no errors and non-nought when it printed one, so that a recipe or a shell && can be told by it. Warnings do not change the status: a configuration somebody is in the middle of writing must not fail a build it is not part of yet. A command line that is neither of the two shapes above is a third status, which is not a statement about any file.
Requirement
Tcsverify states no rule of its own. Every problem it reports comes from the same rules in tcslibgs that the programs reading these files use, so a set it accepts is a set they accept. A verifier with rules of its own would eventually disagree with them, and then a passing check would mean nothing.
Requirement

Every program that reads these files asks tcsverify about them as it starts, before anything else is read from them, and says what it says in its words: tcspecial, tcsmoc, tcssim and tcsgui report the same problems, each with its file and line, as tcsverify does for the same files. What a program then refuses for its own reasons -- tcssim a payload it has nothing to stand in for, tcsmoc a configuration it does not control -- is said beside them. Where a rule is the simulator's rather than the file's, it is in tcslibgs all the same (simulable), so that tcsverify asks it too.

Which of them stops a program is the program's business. tcspecial refuses a configuration with a warning in it, as above; tcsmoc reads the simulator file only to show it, so a problem there is said and costs the panels their simulator half.

Requirement
A program with a main window shows what it found as it started in a window of its own, before the main window, and the main window opens only once that one has been closed -- or not at all, where what was found stops the program. tcsmoc and tcssim share the window (tcsverify/ui/startup.slint); tcsgui's is the one that offers a fix for each problem, and lists beside its own faults whatever else tcsverify finds, warnings included. Everything is said on stderr as well.
Requirement
A configuration's simulator file is checked by name: that it parses, and that every simulated payload is a payload of the graph, every group it names is declared and every group it declares is used, and every setting is one. A payload with no entry is not a problem: the one a bench has for real is left out on purpose.

That tcsverify states no rule of its own is what the library's verify::Problem type is for. A loader wants the first thing wrong with a file and nothing else -- it cannot run, and which of several mistakes it names changes nothing -- where a reader wants all of them. So the rules are stated once and collect their answers: Graph::load refuses a file at the first thing in it that does not read and SimConfigFile::resolve at the first problem; Graph::check and SimConfigFile::problems return the lot.

Where a line number comes from

Nothing in the library has seen the file. A parser hands up a document and the lines are gone by then, so a problem says which payload or group it is about and tcsverify works out where that name is written -- the same thing a reader does with the error a program prints. The alternative, carrying a line number through every struct in the library, would put one file format's shape into the types that exist so that two formats can describe one thing.

Requirement
A problem about a payload or a group is reported from the line its name is written on. One about a section is reported from the line the section begins on, and one about the file as a whole from its first line.
Requirement
A file that does not parse is reported from where the parser stopped. YAML's parser says where that was. XML's does not, so the line is the reader's position when it failed: the < of markup that does not parse, and otherwise just past the element it was refused at -- an element nothing has a field for, or a missing one's parent closing. The message says it too, as at line N column M, which is how YAML's is written.
Requirement
The simulator file is checked against the tcspecial configuration only when that configuration has no problems of its own. There would be nothing to check it against otherwise -- the payloads that did not settle are not handlers -- and a join against the rest reports every one of them as unsimulated, which is a page of problems the verifier caused rather than found. It says that it did not do it, rather than leaving it unsaid.

GUI Framework

The testing GUIs will all use the Rust slint crate with black on white. All windows will have a go away box which will shut down the entire application, i.e. tcspecial, tcsmod, and tcssim.

Hints

These are some things that Claude doesn't seem to figure out by itself.

  • Do not pass an i32 to PollFd::new() as the first argument. Instead, pass the i32 value as the result of applying BorrowedFd::borrowed_raw() to it.
  • The crate libc must be included to Cargo.toml for all crates to get definitions of AF_UNIX and other address families.
  • The crate serde_json is in Cargo.toml for the command link, which carries JSON between the ground and the spacecraft. No configuration file is JSON.
  • into_raw_fd() must not be used to convert TCPStream and UDPSocket types to RawFDs.
  • as_raw_fd() must be used to convert TCPStream and UDPSocket types to RawFDs.
  • Do not use tokio. Use threads directly.

Possible Enhancements

Build-time selection of CI and DH protocols to OC/spacecraft radio

Though most or all of on-board spacecraft protocols are based on datagrams, use of error correcting stream-based protocols are also a reasonable choice for this purpose. Build-time selection of these seems useful.

Special tty-based timing of input

Could use the ioctl_tty/termios maximum number of characters to read a message.

Tcspecial manual/auto fail over

Requires sharing the state in a consistent way.

Support for non-Linux ReadyWait

The ReadyWait trait could be extended to other operating systems.

Development Approach

As of this writing, this design document is all there is of TCSpecial. The intent is to use AI to generate it from this code. Going from design to code, if AI is ready, should both save time and improve quality but these will only be true to the extent that the design is accurate and complete. Right now I am using Claude Code as the AI code generator.

Requirements

These are the requirements TCSpecial was written against, as they were written, with the implementation notes that say where the software does something else. They are here rather than in a file of their own so that a requirement and the design that answers it are read together: a requirement kept somewhere else is a requirement nobody reads while making a decision.

TCSpecial is a framework for passing commands to payload devices from an operations center and for conduiting telemetry from the payloads back to it. What follows sets out the requirements for its operation.

About the names and the notes

The requirements below are as they were written, with two types of change.

The names are the ones the software goes by. Three were settled differently after this was written: the spacecraft process is tcspecial, not tcssvr; the library both ends share is tcslibgs, not tcsdefs; and the testing GUI became two programs, tcsmoc for the ground and tcssim for the payloads, rather than one tcstest. A third test program, tcsverify, checks the configuration files without running any of them.

Four words changed as well, and these requirements use the old ones throughout. What a requirement calls a payload is a device where it is hardware, and a node where the statement is about anything the configuration can name -- a device, a MOC link, or a compute element. Where it says device and means an entry in the filesystem, that is a device file. Where it says OC it means the MOC, the mission operations center. The requirements stand as written; manual.rst gives the vocabulary in force.

A Not implemented or Implemented differently note says where the software does not do what a requirement says. The requirement stands as written: a note records what is so today, and is not an amendment. What each of them does instead is described in design.rst.

Where a note calls something experimental, it means what design.rst and manual.rst mean by it: written, parsing, reaching a handler and tested, but never run against the hardware it describes and in no configuration this tree ships. Six things are in that state, all of them types of payload interface: RS-485 on a serial line, and the can, iio, rpmsg, gpio and hdlc types.

The name of the project is TCSpecial

TCSpecial shall include a process named tcspecial that runs on the spacecraft

TCSpecial shall include a library named tcslib that links with misssion control software

TCSpecial shall include a library named tcslibgs that is shared between tcspecial and tcslib

TCSpecial shall include applications named tcsmoc and tcssim that provide GUIs for testing tcspecial, tcslibgs, and tcslib

Note

Implemented as two programs rather than one. Tcsmoc stands in for the operations center and tcssim for the payloads, because a test needs both ends of every link and one window driving both says less about either.

TCSpecial must have a trait named Command Interpreter (CI) that parses commands sent by tcslib

Note

Implemented differently. CommandInterpreter is a struct, not a trait: there is one command interpreter and nothing has needed a second implementation of it. The traits in tcspecial are at the layer below -- EndpointWaitable, EndpointReadable, EndpointWritable -- where there really are many implementations, one per type of endpoint.

TCSpecial must have a trait named data handler (DH) that is called by CI to process commands

Note

Implemented differently, for the reason above: DataHandler is a struct. What differs between handlers is the endpoints they open, which is where the traits are.

CI must be implemented by CI_UDP that communicates with tcslib code via UDP/IP

Note

Implemented differently. There is no CI_UDP: the command interpreter speaks UDP itself, on two sockets -- one for commands about the spacecraft and one for commands about a payload.

DH_UDP must implement DH and use UDP to send telemetry to tcslib

Note

Implemented differently. There is no DH_UDP. A node's endpoint is per type -- UdpEndpoint, TcpEndpoint, SerialEndpoint, I2cEndpoint, SpiEndpoint, DeviceEndpoint, the Unix socket pair, and the experimental CanEndpoint, IioEndpoint, RpmsgEndpoint and GpioEndpoint. Telemetry no longer leaves from the payload's own node: every payload had an OC endpoint of its own, and a node's data now reaches the ground along the links the configuration draws, through the moc node, which is the one UDP socket to the ground whatever types the nodes behind it are. What that is for is that the graph decides where a datum goes -- to the ground, to a log, to a computation, or to all three -- and a payload wired to send its own telemetry could only decide the one.

CI must support the following functions for implementing commands: ping(), exit(), status(), config(), start_dh(), stop_dh(), status_dh()

Note

ping and config are there. The three that name a payload are there under other names, and they name a node by id rather than a handler by name: START_NODE, STOP_NODE, QUERY_NODE -- which is what status_dh asked for -- and QUERY_NODE_SAMPLE, for the last datum a node moved each way. The five commands that named a handler by name (START_DH, STOP_DH, QUERY_DH, QUERY_DH_SAMPLE and CONFIG_DH) are gone, and their opcodes are not reused: a ground station of an older build is told its command is not one, rather than having it acted on as another. exit and status are not implemented; see below. Three commands this did not ask for were added: connect, which is each end saying what configuration it read, restart_arm with restart, and SET_NODE, which sets an attribute the commands section says the ground may set.

CI must have an element named dh of type BTreeMap

Note

Implemented as a BTreeMap, of nodes rather than of handlers and under another name: Fabric::nodes, a BTreeMap<String, Mutex<Box<dyn Port>>>, keyed by the name the configuration gives a node. It was Arc<Mutex<BTreeMap<DHName, DataHandler>>>. The lock moved inward with the change: one mutex per node rather than one over the whole map, so a node held open for a slow read does not hold up a command about another.

CI must support sending telemetry responses to all CI commands

Each CI telemetry response to a command must contain a timestamp, command sequence number, a success or error indication

Note

Implemented. TelemetryHeader carries the sequence number, the telemetry type, the status and the time the spacecraft made the answer. Two responses used to carry a time of their own and the other eight carried none; it is in the header for all of them now. A timestamp is microseconds since the UNIX epoch, wherever one is carried: eight bytes, least significant first, and all ones for a clock the spacecraft could not read.

CI must periodically send a Telemetry::Beacon message with a timestamp and unique sequence number

Note

Implemented, from one stated socket to another: a beacon went to a multicast group so that any number of ground stations could hear it, and the two attributes that took were an address that was nobody's and an interface whose being wrong lost every beacon in silence. The number is counted by the sender from one, so it starts again when tcspecial does, and the MOC tells a gap from a restart by that. A beacon also carries what a CONNECT is answered with -- the software version, the configuration version, and the digest of the configuration file the spacecraft read -- which this did not ask for.

It no longer carries a timestamp. Since the beacon became the fixed 34-byte layout described under Beacon, which has no time in it, the ground goes by when each beacon arrives, which it did already. This requirement says otherwise and is not met: one of the two is to change.

CI must parse commands from the ground receved via UDP/IP

All CI commands must include a sequence number that increments for each command sent

CI must support an EXIT command that terminates the tcspecial process

Note

Not implemented. There is no EXIT command. RESTART comes nearest, and is deliberately not the same thing: it has to be armed by a RESTART_ARM first, so that one lost or malformed datagram cannot stop the flight software.

CI must support a STATUS command that sends telemetry containing status to ground

Note

Not implemented. There is no STATUS command for the interpreter as a whole. QUERY_NODE answers for one node -- whether it is enabled and what it has moved -- and QUERY_NODE_SAMPLE for what it last took and gave; a ground station asks about every node by asking about each.

Tcspecial includes zero or more components named Data Handler (DH)

Note

Implemented, as nodes: a configuration draws zero or more, and what a requirement calls a handler is one node of the graph. Where a handler was a payload and nothing else, a node is a payload, a link to the ground, a log or a computation, and the three kinds that are not payloads are what let a decision be made aboard.

The top part of the tcsmoc window is for sending commands to tcspecial's CI and viewing telemetry from tcspecial's CI

The middle part of the tcsmoc window is for viewing telemetry from individual DHs

Note

Implemented, one panel per node the configuration draws. What the panel shows is the ground's own account of what arrived -- when it came, the head of it, and how many have come since the MOC started -- and not the spacecraft's count of what its handler had read. The count shown before was the spacecraft's, which is the other direction, and read nought for a payload nobody sends to.
